  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Client (JS) is employed as an HGV driver through an agency, typically earning around £300 per week net, with additional income of £20 per week from a volunteer role with the Sea Cadets. Client expects to retire within the next two years (intended retirement date 2027-10-14, approximately age 71). Spouse (Kim) earns approximately £1,100 per month net and intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully.
    File:     Fact Find (P5, P13) confirms: Client employed as HGV Driver, employer CDH Recruitment, with Basic Income of £1,200.00 net monthly (approximately £300 per week), State Pension £230.00 monthly. Fact Find shows Kim's Net Basic Monthly Income as £1,200.00. Intelliflo cashflow modelling (P3, P4, P9, P10) shows Employment Income of £16,640 for client and £13,200 for Kim in first year, continuing through projection periods to at least age 90/86. Risk profile report (P19) shows retirement date as 7 June 2031 (age 75 for client, derived from birthdate 07/05/1960 shown in P5). Zurich Policy Info (P20) shows illustration retirement date as 7 June 2031 (age 75).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states intended retirement date approximately 2027-10-14 (within two years from 14/10/2025 report date, age ~71), but P20 Zurich illustration shows retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75) and P19 Risk profile shows same retirement date 7 June 2031
      - Fact Find (P5) shows client age as 65 with date of birth 07/05/1960, which would make client 65 as at the fact find date (not 69 as stated in extraction ambiguity)
      - Kim's employment income in Fact Find (P13) shown as £1,200.00 net monthly (£14,400 annualised), but cashflow modelling (P3, P10) shows Kim's employment income as £13,200 in combined household figure, suggesting discrepancy in either Kim's income amount or its treatment in projections
      - Report does not explicitly state whether client's employment income (£300 per week HGV driver plus £20 per week volunteer) continues to intended retirement date or ceases at retirement, or how it is modelled in cashflow projections given the ambiguity about retirement timing
      - Intelliflo projections (P3, P4, P9, P10) show employment income continuing through age 90/86 in all years displayed, contradicting any interpretation that employment income ceases at age 71 in 2027
    The requirement examines consistency of employment details across the suitability report and supporting evidence. Multiple discrepancies emerge: (1) Retirement timing contradiction: The report asserts 'within the next two years' from 14/10/2025 (implying age ~71 in 2027), but the Zurich Policy Info (P20) and Risk profile (P19) both show retirement date as 7 June 2031 when client reaches age 75. This is a four-year difference and material to cashflow modelling. (2) Age discrepancy: Fact Find (P5) records client age as 65 with DOB 07/05/1960, which as at 08/12/2025 (ID verification date) would make client 65, not 69 as implied by the extraction. However, if report date is 14/10/2025 and DOB is 07/05/1960, client would be 65 years 5 months at report date, approaching 66. (3) Employment income continuation: The report does not explicitly state in the extracted passages whether £300/week HGV driver income and £20/week volunteer income continue to retirement or cease at the stated retirement date. Intelliflo projections (P3, P4, P9, P10) show employment income of £16,640 (client) continuing throughout the projection period to age 90. If client retires at 71 as the narrative suggests, employment income should cease then, yet the cashflow shows it continuing. This is material because employment income underpins affordability of the retirement objective (£1,700 net monthly household income target stated in P6). (4) Kim's retirement age: Report states Kim intends to retire at 66. Fact Find (P5) shows Kim age 65. If current age 65 and intended retirement at 66, this is imminent (within one year), but cashflow projections show Kim's employment income of £13,200 in combined totals continuing through the projection period. (5) Guards: The ValueMatch check found that 2027 (intended retirement date year extracted) did not match any value in passages, with 2025 being nearest. The extracted candidate readings acknowledge ambiguity about employment income continuation. The guard notes that intended retirement age in a fact find is often superseded by a later file note, and P20 (Zurich illustration) and P19 (Risk profile) provide later evidence of age 75 retirement date (2031), not 2027. However, the fact that the report's own narrative states 'within the next two years' and the suitability recommendation is premised on retirement planning is contradicted by the actual retirement date used in illustrations (2031, not 2027). This is material inconsistency.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: Client (C1) HGV driver income £300/week net (£1,300/month equivalent); volunteer income £20/week net (£86.67/month equivalent); state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month equivalent). Spouse (C2) employment income £1,100/month net; state pension £230/week net from age 67 (£1,000/month equivalent). Total current monthly income £3,486.67. Household expenditure £1,700/month current and expected in retirement. Annual expenditure £20,400 (stated as approximately £1,700 on page 14). State pension income stated as £11,973 per annum.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] and [P10] record: C1 employed income from CDH Recruitment £1,200.00 monthly net (not weekly). State Pension £230.00 monthly (not weekly). [P10] shows Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00. Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00 per [P1]. [P17] confirms C1 'typically earning around £300 per week net' and volunteer role '£20 per week', state pension '£230 per week', and C2 'approximately £1,100 per month net'. [P9] states 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives C1 HGV monthly equivalent as £1,300 (£300 × 52 ÷ 12); Fact Find [P10] records employed income as £1,200.00 monthly - a £100/month discrepancy
      - Report states state pension £230/week; Fact Find [P10] records State Pension as £230.00 monthly - frequency mismatch of approximately £770/month
      - Report total monthly income £3,486.67 relies on £1,000/month state pension (£230 × 52 ÷ 12); if fact-find state pension is £230 monthly instead, total would be £2,716.67 not £3,486.67
      - Report states annual state pension £11,973; calculation of £230/week × 52 weeks = £11,960, discrepancy of £13 per annum; if £230 monthly × 12 = £2,760, which contradicts £11,973 figure
      - Report household expenditure £1,700/month stated consistently; Fact Find [P1] calculates Total Monthly Household Expenditure as £1,718.00
      - Report annual expenditure £20,400; this equals £1,700 × 12, but Fact Find states £1,718.00 monthly which would be £20,616 annually
    Critical ambiguity in fact-find encoding: [P10] records state pension as £230.00 with 'Monthly' frequency, yet [P17] suitability report and [P1] fact-find narrative both refer to state pension as '£230 per week'. The £230 weekly figure is the correct UK state pension rate (as at October 2025), making the monthly recording in [P10] an encoding or data entry error in the fact-find rather than a true income figure. Applying the correct weekly frequency: £230/week × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000/month, which matches the report's derivation. However, the £1,200 monthly employed income in [P10] conflicts with the £300/week figure stated in [P17] and [P1]. £300/week × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300/month, not £1,200/month. The report correctly derives £1,300/month from the £300/week statement. The fact-find appears to record £1,200.00 as a rounded or incorrect conversion. The report's total monthly income of £3,486.67 is correctly derived from: HGV £1,300 + volunteer £86.67 + C2 employment £1,100 + state pension £1,000 = £3,486.67. The state pension annual figure of £11,973 in [P9] is inconsistent with £230/week × 52 = £11,960 (£13 discrepancy, immaterial). Expenditure: report states £1,700/month; fact-find calculates £1,718.00 (£18 discrepancy, within 5% tolerance). Annual expenditure £20,400 = £1,700 × 12, which is the consolidated household figure from page 3, not derived from the £1,718 line-item total. The consolidated narrative figure of £1,700 is what governs the cashflow modelling and is corroborated by [P9] and [P17]. The fact-find line-item total of £1,718 represents a different calculation (detailed line-by-line summary) and cannot be directly reconciled line-by-line to the consolidated figure per the guard on consolidated household expenditure.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Client receives full State Pension of £230 per week (stated as in payment on page 3). Kim will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age of 67 (stated as not yet in payment). Client receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum in cashflow modelling (page 14). Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67 (page 14).
    File:     Passages corroborate: £230 per week for client in [P9]; state pension age 67 for Kim in [P5]; £11,973 per annum for Kim from age 67 in [P5]; £11,973 per annum stated for client in [P5]. However, [P1] states client's state pension age as 66, not 67. Kim's state pension age is stated as 67 in [P5] and also 66 in [P1] as 'Retire age 66 in 2026'. Cashflow modelling in [P3] shows income line items but does not explicitly state state pension amounts. Planning scenario [P4] shows charts but does not state pension figures. Intelliflo planning documents [P1] and [P2] show state pension age as 66 for client.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client's state pension age is implicitly 67 (derived from Kim's age 67 stated as 'State Pension age' in canonical model); evidence in [P1] shows client state pension age as 66
      - Report states Kim will receive state pension from age 67; [P1] shows 'Retire age 66 in 2026' which if applied to Kim (born 07/05/1960) would place state pension age at 66, contradicting the 67 stated
      - Frequency conversion: £230 per week × 52 weeks = £11,960 per annum; report states £11,973 per annum for client state pension on page 14, discrepancy of £13 per annum
    The requirement compares state pension entitlement across the report. Three issues emerge: (1) State pension age discrepancy: The canonical model does not explicitly state the client's state pension age, only Kim's at 67. However, [P1] intelliflo planning baseline scenario explicitly shows 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' for the client (JS), contradicting the implied age 67. The guard notes that state pension age and retirement age are routinely conflated; [P1] shows 'Retire age 66 in 2026' for JS but 'Retire age 71 in 2027' for KS, suggesting retirement and state pension age are separate. The state pension age of 66 for a client born in 1956 (age 69 on report date 14/10/2025) is plausible. (2) Frequency reconciliation: The report states £230 per week in narrative and £11,973 per annum in cashflow modelling. Weekly to annual conversion at standard tolerance: £230 × 52 = £11,960, not £11,973. The discrepancy of £13 per annum (0.11% variance) falls outside exact reconciliation but could reflect rounding in modelling. (3) Kim's state pension age: [P1] planning baseline shows both clients' retirement ages and state pension age 66, but the report's canonical model and [P5] state Kim's state pension age as 67. The evidence in [P1] is an intelliflo planning document, which may not be the suitability report itself. The suitability report [P5] consistently states Kim's state pension age as 67 and that she 'will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67'. For Kim's amount, £230 per week × 52 = £11,960, also showing a £13 discrepancy. The cash flow modelling reconciliation is imperfect but the £230 weekly figure is corroborated in [P9]. The state pension age stated in the report (67 for Kim) contradicts the planning tool evidence (66 in [P1]) but aligns with [P5]. The client's state pension age is not explicitly stated in the report canonical model, leaving ambiguity about whether the report relies on age 67 or permits the age 66 shown in planning tools.

This arrangement is completely absent from the report's table of 5 existing arrangements. The fact find also shows total lump sum contribution value of £116,555.45 for Aviva Platform specifically. This is a separate policy from the report's Aviva arrangement (SP50163762). The report claims 5 arrangements totalling £116,998.47; the fact find evidence shows at least 6 distinct arrangements including Aviva Platform. This is a membership gap: a plan present in the fact find but absent from the report's enumeration of existing arrangements.   [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report contains six internal inconsistencies: (1) Aviva advisability statement says plan is '0.06% more expensive' but comparison table shows 0.50% vs 0.44% = 0.06% cheaper; (2) Standard Life advisability statement says '0.08% more expensive' but comparison table shows 0.18% vs 0.44% = 0.26% MORE expensive; (3) State pension stated as £230/week (£11,960 pa) but cashflow modelling uses £11,973 pa; (4) Existing arrangements total £116,998.47 but narrative states 'approximately £110,000' and recommendation shows transfer value £110,185; (5) Knowledge & Experience states client has not invested in stocks & shares yet Capacity for Loss says underperformance would not impact living standards because pensions form basis of retirement, but recommendation rationale states client is 'not reliant upon pension funds'; (6) Aviva existing arrangement SP50163762 (£3,744.36) is recommended to switch to 'Aviva Platform' with no clear explanation of rationale for same-provider consolidation.
    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external corroborating documents are required or searched. The inconsistencies are internal contradictions within the suitability report's own stated figures, narratives and logic.
    Discrepancies:
      - Aviva charge narrative (page 7) states 0.06% more expensive; comparison table (page 11) shows Aviva 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% equalling 0.06% CHEAPER, not more expensive - statement direction reversed
      - Standard Life charge narrative (page 8) states 0.08% more expensive; comparison table (page 11) shows Standard Life 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.26% MORE expensive than stated, not 0.08% and direction is actually correct but magnitude wrong
      - State pension frequency conversion: £230/week × 52 weeks = £11,960 pa, but page 14 cashflow modelling uses £11,973 pa - difference of £13 per annum unexplained
      - Total pension value: page 5 table sums to £116,998.47 (People's £6,601.55 + Scottish Widows £211.32 + Aviva £3,744.36 + Zurich £103,439.24 + Standard Life £3,002), page 3 narrative states 'approximately £110,000', page 9 shows transfer value £110,185 - three different figures without reconciliation
      - Capacity for Loss statement: pension funds described as 'basis of your retirement provision' vs Attitude to Risk justification: client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' - these contradict each other on the same document's assessment of client dependency
      - Aviva arrangement: existing arrangement SP50163762 with value £3,744.36 is recommended to 'Switch to Aviva Platform' (same provider, same policy number) without clear explanation of why this same-provider consolidation differs from the existing arrangement
    All six internal inconsistencies are genuine contradictions or arithmetic mismatches within the report itself. (1) Aviva charge: the narrative statement on page 7 says the plan is '0.06% more expensive' but the comparison table on page 11 mathematically shows 0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% saving, meaning the existing plan is cheaper, not more expensive. The narrative direction is wrong. (2) Standard Life charge: page 8 claims '0.08% more expensive' but page 11 shows 0.18% - 0.44% = -0.26%, meaning the recommended solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than the existing plan. The report both overstates the saving and gets the direction backwards. (3) State pension: £230 per week multiplied by 52 weeks equals £11,960, not £11,973. The £13 discrepancy is unexplained and represents a frequency conversion error. (4) Total pension value: the report asserts three separate figures (£116,998.47 from table, approximately £110,000 from narrative, £110,185 from recommendation transfer value) that do not reconcile. The difference of approximately £6,800 to £6,998 between the table total and the transfer value is material and unexplained. (5) Capacity for Loss vs Attitude to Risk: the report states pension funds form 'the basis of your retirement provision' when assessing capacity for loss, yet later justifies Risk 5 portfolio by saying the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds'. These statements are contradictory on whether pensions are central to retirement income. This is substantive inconsistency in the financial assessment itself, not a rounding issue. (6) Aviva recommendation: the report recommends switching from Aviva SP50163762 to 'Aviva Platform' - a same-provider arrangement change with no clear explanation of why this internal consolidation is preferable. The arrangement identifier and provider are identical, making the recommendation rationale unclear. These are all report-internal contradictions and do not require external evidence to verify. The arithmetic mismatches (state pension, charge percentages, pension totals) are calculable from the report's own stated figures. The logical contradictions (capacity vs reliance on pensions, same-provider switch without rationale explanation) are apparent within the document's own statements.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts the following income and expenditure figures at varying frequencies: HGV driver income £300/week net; volunteer income £20/week net; spouse employment £1,100/month net; client state pension £230/week net; spouse projected state pension £230/week net. Monthly equivalents derived as: £1,300 (£300×4.33), £86.67 (£20×4.33), £1,100, £1,000 (£230×4.33), £1,000. Total monthly income net calculated as £3,486.67. Report states on page 3 'combined value of approximately £110,000' for pensions but page 5 table totals £116,998.47; page 14 cashflow shows state pension input as £11,973 pa.

State pension verified as '£230 per week' in report quote matching [P2].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives HGV income monthly equivalent as £1,300 (£300×4.33) but Fact Find [P2] shows employed basic income as £1,200/month for same employment - a £100/month difference (7.7% variance).
      - Report derives volunteer income monthly equivalent as £86.67 (£20×4.33) but Fact Find [P1] shows 'Entertainment £100.00 Monthly' in expenditure not income, and no volunteer income line item appears in Fact Find income section.
      - Report states total monthly net income as £3,486.67 but Fact Find [P2] 'Total Net Monthly Income' shows £1,430.00 - a £2,056.67 discrepancy; this is explained by Fact Find only capturing client's employed income £1,200 and state pension £230 (totalling £1,430), whereas report includes spouse's £1,100 employment income, but Fact Find total still does not reconcile to report's stated components even accounting for spouse income.

    The weekly-to-monthly conversion uses 52-week annualisation divided by 12 months (4.333), which is the correct method and falls within the guard allowing 0.4% arithmetic variance. However, multiple material discrepancies exist: (1) The HGV driver income appears in two forms - report assertion of £300/week net and Fact Find entry of £1,200/month net - a 7.7% variance exceeding tolerance. When converted to common basis: £300/week × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300/month vs Fact Find's £1,200/month; this is not rounding variance but a substantive difference of £100/month. (2) The £20/week volunteer income has no corroboration in Fact Find income section; [P1] shows £100 entertainment expenditure but no volunteer income. (3) The state pension £11,973 pa asserted on page 14 exceeds the calculated £230×52=£11,960 by £13, which is immaterial rounding (0.11% tolerance). (4) The three different pension total values (£110,000 narrative, £116,998.47 table, £110,185 transfer value) represent material inconsistency within the report itself. The total monthly income figure of £3,486.67 cannot be reconciled to Fact Find's £1,430 total, though this reflects different scope (report includes spouse income not fully captured in Fact Find total line). The primary reconciliation failure is the HGV income frequency/amount mismatch (7.7% variance) and pension value inconsistencies (5.8%-6.8% range).

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts client retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (age 71); timeHorizon values stated as LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm; intendedRetirementAge 66 is also extracted but this appears to be a separate field. The report includes investment period of 2 years in risk profile section and projects to age 75 in multiple pension illustrations.
    File:     Evidence shows multiple retirement dates and ages across documents: P10 intelliflo proposed scenario shows 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027 and 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026. P11 Risk profile shows 'Investment Period: 2 years'. P15 Zurich plan shows 'Selected Retirement Age: 65' and 'Planned Retirement Age for this plan is 75'. P1-P7 pension switch reports project to age 75. P12 Aviva policy states decision required 'before their 75th birthday'. P6 fact find does not explicitly state retirement date. P24 suitability report discusses life expectancy to age 86 for client. Standard Life illustration (P17) references retirement date. Disparate retirement ages appear across documents: 65, 66, 71, 75.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report extracted timeHorizon contains three values (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) but only one targetDate (2027-10-14) and one targetAge (71), creating ambiguity about which objective maps to which horizon
      - Report extracts intendedRetirementAge as 66 but targetAge as 71 - a five-year gap unexplained within the report structure
      - Zurich plan (P15) shows 'Selected Retirement Age' of 65 while report targetAge is 71
      - Multiple pension plans in file project benefits to age 75 (P1, P5, P7, P12) but report retirement target is age 71
      - Risk profile (P11) states 'Investment Period: 2 years' which maps to 2025-2027, consistent with targetDate 2027, but pension illustrations project to age 75 not age 71
      - intendedRetirementAge extracted as 66 matches Kim's retirement age in P10 (2026) but client (JS) is age 69 and retiring at 71 in 2027 per same document
    The core retirement target is corroborated: report targetDate 2027-10-14 and targetAge 71 match P10 which shows 'Retire (John) age 71 in 2027'. The 2-year investment horizon from 2025 to 2027 is supported by P11 risk profile stating 'Investment Period: 2 years'. However, the report structure contains an internal inconsistency in its extraction: intendedRetirementAge of 66 does not match the client's 2027 retirement target of age 71. This 66 figure appears to reference Kim's state pension age (also shown as 66 in P10) rather than the client's retirement age. The extraction of three timeHorizon values without corresponding objectives creates ambiguity about which applies to which goal. The pension plans in the evidence use age 75 as their contractual retirement age (e.g., P15 Zurich 'Planned Retirement Age for this plan is 75'), which is a guard condition: selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date. The actual investment term for the pension switch is 2 years to age 71 (2027), not to age 75. This distinction is critical because the investment strategy and charges analysis in the switch reports (P1-P7) project outcomes to age 75 but the client intends to retire at 71, creating a 4-year discrepancy between the planning horizon and the contractual plan horizon. The fact that multiple objectives are extracted with multiple timeHorizons but only one explicit targetDate suggests the report may contain multiple goals (immediate pension switch, ongoing investment, protection) that are not clearly delineated. Evidence supports the 2027 / age 71 retirement target but does not explicitly confirm all three timeHorizon classifications or explain their relationship to specific, named objectives.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states three prioritized objectives: (1) Priority 1: Ensure continuation of lifestyle through lifetime; (2) Priority 2: Retire in 2 years time with household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) Priority 3: Clearer understanding of pension arrangements and streamlined approach to managing five separate pension plans. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts retirement target date 2027-10-14 (age 71), but does not explicitly confirm whether employment income of £16,640 per annum continues past age 71 into the projection period, creating ambiguity about the true planning horizon and capacity to meet stated objectives post-retirement
      - The stated household income objective of £1,700 net per month (£20,400 per annum) appears in the report but is not evidenced by a corresponding income figure in the retrieved planning passages; no passage quotes or confirms this specific target amount
      - The report's statement that pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' (per extraction ambiguity note) contradicts the later statement that client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential', creating internal inconsistency about whether the stated objective (lifestyle continuation) depends on pension growth or on other income sources
    The three prioritized objectives are stated in the report and corroborated by the Fact Find and planning documents: Objective 1 (lifestyle continuation) is supported implicitly by cashflow modelling showing household expenditure met each year; Objective 2 (retirement in 2 years at age 71 with specified income) is evidenced by timeline in P11 showing 'Retire (John) age 71 in 2027', and objective 3 (pension consolidation and clarity) is explicitly confirmed in P4. However, material ambiguities undermine the completeness of this alignment. First, the extraction notes flag that employment income status post-retirement is not explicitly modelled—the report states retirement is expected 'within the next two years' but does not clarify whether £16,640 annual HGV driver and volunteer income continues beyond age 71, which would materially affect whether the projected cashflow can meet the £1,700 monthly household income objective. Second, the stated household income target of £1,700 net per month is asserted in the report but the specific figure does not appear in any retrieved passage, making it impossible to verify from the evidence whether this target is client-originated or adviser-derived. Third, the extraction notes an internal contradiction within the report itself regarding whether pension funds 'form the basis' of retirement provision (capacity for loss section) versus client being 'not reliant' on them (attitude to risk justification)—this contradiction goes to the heart of whether the investment recommendation (Risk 5, growth-oriented) is aligned with stated objective 1 (lifestyle continuation), since if pensions do form the basis of retirement income, a growth-focused strategy with higher volatility introduces material risk to the primary objective. The planning timeline is clear and the consolidation objective is well-supported, but the time-horizon for achieving lifestyle continuation income depends on clarifying employment income assumptions and resolving the pension dependency contradiction.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts six recommendations, each linked to objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3 (with one recommendation linked to OBJ1 and OBJ3 only). The primary recommendation is an Aviva Pension Portfolio personal pension with a transfer value of £110,185, selected retirement age of 67, and access via Flexi Access Drawdown. The report states the client 'expect[s] to retire within the next two years' (reaching age 71) and prefers 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken.' Planned withdrawals are described as 'Regular' with tax-free and taxable elements available. The cashflow assumes state pension income of £11,973 per annum and annual expenditure of approximately £1,700, with projections running to age 100. The report identifies three specific investment objectives and time horizons: OBJ1 (LongTerm), OBJ2 (MediumTerm), OBJ3 (MediumTerm).
    File:     The evidence identifies one stated goal: 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement' [P1]. The fact find records current total household monthly expenditure as £1,718 with total net monthly income of £1,430, yielding a disposable income deficit of £288 per month [P24]. The client is aged 69 on report date (14/10/2025) with employment income (HGV driver £300/week, volunteer £20/week) continuing. The cashflow analysis shows employment income of £16,640 annually for the client and £13,200 for spouse (total £29,840) in the baseline scenario [P8]. The intelliflo planning baseline and proposed scenarios project cashflow to age 100 with state pension, employment income, liquid assets and accessible pensions [P18]. The report notes 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends' [P10]. The Aviva personal pension illustrations show minimum pension age currently 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028, with access options including Flexi Access Drawdown, annuity, or cash lump sum [P6, P7, P22]. The People's Pension policy information confirms similar access provisions [P19]. Zurich and Aviva illustrations show retirement date modelled at age 75 [P21, P22].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states selected retirement age as 67 but cashflow modelling uses age 75 as the projection endpoint and basis for benefit illustrations
      - Report states 'expect to retire within the next two years' (implying age 71) but selected retirement age extracted as 67, creating a 4-year inconsistency
      - Report projects cashflow to age 100 but illustrations (Critical Yield, projections) are calculated to age 75, with no clear reconciliation of these different time horizons
      - Report assumes 0% of surplus income is saved (all spent) but does not explicitly state whether employment income (£16,640 pa) is modelled to continue to age 71, age 75, or throughout the projection to age 100
      - Current monthly expenditure stated as £1,718 in fact find but retirement expenditure stated as approximately £1,700 per month (£20,400 annually), with no explanation of the transition or change
      - Monthly income deficit of £288 shown in fact find, yet recommendations proceed without addressing how this affordability gap is resolved
      - Report links all recommendations to OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 but does not name or define what these objectives are in the evidence
      - Pension transfer consolidates five existing arrangements (People's £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002) totalling £116,998.47, but report states combined value 'approximately £110,000' and transfer value £110,185, leaving £6,813 unaccounted for in the transfer
      - Report states pension will reach £115,000 at age 75 under mid-growth scenario but does not state whether this is sufficient to meet stated retirement objective of £1,700 net monthly income or support 'large ad hoc spends'
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Definition of OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3: the three objectives linked to recommendations are not named or evidenced in the file, Explicit statement of which employment income continues to which age in the retirement projection, Reconciliation of retirement age (67 selected vs 71 expected vs 75 modelled), Definition of what constitutes the retirement objective and how £1,700 monthly need aligns with projected pension values, Explanation of how current monthly affordability deficit of £288 is addressed by the recommendation, Details of the five existing arrangements' consolidation and the £6,813 difference between aggregate values and stated transfer value
    The comparison cannot be fully performed because the three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) to which all recommendations are linked are not defined in the evidence. The report's canonical model names these objectives but does not spell out what they are—whether growth, income replacement, inheritance, or other goals. Without the objective definitions, it is impossible to assess whether the recommendations 'are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes' as required. The only goal evidenced is 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement' [P1]. The recommendation does state the client prefers Flexi Access Drawdown access [P3], which aligns with the access mechanism available from the Aviva platform from age 55 (57 from 2028) [P6]. However, critical ambiguities undermine the appropriateness assessment: (1) The selected retirement age of 67 conflicts with the stated expectation to 'retire within the next two years' (age 71) and with the cashflow modelling using age 75. The illustrations all project to age 75 [P14, P15, P17], not 67 or 71. No explanation is given for these three different dates. (2) The pension projections at age 75 show £115,000 (mid growth) [P14], but the stated retirement need is £1,700 monthly (£20,400 annually). At typical drawdown withdrawal rates (4-5%), £115,000 would yield £4,600-5,750 annually in sustainable income, appearing sufficient for the stated need. However, this calculation is not made in the report and depends on an unstated assumption about withdrawal rate. (3) The fact find shows a current monthly income deficit of £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718) [P24], yet the recommendation proceeds without addressing how this gap is closed. The report states employment income continues (£16,640 pa) [P8] but does not state to which age, creating ambiguity about the cash flow from age 69 to stated retirement age. (4) The report consolidates five pension arrangements totalling £116,998.47 [derived] but transfers only £110,185, leaving £6,813 unexplained. This discrepancy raises questions about completeness of the transfer and whether all objectives across all arrangements are being met. (5) The recommendations are linked to objectives using numeric codes (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) but these are not evidenced as specific, client-authored goals; they appear to be boilerplate slots. The guard rule requires that a link be to a specific objective, not to a label without definition. The recommendation text states 'I have recommended a Personal Pension for the following reasons' followed by generic benefits (tax efficiency, flexibility, drawdown options) [P16]. These are product features, not evidence that the recommendation serves the stated goal of £1,700 monthly retirement income or any other specific objective. The term of the investment (as selected) is stated as 67 but modelled as 75, and the foreseeable change—retirement within two years—is not reconciled with either of these dates. Access need is addressed (Flexi Access Drawdown is available from age 55/57 on the Aviva platform [P6, P22]), but the relationship between access date and stated retirement date is unresolved.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states a target retirement income of £1,700 net per month (monthly frequency). This is stated on page 3 under 'Your Objectives': 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The cashflow modelling section on page 14 states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' and shows that the forecast assumes this income level. The inflation assumptions show general expenditure inflation at 2%, savings inflation at 2%, state pensions inflation at 2.5%, and Aviva personal pensions inflation at 5%. The modelling uses a retirement age of 75 for drawdown purposes.
    File:     The evidence corroborates the £1,700 net monthly target income. Passage [P1] states under 'Income/Expenditure Goal and Retirement': 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement.' Passage [P2] from the Fact Find records under 'Retirement Planning': 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' Passage [P3] shows current monthly income totalling £1,430 (£1,200 employed + £230 state pension), with expenditure of £1,718 monthly, creating a shortfall of £288. The cashflow modelling in passages [P20], [P22], and [P24] shows the financial projection incorporating pension assets and demonstrating that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states on page 3 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' but the Fact Find [P3] shows calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718, which when annualized is £20,616, not £1,700 annually. This conflates a monthly target income of £1,700 with annual expenditure language of approximately £1,700.
      - The current monthly disposable income is £-288 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718), meaning the client currently spends £288 more per month than earned. The stated target retirement income of £1,700 net per month therefore appears to be what the client needs to sustain, not what they currently have.
      - The report's cashflow section [P24] states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' which is internally ambiguous: this could mean £1,700 per month (£20,400 annually) or £1,700 annually, but the Fact Find shows monthly expenditure of £1,718.
    The target retirement income of £1,700 net per month is clearly stated and consistently corroborated across the evidence. The client's stated objective in both the Fact Find [P2] and Policy Research [P1] is to 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month,' and this exactly matches the report's assertion on page 3. The inflation assumptions are disclosed (2% general expenditure, 2.5% state pensions, 5% Aviva personal pensions) and the retirement age for drawdown is stated as 75. The cashflow modelling incorporates these assumptions and demonstrates no shortfall is projected throughout the client's lifetime. The apparent confusion regarding whether the language refers to monthly or annual expenditure in the report ('In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700') is resolved by reference to the primary source: the Fact Find records £1,700 net per month as the household income need, and the current monthly expenditure baseline is £1,718 per month. The report's use of 'annual expenditure' terminology alongside the £1,700 figure appears to be imprecise language rather than a substantive inconsistency, as the modelling itself uses the correct monthly figures from the Fact Find. The guard regarding targets expressed in today's terms versus escalated figures does not apply here, as the £1,700 is used consistently throughout as a current/today's terms target, with inflation applied only to the modelling assumptions. The target income is evidenced, consistent with client statements, and the modelling assumptions are disclosed and reasonable.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.6, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10). Report asserts CFL rating of 'Medium' but no passage in the file explicitly states or derives a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating. The questionnaire response shows risk rating 4, later increased to 5, but no corresponding capacity for loss numerical score is recorded. Report states CFL is 'separately assessed' from ATR, yet the only capacity for loss evidence in the file is the questionnaire field showing 'No additional notes were recorded' [P5,P20]—no substantive separate assessment appears. Report bases CFL on the statement that underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living' and that pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' [P11], but then contradicts this by stating client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' [P11]. These two statements are internally contradictory about the role of pensions in meeting living standards. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Report asserts Capacity for Loss rating of 'Medium'. Basis stated: (1) Client receives £230/week State Pension + £300/week HGV driver income + £20/week Sea Cadets income; (2) Spouse contributes £1,100/month and receives ~£230/week State Pension at SPA; (3) Combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of ~£1,700/month; (4) Client holds ~£6,000 savings with no liabilities; (5) 'Any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living'. Report states CFL is 'separately assessed' (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true). Report also states client 'not reliant upon pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential' and risk rating 5 is 'higher than your agreed risk rating'.
    File:     Evidence confirms: £230/week State Pension [P11], £300/week HGV income [P11], £20/week volunteer income [P11], £1,100/month spouse contribution [P11], ~£230/week spouse State Pension [P11], ~£1,700/month household expenditure [P11], ~£6,000 savings [P1,P11], no liabilities stated [P11]. Financial planning projections [P1,P4,P9,P12] show consistent surplus income across baseline and proposed scenarios with liquid assets remaining above £122,975 throughout plan period even under stress test with -30% market crash and 6% inflation [P15]. Stress testing confirms 'no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used' under adverse scenarios [P15]. Risk profile document [P19,P20] confirms questionnaire-based natural risk level was 4 ('Cautious Balanced') but no capacity for loss assessment detail is recorded—only a placeholder 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss questionnaire section [P5,P20]. No separate quantified capacity for loss assessment or loss-tolerance scenario is documented in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts CFL rating of 'Medium' but no passage in the file explicitly states or derives a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating. The questionnaire response shows risk rating 4, later increased to 5, but no corresponding capacity for loss numerical score is recorded.
      - Report states CFL is 'separately assessed' from ATR, yet the only capacity for loss evidence in the file is the questionnaire field showing 'No additional notes were recorded' [P5,P20]—no substantive separate assessment appears.
      - Report bases CFL on the statement that underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living' and that pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' [P11], but then contradicts this by stating client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' [P11]. These two statements are internally contradictory about the role of pensions in meeting living standards.
      - Report asserts CFL rating supports risk level 5 (Balanced/higher-risk), yet the financial planning stress tests [P15] show the plan remains solvent even with -30% portfolio loss, suggesting the client could absorb substantial loss without impact on essential expenditure—a characterization more consistent with higher capacity, not medium capacity.

The report asserts 'Medium' but no passage defines what 'Medium' means or confirms this rating was assigned in any assessment., Documented capacity for loss questionnaire responses or assessment notes. For example, the report does not cite evidence stating 'client can tolerate loss of up to £X' or 'loss of X% would not affect essential living,' despite such reasoning typically underpinning a capacity rating., Explicit confirmation that the CFL assessment was conducted separately from the ATR assessment, beyond the canonical model field isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr=true. The file shows ATR was discussed and questionnaire completed, but no separate CFL discussion record exists.
    The report's assertion that capacity for loss is 'Medium' cannot be verified against the evidence. While the financial reasoning provided—stable income, regular surplus, liquid savings, no liabilities, and financial projections showing resilience to -30% loss and high inflation—is all present and corroborated in the file, the file does not itself assign or derive a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating. The Capacity for Loss questionnaire section in the Risk Profile [P5,P20] contains only a placeholder with no recorded notes or scoring. This is a missing evidence issue: a CFL rating is explicitly asserted but no document in the file shows how that rating was determined or what scale it sits on. The internal contradiction between stating pension funds 'form the basis of retirement provision' (suggesting reliance and hence lower capacity) versus 'not reliant upon pension funds' (suggesting higher capacity) further undermines the assessment's clarity. The guard allows that CFL restated as a consequence of ATR is weak assessment to be flagged rather than rejected on face; however, here the assertion goes further—it claims separate assessment occurred yet provides no evidence that it did. The financial indicators (income, surplus, assets, stress-test resilience) support the capacity to absorb losses without detriment to essential living, but the report does not anchor the 'Medium' rating to any of these factors with quantified reasoning. A capacity for loss assessment grounded only in the existence of employment income and household surplus, without explicit loss-tolerance quantification or scenario modelling tied to the 'Medium' label, is not sufficiently evidenced.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report does not assert any specific tolerance for loss figure or need-level risk metric. However, it does address capacity for loss qualitatively: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' (P18). The report also states the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period' (P18), and recommends a Risk rating 5 Balanced strategy. The report notes the agreed risk level considered 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return' (P14).
    File:     The case file evidences capacity for loss through: (1) Questionnaire responses dated 18/09/2025 (P11) which recorded responses to capacity for loss questions but no quantified tolerance figure; (2) Risk profile report (P19, P14) stating 'The agreed risk level considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return' with an agreed risk level of 5; (3) Pension switch report (P7) confirming 'The client's agreed risk level is 5'; (4) Financial planning scenario (P10) showing projected cash flows and asset allocation to age 75 and beyond, with stress testing at -30% market crash and 6% inflation; (5) Suitability report (P18) noting state pension income of £230/week, employment income (HGV driver £300/week plus Sea Cadets £20/week), and spouse contribution of £1,100/month. No document quantifies tolerance for loss as a percentage or monetary amount of potential loss the client can tolerate.
    Discrepancies:
      - No quantified tolerance for loss figure appears in any document; the concept is addressed qualitatively only through capacity for loss assessment and cash flow modelling rather than explicit loss tolerance measurement.
      - The report asserts the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' (P18) as justification for risk rating 5 with best growth potential, yet capacity for loss reasoning cites pension funds as 'form the basis of your retirement provision' (P18) — these statements create an internal tension about the role of pension funds in retirement security.
      - The extraction notes an ambiguity about whether the recommendation is based on initial agreed rating of 4 then increased to 5, or questionnaire result of 4 overridden to 5 — the report (P18) states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' but also 'aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' which contradicts 'agreed' if 5 was the agreement.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Tolerance for loss quantified as percentage or monetary amount of acceptable loss before financial objectives are materially compromised, Need-level risk assessment: a separate metric identifying what risk level is required to meet the stated financial objectives, distinct from attitude to risk and capacity for loss, Explicit risk scale definition used for the agreed rating 5, mapped to specific asset class composition and loss scenarios, Documented evidence of client acknowledgement of specific loss scenarios at risk rating 5 (not merely general acknowledgement of ups and downs)
    The requirement asks whether tolerance for loss and need-level risk are evidenced and consistent. The comparison cannot be made because neither tolerance for loss nor need-level risk is quantified in the suitability report. The report addresses capacity for loss qualitatively (pension not essential to day-to-day living; income from employment and state pension sufficient; stress test shows resilience to -30% loss and 6% inflation), but does not quantify how much loss the client is willing to tolerate before objectives fail, nor does it calculate the specific risk level required to meet the 10-year growth objective stated in P18. The extraction notes ambiguity about whether risk rating 5 represents an upward override from questionnaire result 4: P18 states 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' (suggesting override) but also 'aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' (suggesting 5 is higher than a prior agreement, making the sequence unclear). The internal tension between 'not reliant upon pension funds' and 'pension funds form basis of retirement provision' (both P18) is not reconciled; these are different assertions about the same asset base's role. Risk rating 5 Balanced is described qualitatively (P1, P5, P14) but no document shows the calculation of what risk is needed to meet a 10-year doubling or growth objective from £106,000 (P14), nor does any document quantify tolerance for loss as a percentage fall the client accepts. The financial planning (P10) models a -30% stress but does not state this is the limit of acceptable loss. No evidence in the pack separates need-level risk (what return is required) from attitude to risk (what the client prefers). The guard states absence of quantified tolerance is a documentation gap, not a contradiction; this applies here.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a risk override from 4 to 5. The canonical model states: hasOverride=true, direction='Increased', fromCode='4', toCode='5'. The justification provided is: (1) 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension' (ClientChoiceOverride topic); (2) 'You are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal' (GrowthObjective topic). Client acknowledgement states: 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' (VolatilityAwareness topic).
    File:     P2 (Risk profile report) states: 'Natural risk level' is based on questionnaire completed 18/09/2025, with result being rating 4 'Cautious Balanced'. The section headed 'Agreed risk level' states 'No additional notes were recorded'. P4 (Suitability Report) confirms: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' P14 (Risk profile report appendix) shows the Attitude To Risk questionnaire responses for 18/09/2025, confirming rating 4. P6 shows Risk level 5 'Balanced' descriptor. P1 and P14 both show sections for 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' with entries stating 'No additional notes were recorded'. P5 (File notes) contains case management notes but does not record the specific discussion, rationale or client consent regarding the override from 4 to 5.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts detailed justification for the override (client choice, growth objective, volatility awareness) but the Risk profile report (P2, P14) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the 'Agreed risk level' section, contradicting the detail claimed in the suitability report about discussions held
      - The canonical model indicates client acknowledgement of volatility risk was obtained and recorded, but P1 and P14 show the 'Agreed risk level' section as containing no recorded notes
      - File notes (P5) do not record the conversation, rationale explanation or client acknowledgement that the suitability report asserts took place
    A risk override from rating 4 to rating 5 is asserted in the report with detailed justification and client acknowledgement. The questionnaire result of 4 is corroborated by P14. The agreed rating of 5 is corroborated by P2, P4, P6, P16 and P20. However, the process and justification for the override present a material gap. The report states specific reasons were discussed and client consent was obtained and is aware of the higher risk. Yet the Risk profile report (P2, P14), which is the contemporaneous risk documentation, records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections. This creates a contradiction: either the detailed discussion described in the suitability report occurred and should have been recorded in the risk profile document, or it did not occur and the suitability report's assertions are unsupported. The file notes (P5) contain general case management but do not evidence the override discussion, justification or client acknowledgement. The canonical model extraction's own ambiguity notes flag this: 'Client completed questionnaire indicating risk rating 4, but adviser and client agreed to increase to risk rating 5. Report states "You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5" but also notes this is "higher than your agreed risk rating" - the sequence of agreed ratings is ambiguous.' The suitability report text in P4 states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' which supports that an override occurred, but the supporting documentation contemporaneous to that agreement (P2, P14) records no notes, no discussion and no justification being documented at the time. The override was made, and the client has acknowledged the higher risk in the final report, but the justification and process are not adequately evidenced in the supporting file.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a target risk rating of 5 (Balanced) for the recommended investment strategy. The client's agreed risk rating is 5. The report states the client's capacity for loss is rated as Medium. The report also confirms the client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire scoring 4, but adviser and client agreed to increase to risk rating 5, with the client confirming 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5' and stating 'this is higher than your agreed risk rating'. The recommended solution places £110,185 into an Aviva Pension Portfolio with a Balanced portfolio allocation (50% CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund, 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio).
    File:     [P3] documents the client's natural risk level from the questionnaire completed 18/09/2025 as 4 (Cautious Balanced). [P1] and [P2] confirm the agreed risk level following discussion as 5 (Balanced), describing the Balanced investor profile as someone who does not see themselves as particularly cautious and understands that risk may be needed to meet investment goals. [P5] states the client was shown a projection based initially on the natural risk level (questionnaire result), and following discussion 'we altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved', resulting in agreed risk level 5. [P5] also records capacity for loss assessment: 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' because the client is in receipt of state pension (£230/week), employment income (£300/week HGV driver, £20/week volunteer), and 'Kim contributes a further £1,100 per month from her employment'. The report states 'you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'. No explicit quantified capacity for loss rating (such as 'Medium') is found in the file; capacity assessment is qualitative only.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model extracts capacity for loss rating as 'Medium', but no passage in the file uses the term 'Medium' to describe the client's capacity for loss. [P5] describes capacity qualitatively (no detriment to day-to-day living, not reliant on pension funds) but does not assign a numerical or categorical rating such as 'Medium'.
      - The report's statement that the recommended strategy 'exceeds' or equals the agreed risk rating cannot be directly compared because the report asserts target risk rating 5 and agreed rating 5, appearing to match, yet one discrepancy exists: [P5] notes the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' as justification for taking risk 5, but earlier in the same passage states 'pension funds which form the basis of your retirement provision' as the reason underperformance would not impact day-to-day living. These two statements are internally contradictory and suggest either (a) the pension funds are the basis of retirement provision (implying reliance) or (b) the client is not reliant on them (implying they are not the basis). The report cannot use both to justify the same risk rating without clarifying which is true.
    The trigger is satisfied: investmentOrPensionRecommendation = true. The check requires determining whether the recommended strategy (target risk 5) exceeds the client's risk tolerance or capacity for loss. The canonical model asserts agreed risk rating 5, strategy risk rating 5, and capacity for loss as Medium. On one scale, 5 = 5, so the strategy does not exceed the agreed rating numerically. However, the assessment rests on two problems: (1) The capacity for loss rating 'Medium' is extracted but appears nowhere in the evidence. [P5] provides only qualitative capacity assessment, not a categorical rating. The file does not state 'Medium' capacity, 'Low' capacity, or any categorical label. The comparison between strategy risk (5) and capacity for loss (rated 'Medium' in the model) cannot be substantiated because the 'Medium' rating is not evidenced. (2) The justification for risk 5 contains an internal inconsistency in [P5]: the passage simultaneously asserts the pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' (suggesting high reliance) and that the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' (suggesting low reliance). These cannot both be true without clarification. The report uses both to support the same conclusion (risk rating 5 is appropriate), but they logically conflict. On the risk scale itself, the questionnaire result was 4, and the agreed/recommended rating is 5. [P1], [P2], [P3], [P5] all corroborate that a questionnaire score of 4 was discussed and increased to 5 following adviser and client discussion. This increase is evidenced and acknowledged by the client in [P5]. The comparison can proceed on the scale as presented: questionnaire 4 → agreed 5 → strategy 5. No evidence states the strategy exceeds 5 or that 5 exceeds the capacity for loss once capacity is properly determined. The risk level 7 in the existing arrangements (referenced in the canonical model) does not map to the new strategy and is not claimed to be the recommended rating. The key gap is that the capacity for loss rating 'Medium' has no evidentiary basis in the file. This prevents a full comparison of strategy against capacity. The file contains qualitative capacity reasoning but no categorical capacity rating to match against the strategy rating of 5.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a questionnaire-assessed risk rating of 4 ('Cautious Balanced') which was then increased to an agreed risk rating of 5 ('Balanced'). The report states: 'You completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire. We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' The client is described as 'Balanced' with the label applying to risk rating 5 on a 1-9 scale. The report documents client acknowledgement that this increase is 'higher than your agreed risk rating' and the client confirmed willingness to proceed at risk rating 5.
    File:     The Risk profile report [P1] documents a 'natural risk level' (questionnaire result) and then an 'agreed risk level' of 5 'Balanced'. The SIPP research report [P2] and [P6] confirm 'Agreed risk level' of 5 'Balanced' with identical description. File notes [P11] reference current risk ratings for existing arrangements: 'Aviva current risk – 2', 'Zurich current risk – 7', 'Standard Life current risk – 4'. The ATR questionnaire responses [P16] show the client described their investment approach as 'Cautious', would feel anxious with losses over £4,000 on £20,000, associates risk with 'Caution', and is 'More cautious' compared to others—all consistent with a cautious profile, not balanced. The KIID [P17] for one recommended fund shows it is rated 4 in Risk and Reward Indicator ('medium level of volatility'), with asset allocation 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds, 0-25% other—not a Risk 5 Balanced profile.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts questionnaire result was 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); questionnaire responses [P16] show client self-describes as 'Cautious' and 'More cautious compared to other people', consistent with cautious profiling, but the label '4 Cautious Balanced' does not appear in the questionnaire data itself—only risk level 4 is stated in file notes [P11] for Standard Life existing arrangement
      - Report states agreed risk level is 5 'Balanced'; risk profile documents [P1], [P2], [P6], [P12] all confirm agreed level 5 'Balanced'—no conflict here
      - Report describes Risk 5 'Balanced' investor as one who 'do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person' and 'can be inclined to look for a combination of investments with differing levels of risk'—yet questionnaire responses [P16] consistently show cautious characteristics (anxious at >£4,000 loss on £20,000; associates risk with 'Caution'; describes self as 'More cautious')
      - Report recommends portfolio of CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%), both described in SIPP research [P6] as mapped to Risk Level 5; however KIID [P17] for one fund shows Risk and Reward Indicator of 4 ('medium level of volatility', 45-70% equities), creating apparent mismatch between stated Risk 5 allocation and fund's own risk disclosure category 4
      - File notes [P10] record that 'Aviva – Alternative option: Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR'—implying client's ATR requires Risk 5, yet questionnaire data and existing arrangements show risk ratings of 2, 4, and 7 across different pensions, with no explicit confirmation that all existing pensions were unsuitable due to not meeting Risk 5 ATR
    The core comparison is between the report's assertion that risk rating was increased from 4 (questionnaire) to 5 (agreed) and what the evidence shows. The questionnaire responses [P16] do not explicitly state a numerical risk score; they show responses consistent with a cautious investor profile (describes approach as 'Cautious', would be anxious at >£4,000 loss, associates risk with 'Caution', is 'More cautious' than peers). File notes [P11] confirm Standard Life current risk is 4, and file notes [P10] state that only Aviva fund available is 'risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR', implying the agreed ATR target is Risk 5. This is consistent with the override documented in the report: questionnaire likely produced 4, agreed rating was raised to 5 with client acknowledgement. The override is explained in the report with justifications: client's wish for growth over 10 years and non-reliance on pension funds for immediate living expenses. However, a discrepancy arises between the questionnaire profile (consistently cautious in tone and responses [P16]) and the Risk 5 'Balanced' profile description (not seeing self as 'particularly cautious'). This is not a conflict between two sources calling the same thing different names—it is a substantive mismatch between what the questionnaire responses actually say about the client and what the Risk 5 Balanced profile description asserts. Additionally, the KIID for the recommended HSBC fund [P17] shows a Risk and Reward Indicator of 4 ('medium level of volatility', 45-70% equities), while the portfolio is stated to be Risk 5 'Balanced'. The guard permits comparison across scales only where scales are compatible; the 1-7 Risk and Reward Indicator (KIID) and the 1-9 Risk Rating Scale (adviser) are different scales. The KIID category 4 on 1-7 scale would approximate to mid-range risk (roughly 4-5 on a 1-9 scale if linearly converted), but the fund document itself classifies this fund at indicator 4, not 5. This suggests either the fund does not match the Risk 5 profile it is stated to meet, or the KIID risk rating reflects a different assessment than the adviser's risk rating. The document does not explain why a fund rated at category 4 in its KIID is suitable for a Risk 5 portfolio. No scale-identification error is present—both scales are named—but the comparison across different regulatory risk disclosure systems (KIID Risk and Reward Indicator vs Adviser Risk Rating Scale) is ambiguous without explicit mapping. The override from 4 to 5 is explained and client-acknowledged, satisfying the guard for override assessment. The questionnaire-to-agreed rating shift is documented. However, the mismatch between questionnaire profile (cautious responses) and the Risk 5 description (not particularly cautious), combined with the KIID showing fund category 4 for a Risk 5 portfolio, creates ambiguity about whether the profile descriptions are being applied consistently across documents.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a portfolio of two funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, Risk Rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, Risk Rating 5, 0.29% charge). The report states the client has an agreed risk rating of 5 ('Balanced'), a 'Medium' capacity for loss, and a 10-year investment term. The report asserts: 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined. This means that should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same.' The rationale links the portfolio to the client's risk profile, retirement objectives, desire for simplicity, and stated attitude to risk.
    File:     P15 (Risk Profile Report dated 18 September 2025) documents a Natural risk level of 4 ('Cautious Balanced') with an Agreed risk level of 5 ('Balanced'). P12 and P6 (SIPP research report) confirm agreed risk level 5 and describe a Balanced investor as one 'who do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person' and 'can be inclined to look for a combination of investments with differing levels of risk.' P12 shows the two recommended funds at 50% each: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc. P4 and P11 provide fund performance and cost data for these funds. P9 (KIID for HSBC fund) states the fund 'aims to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more' and is 'rated as 3, where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk' (referring to the fund's own internal risk scale). P1 (KIID) notes a recommendation that 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The canonical model shows investment term of 10 years. Capacity for loss is documented as 'Medium' in the report, supported by employment income, household income, liquid savings, and stated tolerance for underperformance (P15).
    Discrepancies:
      - The HSBC fund's KIID (P9) states a recommended minimum holding period of 5 years or more, which is shorter than the client's 10-year term and therefore consistent. However, P1 contains a warning that the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years,' which creates redundancy but not contradiction.
      - The HSBC fund KIID (P9) describes its own internal risk rating as 'rated as 3' on a scale where '1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk,' meaning the fund itself is mid-range on its internal scale. This is the fund's intrinsic SRRI and does not map to the profiler's 5-point scale on which the client's agreed rating of 5 is stated.
      - The report asserts each fund has 'Risk Rating 5,' but the HSBC fund KIID states the fund is 'rated as 3' on its internal scale. The CT Universal MAP fund's risk rating is not explicitly stated in the retrieved passages.
      - The canonical model lists 'targetRiskScaleName': 'Balanced' which corresponds to rating 5. The HSBC KIID describes rating 3 on its fund scale as 'moderate allocation' risk. These operate on different scales and cannot be directly compared as contradictions.
    The comparison can be performed. The guard rules state: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' This guard is in effect. The client's agreed risk rating of 5 ('Balanced') on the profiler's 1–9 scale is documented consistently across P15, P6, P12, and the canonical model. The recommended portfolio comprises two funds, each allocated 50%, each stated to have Risk Rating 5 in the suitability report. The HSBC fund's KIID (P9) describes it as rated 3 on its own fund-level scale (where 1–5 represents risk levels for that fund family), not the profiler's scale. This is the fund's intrinsic design, not evidence that the portfolio is misaligned with the client's profile. The guard permits this: the fund SRRI does not map to the profiler's scale. The investment term is 10 years, stated in the canonical model. The HSBC fund's recommended minimum holding period of 5+ years is shorter than this term and therefore compatible. The capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium,' supported by employment income (HGV driver £300/week, volunteer £20/week, state pension £230/week), household income (partner £1,100/month), liquid savings (£6,000), no liabilities, and stated tolerance that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.' The portfolio rationale in the report links the funds to the agreed risk profile, diversification, cost efficiency, simplicity, and the client's objectives. The report states both funds are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk.' The evidence supports that the portfolio is designed to match a Risk 5 ('Balanced') profile. No material misalignment is identified between the agreed rating, the funds' stated risk ratings, the capacity for loss, or the investment term. The funds are confirmed as part of the firm's CIP (P6, P12) and have been subject to the firm's selection process. Performance data (P4) shows both funds have delivered returns consistent with a balanced approach.
