  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Report asserts: (1) Client completed ATR questionnaire scoring risk rating 4, adviser and client agreed to increase to risk rating 5, client confirmed awareness this is 'higher than your agreed risk rating' and happiness to proceed. (2) Capacity for loss rated as 'Medium', based on state pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse income £1,100/month, combined household income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living'. (3) Investment term 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: Risk 5 Balanced portfolio, two funds each rated risk 5, 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (0.18% charge), 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (0.29% charge). (5) Strategy rationale: 'funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined...should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same'; provides diversification; blend of active/passive; client wants simplicity; charges competitive; funds performed well; part of CIP with governance. (6) Override justification: Client 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Risk profile report (P1, P8) records questionnaire completed 18 September 2025, natural risk level 4 'Cautious Balanced'; agreed risk level 5 'Balanced' following interactive discussion considering attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return; no additional notes recorded on agreed risk level. (2) P1, P3, P4: 'No additional notes were recorded' on risk level justification, investment target, and capacity for loss questionnaire sections. (3) P2, P5: Defaqto SIPP research report (dated 13 October 2025) confirms agreed risk level 5 'Balanced' and notes 'The agreed risk level considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal.' (4) File notes (P7, P10) record existing arrangements: Aviva risk 2, Zurich risk 7, Standard Life risk 4, Peoples Pension and Scottish Widows alternatives discounted. (5) P10: Zurich 'blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5'. (6) P14: Recommended investment £110,185 with £0 monthly contribution. (7) P5, P15: Client has no understanding of stocks/shares, enterprise schemes, pension planning; has only invested in cash/savings by self; does not check portfolio annually but states 'Yes, by myself'. Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses (P15) not fully quoted in suitability report. (8) P17 (suitability report text): Repeats override statement 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period' but extraction shows this conflicts with capacity for loss statement that 'pension funds form the basis of your retirement provision'. (9) Investment term: P8 shows 'Investment Period 2 years', not 10 years as stated in report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states investment term is '10-year period' (override justification, page 4); risk profile report evidence shows 'Investment Period: 2 years' (P8). These are materially different time horizons.
      - Report states override justification includes 'You are not reliant upon your pension funds' and 'wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'; capacity for loss assessment states 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' because 'pension funds form the basis of your retirement provision' (internal extraction note citing capacity for loss section, page 4). These are logically contradictory—cannot simultaneously be 'not reliant' on pension funds AND have them form 'basis of retirement provision'.
      - Report asserts risk rating increase from 4 to 5 with justification and client acknowledgement; risk profile report (P1, P3) records 'No additional notes were recorded' on agreed risk level justification, suggesting limited documented discussion of override rationale beyond questionnaire result.
      - Report lists investment term and strategy rationale without reference to risk profile report's 2-year investment period modelling (P8), creating inconsistency in stated investment horizon.
    The comparison can be performed on the core elements: ATR, CFL, strategy risk rating, and term. (1) Risk rating: Report states questionnaire result 4, agreed 5 (Balanced). File evidence (P1, P2, P5, P8) corroborates agreed rating 5 on stated 1-9 scale. Override is flagged as client choice with acknowledgement of volatility awareness (P17). This is supported. (2) Capacity for loss: Report rates as 'Medium' based on employment income, household surplus, savings and underperformance tolerance. Evidence (P1) shows risk level 5 'Balanced' was agreed 'considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal'; file notes (P10) support consolidation and readiness for retirement goal. CFL rating is supported by income/surplus narrative. (3) Critical discrepancy on investment term: Report's override justification explicitly states '10-year period'; risk profile report (P8) models 'Investment Period: 2 years'. This is a material mismatch. The questionnaire and projections appear to be based on a 2-year horizon, yet the suitability report justifies an upward risk override by reference to a 10-year horizon. This guard note applies: overrides require clear justification. An override justified by a different time horizon from the one modelled introduces doubt about whether the override was properly assessed. (4) Logical contradiction in capacity for loss narrative: The capacity for loss section states pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' (supporting Medium CFL), but override justification states client 'not reliant upon your pension funds'. These cannot both be true. The extraction's own ambiguity flag notes this contradiction. The report does not reconcile these statements. This is an unsupported, contradictory statement about capacity for loss and strategy rationale. (5) Risk-profile alignment: Both recommended funds are individually and combined risk rated 5, matching agreed rating; file notes (P10) discuss alternatives (Aviva risk 4, Zurich blend 7+2=5, Standard Life risk 4) and rationale for recommendation. Strategy risk aligns with agreed rating. (6) Strategy risk vs. CFL: Strategy risk 5 is equal to agreed ATR/CFL rating 5; no excess. This satisfies the severity guidance test. (7) Guard application: Boilerplate risk warnings in P19, P20, P22 are standard KIID/prospectus text, not unsupported client-specific statements. The contradictions identified concern client-specific statements about reliance, term, and capacity, not generic risk language. (8) Tolerance for loss not quantified: No large-loss scenario modelling in the pack. Severity guidance flags this as Low severity where not quantified and no modelling exists. Here, no modelling is evident; this is a gap but not itself a primary concern given CFL is separately assessed and rated.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report states client knowledge level is 'Moderate'; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' across general investing experience and multiple specific product types. Report describes client as 'understanding that returns are not guaranteed' suggesting some grasp of investment concepts; questionnaire explicitly records 'No understanding / knowledge' on stocks and shares, unit trusts, and pension planning products. The report asserts the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' which is consistent with the questionnaire answer 'No' to stocks and shares investment within 5 years. No discrepancy here. 10 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's stated knowledge level is 'Moderate'.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025 records the client's responses as follows: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself? No understanding / knowledge' [P10]. On understanding of specific products: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge' [P1]; 'Do you understand how Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge' [P10]; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge' [P1]. On experience: 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years? No' [P1]; 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years? No' [P1]; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? No' [P1]. The suitability report narrative states: 'Although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' [P15].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client knowledge level is 'Moderate'; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' across general investing experience and multiple specific product types
      - Report describes client as 'understanding that returns are not guaranteed' suggesting some grasp of investment concepts; questionnaire explicitly records 'No understanding / knowledge' on stocks and shares, unit trusts, and pension planning products
    The questionnaire responses on file directly contradict the report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge. The client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to the direct question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' [P10]. This is not a self-deprecating aside but a formal assessed response in the risk profile questionnaire. The client also answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to specific questions about stocks and shares [P1], unit trusts [P10], and pension planning products [P1]. The report's reference to the client understanding that 'returns are not guaranteed' [P15] appears to reflect the client's acknowledgment of investment risk rather than evidence of 'moderate' knowledge of investment products or mechanisms. The questionnaire evidence predates the suitability report (dated 18/09/2025 vs 19/09/2025) and is the formal assessment document. The fact find explicitly notes 'Your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P15], which contradicts any characterization as having 'moderate' knowledge. The guard permitting a later explanation given at meeting does not apply here: the questionnaire is the contemporaneous assessment and no evidence in the file records a discussion that upgraded the client's knowledge assessment from 'no understanding' to 'moderate' between the questionnaire completion and the report date.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the client has previously taken out cash savings products, has NOT taken out a stocks & shares investment by himself, and HAS held group pensions for some time. Specifically: 'You confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products' and 'but have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' and 'You have however held your group pensions for some time' (page 4).
    File:     The Risk profile questionnaire [P1] shows: client answered 'Yes, by myself' to 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' supporting cash savings experience. However, it also records 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' and 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' The questionnaire shows 'No' to 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' and 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' The Fact Find [P5] records client as 'Employed' with 'HGV Driver' occupation at 'CDH Recruitment', and [P18] shows existing personal pension arrangements with Aviva Platform and The Peoples Pension. Multiple pension documents ([P2] Standard Life, [P3] Scottish Widows, [P6][P8] Aviva, [P9] Zurich) evidence existing pension holdings. However, the questionnaire distinguishes between holding pensions (passive workplace/existing schemes) and having taken out pension investments by the client themselves — the questionnaire records 'No' to active pension product investment within 5 years.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' which is consistent with the questionnaire answer 'No' to stocks and shares investment within 5 years. No discrepancy here.
      - The report asserts the client 'held your group pensions for some time' — the questionnaire records 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' which on its face contradicts holding pensions for some time. However, the guard explains this: holding a workplace pension is passive experience; distinguishing it from self-directed investing is accurate.
      - The questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for both stocks & shares AND pension planning. The report does not explicitly state the client has 'no understanding' of these areas — it only negates self-directed investment experience. The questionnaire establishes understanding level separately from experience level: no understanding of pension planning is recorded despite existing holdings, indicating passive/inherited rather than self-directed arrangements.
    The report's statement about product experience is supported by the questionnaire evidence. The client has cash savings experience (confirmed as 'by myself'), has not taken out stocks & shares by himself (confirmed), and holds pensions — but the distinction matters. The questionnaire response 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' combined with 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning indicates the client's pension holdings are passive (inherited group pensions or existing arrangements, not actively selected). The multiple pension documents ([P2], [P3], [P6], [P8], [P9]) show existing holdings with various providers, but these represent historical accumulations rather than evidence of client knowledge or active investment selection. The guard acknowledges that holding a workplace pension is passive experience and that plan ownership does not prove understanding. The critical gap is that the report states the client 'held your group pensions for some time' without evidencing any understanding of pension planning or active selection; the questionnaire explicitly records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and 'No' to recent pension product investment. The recommendation is Risk 5 Balanced portfolio in a new SIPP structure, which requires understanding of self-directed pension investment and portfolio complexity. No evidence in the file documents the client's comprehension of: (1) SIPP mechanics and flexibility; (2) the recommended balanced fund portfolio; (3) the consolidation strategy across multiple providers; or (3) any explanation of why passive existing arrangements should be consolidated into an active SIPP. The risk profile [P11] notes describe Risk 5 as appropriate 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' — but the questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks & shares and pension planning. This is the material inconsistency: the report recommends a complex self-directed pension product to a client recorded in the same evidence pack as having no understanding of either stocks & shares or pension planning, with no active experience beyond passive holdings.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies the recommended product as 'Aviva Platform Personal Pension with Flexi Access Drawdown' and rates it as 'Complex'. The complexity drivers listed are: (1) Multiple investment funds (6,900+ available); (2) Flexible drawdown mechanisms; (3) Tax planning considerations (25% tax-free lump sum, income withdrawal taxation); (4) Inheritance Tax planning changes from 6 April 2027; (5) Death benefit options with different taxation regimes depending on death age; (6) Lifetime allowance and annual allowance considerations. The report states on page 4 that the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' and describes the client's knowledge and experience level. The report also states (page 4) that the client 'understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken.' The report indicates the client is Risk 5 (increased from an initial Risk 4 questionnaire result).
    File:     The case file contains extensive product literature on the Aviva Pension Portfolio and Flexi Access Drawdown mechanism. Key Features document [P20] sets out that the product offers 'flexibility and a range of options to control how your retirement pension pot is invested' and states retirement options include taking up to 25% tax-free lump sum with drawdown, annuity purchase, or full cash lump sum. The Terms and Conditions [P1] detail Flexi-access drawdown options including single drawdown and Self-Select phased income drawdown. [P4] explains the Lifetime Allowance was removed from 6 April 2023 and is replaced by Lump Sum Allowance (£268,275) and Lump Sum and Death Benefit Allowance (£1,073,100), Annual Allowance (£60,000 per annum 2024/2025), and notes changes from 6 April 2027 regarding IHT treatment of pensions. The Fund Factsheets [P8], [P9] show the recommended investment (HSBC CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) with ongoing charge 0.29% and minimum investment £250,000, rated 4 on Risk and Reward scale with exposure to equities 30%-70%. The SIPP research report [P16], [P19] provides investment performance data and holdings information. However, the file does not contain explicit evidence of the client's prior investment knowledge or experience beyond the statement in the report that the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself.' No evidence of client questions, explanations, comprehension testing, or communications regarding the complex features is present in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has not taken out stocks & shares investments themselves (page 4), yet recommends a Risk 5 complex product with 6,900+ fund choices and multiple tax planning features requiring active decision-making. This creates tension between stated limited experience and product complexity.
      - The report states the client 'understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility' of Flexi Access Drawdown, but the file contains no evidence—such as Q&A, adviser notes, or client acknowledgements—corroborating this understanding or demonstrating comprehension of the complex features (death benefits, tax treatment after age 75, IHT changes from 6 April 2027).
      - The report identifies multiple complex features (IHT changes from 6 April 2027, death benefit taxation regimes, Lump Sum Allowance, Annual Allowance) but the file does not show how these were explained to the client or whether the client was asked questions to verify understanding of these features.
      - The internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction (page 4 states pension funds 'form the basis of your retirement provision' for capacity-for-loss assessment, yet page 4 states client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' for attitude-to-risk justification) creates ambiguity about whether the product's complexity is truly suitable for the client's actual dependency on these funds.
    The requirement is to assess whether the complexity of recommended products is consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience, and whether the rationale is adequately justified. On the primary suitability assertion: The report classifies the product as Complex and identifies valid complexity drivers (6,900+ fund choices, flexible drawdown, tax planning, IHT changes, death benefit taxation, allowances). The product literature [P20], [P1], [P4] corroborates that these features exist and are genuinely complex. On consistency with client knowledge: The report itself acknowledges on page 4 that the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself,' which indicates limited prior investment experience. The Severity guidance states the highest severity concern is 'complex product recommended to a client the file records as having no or limited relevant knowledge.' The client is here recorded as having limited (or no) relevant prior stocks & shares experience. On evidence of comprehension: Where a product is complex, the decision logic states 'comprehension is corroborated in category C where the product is complex.' The file contains no evidence—fact find notes, adviser-client discussion records, question-and-answer logs, or client acknowledgements—that the client understood or was tested on understanding of: (1) the 6,900+ fund selection mechanism; (2) the implications of Flexi Access Drawdown (ongoing investment decisions required, income timing flexibility, interaction with tax planning); (3) the death benefit taxation differences before and after age 75; (4) the IHT changes from 6 April 2027; (5) the interaction of Lump Sum Allowance and Annual Allowance. The report asserts on page 4 that the client 'understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility,' but this is stated by the report and not evidenced by any supporting dialogue, explanation notes, or client comprehension verification in the file. The file holds extensive product literature and regulatory information [P1], [P4], [P8], [P9], [P20], [P21], [P23], [P24] confirming the product is genuinely complex and these features exist. However, no evidence bridges the gap between the client's stated limited experience ('not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself') and the demonstrated complexity of the recommended product. The guard 'A mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex; do not escalate on product volume or value alone' does not apply: Flexi Access Drawdown with 6,900+ fund choices and active management requirements is not mainstream in the sense of low-discretion default arrangements; it is genuinely complex and does require client participation in investment selection and drawdown decisions. On adequacy of supporting rationale: The report provides a broad list of complexity drivers but does not explain how these were discussed with the client, what the client's specific needs are regarding each feature (e.g., why 6,900 fund choices are necessary for this client), or how the adviser satisfied themselves the client comprehended the product. The rationale for suitability rests on assertions (client 'understands pension freedoms') that are not supported by evidence in the file.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts affordability based on: (1) Initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% advice fee); (2) Ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 per annum (1% ongoing charge, first year); (3) Combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (4) Client holds savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (5) Client confirmed potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living; (6) No issues with cashflow forecast.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] evidences: Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income of £(288.00) — a monthly deficit of £288. Current liquid assets are £6,000 savings [P5]. The planning scenarios [P2] and [P5] show employment income continuing until 2027 (client age 71), with State Pension of £11,973 p.a. beginning at age 66. No ongoing pension contribution commitments are identified in the recommendations [P19]. The initial advice fee of £3,305.55 is stated in [P7] and [P22] as being paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer [P7]. The ongoing charge of £1,101.85 is stated as paid via the Aviva Platform [P22].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states combined income 'comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' but Fact Find [P1] shows Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00, yielding a monthly deficit of £(288.00), not a surplus
      - Report asserts 'no issues with your cash flow forecast' but Fact Find [P1] current monthly cash flow shows disposable income of £(288.00) — a structural monthly shortfall
      - Report states affordability based on surplus income but the underlying fact find shows monthly expenditure exceeds net income by £288
    The report's affordability assertion rests on stated surplus income and adequate emergency reserves. However, the supporting evidence reveals a critical inconsistency: the Fact Find [P1] on which the affordability assessment must rest shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 against Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, producing a Monthly Disposable Income of £(288.00) — a recurring monthly deficit, not surplus. The report's reference to expenditure 'of approximately £1,700 per month' aligns with the £1,718.00 figure in the Fact Find, but the report omits that net income (£1,430.00) falls substantially short of this amount. The initial fee of £3,305.55 is correctly identified as payable from pension transfer values [P7], so the guard excluding one-off transfers applies and this does not create a new affordability commitment from income. The ongoing charge of £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £92 per month) [P22] is small relative to the identified monthly shortfall of £288, but it is an additional commitment on top of a household that is already running a monthly deficit. The £6,000 emergency reserves are noted in both the report and the Fact Find [P1], but given the structural monthly shortfall, these reserves face depletion risk. The planning scenarios [P2], [P5] show Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions remaining stable through the projection period only because employment income is modelled to continue until 2027 (client's stated retirement age of 71). The ambiguity flagged in the extraction metadata about whether employment income continues beyond retirement (candidate readings differ on this) creates further uncertainty. The report's conclusion that there are 'no issues with your cash flow forecast' cannot be reconciled with the Fact Find evidence of a £288 monthly deficit. This is a material data inconsistency, not a difference in timing or basis that a guard would explain.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: Total monthly net income £3,486.67 (HGV driver £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + employment £1,100 + State Pension £1,000). Total monthly expenditure £1,700. Monthly surplus £1,786.67. Total assets £6,000 (cash). Total liabilities £0. Net worth £6,000. State Pension income modelled at £11,973 per annum. Cashflow baseline shows no shortfall to age 100. High inflation stress test (6%) shows no shortfall. Combined 30% market crash plus 6% inflation shows no shortfall. Employment income continues; report states client 'expect to retire within the next two years' (age 71). All surplus income spent (0% saved). Male life expectancy age 86, female age 88. Pension funds transfer value £110,185 (consolidating Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002). Recommendation shows growth required 4.8% annually to age 75 to match existing plan at mid-growth 2.95%.
    File:     Fact Find [P2] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00. Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00. Monthly Disposable Income -£288.00 (deficit, not surplus). Expenditure breakdown [P21] itemises: Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food £300, Car/Travel £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100 = £1,718 monthly. Income [P21] shows State Pension £230/month (£1,100 not included as separate line). Employment income CDH Recruitment £1,200 (not £1,300 as HGV driver). intelliflo Baseline [P14] shows opening pension values: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples £6,601, Scottish Widows £211 = £116,997 total (not £110,185). Liquid assets £6,000 [P14]. intelliflo proposed scenario [P12] shows income statement first year: State Pension £11,973, Employment £29,840 combined (JS £16,640 + KS £13,200), Total Regular Income £41,813. intelliflo scenarios [P22], [P9] show baseline and proposed projections to age 100 with no red bars (no shortfall). [P23] shows timeline events: 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027, 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026. Expenditure [P8], [P12] detail first year: Tax & NI £3,385, Entertainment £1,200, Household £15,660, Living £1,656, Transport £1,800, Miscellaneous £300 = £24,001 annual (£2,000/month), higher than £1,700 stated.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report total monthly net income £3,486.67 vs Fact Find total net monthly income £1,430.00 — 144% higher in report.
      - Report monthly HGV driver income £1,300 vs Fact Find employment CDH Recruitment £1,200 — £100 difference.
      - Report Sea Cadets volunteer income £86.67/month vs Fact Find shows no separate line item for volunteer work; total income is £1,430 only.
      - Report monthly State Pension £1,000 vs Fact Find State Pension £230/month — £770 difference.
      - Report total monthly expenditure £1,700 vs Fact Find total monthly expenditure £1,718.00 — £18 difference. More significantly, intelliflo detailed expenditure [P8], [P12] shows £24,001 annual or approximately £2,000/month, not £1,700.
      - Report monthly surplus £1,786.67 vs Fact Find shows monthly deficit of -£288.00 (1,430 - 1,718).
      - Report pension consolidation transfer value £110,185 vs intelliflo opening pension total £116,997 — £6,812 difference. Report page 3 narrative states 'approximately £110,000' but table [P14] sums to £116,997.
      - Report states £3,002 Standard Life transfer value but computed figures show charge row EA5 uses fund value £35,805.77 (not £3,002), indicating the Standard Life row in the charge analysis is computed on wrong plan.
      - Report critical yield table [P4] uses mid-growth 2.95% but modelling note [P18] states 2.94% — £4,672 outperformance needed but charge arithmetic shows this implies fund value £110,188.68, matching neither existing total (£116,997) nor recommended (£110,185).
      - Report assumption 'Retirement age 75' for critical yield [P4], [P18] vs planning timeline [P23] showing 'Retire (John) age 71' and 'Retire (Kim) age 66' — events occur before age 75, not at age 75.
      - Report states 'all surplus income is spent' (0% saved) with monthly surplus £1,786.67 but Fact Find shows monthly deficit -£288.00, making surplus spending irrelevant to affordability.
    The comparison reveals material contradictions between what the report asserts about monthly cash position and what the Fact Find and planning documents evidence. Most critical: the report claims monthly net income of £3,486.67 and monthly expenditure of £1,700, yielding a surplus of £1,786.67. The Fact Find [P2] directly contradicts this, showing total net monthly income of £1,430.00 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding a DEFICIT of -£288.00, not a surplus. The guardian about charts not being reliably parsable does not apply here: [P2] is a Fact Find table, not a chart. The discrepancy arises from the report's income construction. The report sums £1,300 (HGV driver) + £86.67 (Sea Cadets volunteer) + £1,100 (employment) + £1,000 (State Pension assumed monthly) = £3,486.67. However, the Fact Find [P2], [P21] shows total income of only £1,430.00. Breaking this down: State Pension £230/month is £1,100 less than the £1,000/month the report uses. The HGV driver income of £1,300 appears nowhere in Fact Find; the only employment line shows CDH Recruitment £1,200. Sea Cadets £86.67 is not itemised in the Fact Find. The canonical model extraction acknowledges ambiguity about whether employment income (£300/week HGV + £20/week volunteer) continues beyond retirement at age 71, but this does not explain the £1,100/month surplus income the report asserts from current income, which the Fact Find directly contradicts by showing a -£288 deficit. Additionally, the intelliflo modelling documents [P8], [P12] show first-year regular expenditure of £24,001 (approximately £2,000/month), not £1,700. The report's affordability conclusion ('no shortfall to age 100,' cashflow 'has no issues') rests on both the income surplus claim and the modelling scenarios [P22], [P9], [P12], all of which show no red bars (no shortfall) to age 100. However, those scenarios use different income figures. The intelliflo Income Statement [P12] for proposed scenario shows Total Regular Income £41,813 annually (£3,484/month combined household), which aligns with the report's stated income if both spouses are included; but the Fact Find [P2] total is clearly marked as household total £1,430.00, not individual. The pension values are also inconsistent: report states £110,185 transfer value; Fact Find and intelliflo [P14] show total pensions £116,997. Report page 3 narrative states 'approximately £110,000' (closer to proposed recommendation) but the underlying Fact Find sums to £116,997. The charge analysis (computed figures) shows the Standard Life row (EA5) is computed on a fund value of £35,805.77 (matching Zurich, not the £3,002 Standard Life value), indicating a labelling or mapping error that corrupts the critical yield analysis. The report's affordability rests on modelled outcomes ('no shortfall'), but the input data contradicts the Fact Find across income, expenditure and asset valuations. A core guard states 'An input the client does not have is a defect in the model, not a rounding difference.' If the Fact Find shows income of £1,430/month, employment of £1,200 (not £1,300 HGV plus £86.67 volunteer), and State Pension of £230/month (not £1,000/month), then the modelling assumptions do not match the established facts. This is a data consistency failure. The 5% tolerance on ValueMatch does not apply: the income discrepancies are far larger (e.g., State Pension £230 vs £1,000 is 335% overstated; HGV £1,300 vs £1,200 employment is within 8% but the sea cadets £86.67 is unsubstantiated). The report's conclusion that affordability is supported by cashflow showing 'no shortfall' cannot be validated where the input data contradictions are this material.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the client holds savings of around £6,000 as at 2025-10-14. It states 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning.' The report identifies two access needs: (1) Emergency access via savings/liquid cash for unexpected emergencies (£6,000), and (2) Ad hoc access to pension funds in future, which 'will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete'. The report confirms the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds'. No specific required emergency fund amount is quantified in the report.
    File:     Fact Find [P6] records cash savings of £6,000.00 as at 22/09/2025. Intelliflo baseline scenario [P16] and proposed scenario [P7] show liquid assets declining gradually: baseline shows £5,912 in 2027, declining to £5,573 by 2035; proposed scenario shows £5,912 in 2027, declining to £5,869 by 2028. The cashflow table [P5] in the baseline scenario shows the same liquid assets throughout (approximately £123k combining liquid and accessible pensions, but the 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' column shows consistent positive cashflow each year with no year showing a deficit that would require drawing on emergency savings. Expenditure analysis [P14] and [P18] in Fact Find shows total monthly expenditure of £1,718 against net monthly income of £1,430, yielding a deficit of £288 per month (£3,456 per annum). Intelliflo baseline [P23] shows first year regular expenditure of £24,001 against total regular income of £41,813, yielding surplus of £17,812. The proposed scenario [P7] asset table shows liquid assets starting at £6,000 in 2025 and declining to £5,956 by 2026, then further declining to £5,825 by 2029.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client 'has confirmed' sufficiency of emergency fund but provides no quantified threshold for what constitutes 'sufficient' for this client's circumstances
      - Fact Find monthly cashflow [P14] shows net monthly deficit of £288 (£3,456 pa shortfall), but Intelliflo planning scenarios [P23] show substantial first-year surplus of £17,812, indicating material inconsistency in underlying financial assumptions between documents
      - Liquid assets projected to decline steadily from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,825-£5,912 by 2029 in both scenarios [P7][P16], yet report contains no discussion of whether depleting emergency reserves is acceptable or how reserves will be replenished
      - Intelliflo baseline scenario [P5] shows 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' column consistently showing zero deficit throughout projection, suggesting no emergency draw requirement modelled, yet the monthly Fact Find [P14] shows ongoing monthly deficit of £288
    The core discrepancy stems from fundamental inconsistency in the underlying financial model. The Fact Find [P14] and [P18] record net monthly income of £1,430 and monthly expenditure of £1,718, creating a deficit position of -£288 per month. However, Intelliflo planning [P23] shows total regular income of £41,813 and total regular expenditure of £24,001, yielding a surplus of £17,812 in the first year. These figures are incompatible — they cannot both be true from the same client circumstances. The report relies on the Intelliflo projections (which show healthy surplus and stability of liquid assets around £5,900-£6,000) rather than the Fact Find deficit position. This is material because if the Fact Find position were accurate, the £6,000 emergency fund would be depleted within approximately 1.7 years (£6,000 divided by £3,456 annual deficit). The Intelliflo scenarios do show liquid assets declining (albeit slowly) from £6,000, but the projections maintain positive 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' throughout [P5], meaning emergency fund is not drawn upon in the model. The report asserts sufficiency of the £6,000 emergency fund but provides no stated requirement or benchmark for what 'sufficient' means. The Value Match confirms the £6,000 figure is corroborated by Fact Find [P14], but the underlying cashflow assumptions that determine whether £6,000 is genuinely sufficient remain internally inconsistent across the file. The report provides access timelines (10-15 working days) for pension drawdown but does not address whether this accessibility satisfies emergency liquidity needs or whether client should maintain additional liquid reserves. No discussion addresses whether the gradual decline in liquid assets through the plan is acceptable given the inconsistent baseline cashflow assumptions.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts affordability is supported by: (1) combined income of approximately £1,430 per month net comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (2) savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (3) client confirmed potential underperformance would not impact day-to-day living; (4) no issues with cashflow forecast. Initial advice fee: £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested). Ongoing annual commitment: £1,101.85 net (1% per annum, first year). Monthly surplus derived from report: £1,786.67 net.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] [P2] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income of £-288.00 (a deficit, not surplus). Current Monthly Cash Flow shows negative disposable income. Fact Find confirms savings of £6,000 [P1]. The intelliflo planning Baseline scenario [P11] shows employment income of £16,640 and state pension totalling combined regular income of £41,813 annually (equivalent to approximately £3,485 monthly gross, or approximately £2,880 monthly net based on tax & NI deductions of £3,385 shown). First year regular expenditure totals £24,001 annually [P11]. The planning documents [P22] [P23] show liquid assets graph indicating cash reserves of approximately £6,000 available. Employment income is stated to continue to age 71 (retirement event in 2027 per [P21]), but the extraction notes ambiguity about whether this ceases at age 71 or continues beyond.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states combined income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of £1,700 per month, but Fact Find shows net monthly income of £1,430 against monthly expenditure of £1,718, yielding a monthly deficit of £288, not a surplus.
      - Report asserts monthly surplus of £1,786.67 (derived figure); Fact Find shows actual monthly disposable income as -£288.00.
      - Report states affordability supported by income meeting expenditure 'comfortably'; evidence shows expenditure exceeds income by £288 per month (20.1% overspend).
      - The £3,305.55 initial fee is stated as 3% of amount invested (£110,185), but cannot be verified against any figure in the passages; passages do not corroborate the 3% rate or the resulting fee amount.
      - The £1,101.85 ongoing annual commitment (1% per annum) is stated in the report but nearest comparable figure in evidence is £1,200 shown in employment income context [P11], not as an affordable commitment amount; no passage supports the £1,101.85 figure.
    The core finding is a material contradiction between what the report asserts about affordability and what the Fact Find evidences. The report claims income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month and derives a monthly surplus of £1,786.67. The Fact Find contemporaneously completed shows the opposite: net monthly income of £1,430 against monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly deficit of £-288.00. This is explicitly recorded in [P1] under 'Current Monthly Cash Flow' section. The deficit is 20.1% of expenditure—a material shortfall, not a comfortable surplus. The report's statement on page 4 that 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' is directly contradicted by the Fact Find's calculation showing a £288 monthly deficit. The initial fee of £3,305.55 cannot be verified in the passages by the stated 3% rate. The ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 also cannot be directly evidenced from the passages. However, the critical issue is affordability: the report asserts the recommendation is affordable based on income comfortably exceeding expenditure, but the contemporaneous evidence in the Fact Find shows income falling short of expenditure by £288 monthly. The savings of £6,000 provide a buffer, but with a monthly deficit, these reserves would be depleted within approximately 21 months (6,000 ÷ 288). The employment income assumption is noted as ambiguous in the extraction (whether it continues beyond retirement age 71 or ceases), but even taking the higher combined income figures from the intelliflo planning baseline (£41,813 annually), the expenditure totals £24,001 annually, leaving a gross surplus of approximately £17,812 annually or £1,484 monthly—before tax and NI deductions of £3,385 annually shown in [P11], reducing the net surplus materially. The report's affirmation of affordability is not supported by the actual monthly cashflow position evidenced in the Fact Find, which shows a deficit position requiring depletion of savings.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from 'Savings / liquid cash' and 'Pension arrangements'. The canonical model shows /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[] populated with these two sources. However, the extracted model shows /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall is absent from the report.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Opening liquid assets (savings) of £6,000 [P9], described as 'Cash Account' with 1.25% expected return p/a [P1]. (2) In the Baseline scenario, liquid assets decline from £6,000 (2025) to £5,912 (2027), £5,825 (2029), £5,740 (2031), reaching £5,573 (2035) and continuing to decline thereafter [P6]. (3) In the Proposed scenario, liquid assets follow the same pattern: £6,000 (2025) declining to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), £5,869 (2028), £5,825 (2029) [P10]. (4) The cashflow tables for both scenarios show 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' of 0 in every year [P4, P3, P12], meaning no surplus is being accumulated—all surplus income is consumed. (5) The Surplus Account balance shown is £0 in both baseline and proposed scenarios [P8, P13]. (6) Expenditure is stated as 'Currently £20,616 p/a' with components: Entertainment & Leisure £1,200 p/a (discretionary), Household Living £15,660 p/a (essential), Miscellaneous £1,656 p/a (essential), Transport £300 p/a (essential), and unspecified other £1,800 p/a (essential) [P10]. (7) The liquidation order shows: Surplus Account (£0) liquidated 1st, Savings (£6,000) liquidated 2nd, then pension arrangements [P1]. (8) The plan extends to 2060 (age 104/100) with positive in-year surpluses throughout until 2056, where a deficit of £(2,930) appears [P3]. (9) No explicit emergency fund quantum is stated in the suitability report or supporting documents.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts funding from 'Savings / liquid cash' but does not quantify or define an emergency fund reserve
      - Liquid assets decline steadily from £6,000 to below £5,600 within 10 years despite stated surpluses, contradicting any assertion of fund preservation
      - Surplus Account balance is £0 in both scenarios despite positive in-year surpluses—0% of surplus income is saved [P8]
      - No explicit statement in report or file about what constitutes an adequate emergency fund or how much is reserved for emergencies versus general liquidity
    The requirement tests whether affordability is supported by evidence of emergency fund provision and whether capital remains accessible when needed. Three issues emerge: (1) QUANTIFICATION: The report is silent on emergency fund size. The extracted model confirms /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall is absent. The only liquid cash identified is £6,000 in savings, which is not explicitly designated as an emergency fund and is shown in the liquidation hierarchy as second (after a zero surplus account). (2) DEPLETION: The cashflow model shows the assumption 'Surplus Income Percentage Saved: 0% of any in-year surplus will be saved' [P8]. This means all annual surpluses flow out rather than accumulate. Simultaneously, liquid assets decline steadily through the projection period [P6, P10]. By 2035, liquid assets have fallen to £5,573 in baseline [P6]. At age 79/75, with ~£5,500 liquid capital against £20,616 p/a expenditure, the buffer is less than 3.2 weeks' worth of living costs. (3) ACCESS & GUARD APPLICATION: The guard states 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital.' The Surplus Account (£0) is liquidated first, savings second [P1]. The savings are being gradually drawn down through the plan to meet cashflow needs, not retained as an accessible buffer. The steady decline in 'Liquid Assets' from £6,000 to £5,573 over 10 years [P6, P10] reflects systematic drawdown. By 2056, a year-on-year deficit of £(2,930) appears [P3], indicating the model itself predicts the liquid buffer will be exhausted. (4) NO DEDICATED EMERGENCY PROVISION: The report does not identify a separate emergency fund, state an emergency fund target, or verify that an emergency reserve survives the projection. The only 'liquid' amount is savings set to decline. This is inconsistent with establishing that affordability accounts for financial resilience and liquidity as required by COBS 9.2.1R-9.2.2R. (5) The report states liquidity needs are met from 'Savings / liquid cash' and 'Pension arrangements' but does not evidence that savings are protected from general drawdown or that an emergency buffer is quantified and maintained.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with Flexi Access Drawdown at a selected retirement age of 67. The report states 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken.' The report asserts this provides flexibility for the client's access needs. No specific access dates, withdrawal amounts, or liquidity requirements are quantified in the extracted model.
    File:     The Aviva Platform Key Features document [P4] states 'You can normally choose to take benefits from your Pension Portfolio at any time from the normal minimum pension age. This is currently age 55. From 6 April 2028 this will be age 57 unless you have a protected pension age.' [P14] confirms under Flexi-access drawdown 'You can choose to draw down any level of income your pension pot can support.' The intelliflo planning documents [P12] and [P19] show the client is age 69 on report date (14/10/2025) with a planned retirement age of 71, not 67. The baseline scenario [P12] shows liquid assets of £6,000 in savings and no quantified emergency fund. The proposed scenario [P19] shows the same £6,000 liquid assets with consolidation into a new Aviva pension of £110,185. The fact find [P21] records £6,000 cash savings. No planned withdrawals, contributions, or access needs schedule is detailed in any document. No emergency fund provision or adequacy commentary is evident.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report selected retirement age is 67; planning documents show planned retirement at age 71 for the client (John)
      - Report asserts flexibility through FAD but does not quantify any planned withdrawal amounts, frequencies, or dates
      - Report does not evidence an emergency fund or address emergency fund adequacy
      - No specific access needs or liquidity requirements from the financial position are documented in support of the wrapper selection
      - Liquid assets of £6,000 are noted but no assessment of adequacy as emergency reserves is provided
      - Capital contributions or ongoing commitments are not quantified in the report's affordability analysis
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific planned withdrawal amounts and dates when capital or income will be required, Emergency fund target and assessment of £6,000 adequacy, Quantified ongoing contribution commitments if any, Detailed cashflow projections showing income, expenditure and surplus at each stage of the plan, Explicit linkage between identified access needs and wrapper suitability, Required access dates for any capital that would be locked during accumulation phases
    The requirement asks whether affordability is detailed and fully supported. The comparison cannot be properly performed because critical inputs are missing from both the report and the case file. The report asserts flexibility through FAD but provides no quantified basis for this assertion: no withdrawal schedule, no identified access dates, no emergency fund assessment, and no demonstration that the recommended wrapper's access terms align with the client's actual needs. The extracted model shows a discrepancy between the report's stated retirement age of 67 and the planning documents' retirement age of 71 for the client. The report does not document planned contributions or withdrawals in a way that can be cross-checked against the planning scenarios. The £6,000 liquid assets are recorded but never assessed for adequacy as an emergency fund. The planning documents show a cashflow and liquidity analysis (visible in [P24] graphs showing expenditure and required withdrawals), but this analysis is not explicitly referenced in the suitability report's affordability section to demonstrate that the recommendation is supported by actual cashflow modelling. The guard for pension access is inapplicable here: the client is 69, well above minimum pension age of 55, so any access restrictions in the wrapper are not a barrier. However, the absence of any documented access need or emergency fund assessment means the suitability of the wrapper cannot be validated against real financial resilience requirements. The report's statement that the client 'prefer[s] the flexibility' of FAD is an assertion of preference, not evidence that flexibility is needed or that access will be available when required.

  [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts State Pension income category for two income streams (client and spouse). Extracted model shows `/financialPosition/incomes[]/startsOn` of "2026-08-07" for one entry. The extraction records employment income starting 2026-08-07 and State Pension entries but does not capture gross amounts (`/financialPosition/incomes[]/gross` is absent from extracted model). Report's own text on page 3 states client receives 'full State Pension of £230 per week' and spouse will receive 'around £230 per week'. Page 14 cashflow modelling inputs show 'state pension income of £11,973 per annum' for client.
    File:     Fact Find [P6] lists State Pension as Monthly income of £230.00 (gross and net both stated as £230.00, dated 22/09/2025). This annualises to £230 × 12 = £2,760 per annum. Intelliflo planning documents [P1] and [P4] show STATE PENSION AGE as 66 for both clients (JS age 69 on report date, spouse age 65). Planning timeline [P4] shows 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026. Planning cashflow table [P12] lists income categories including State Pension but does not display the State Pension figure in the excerpt provided. Page 3 narrative states client receives 'full State Pension of £230 per week' which annualises to £230 × 52 = £11,960 per annum.
    Discrepancies:
      - Fact Find [P6] shows State Pension income as £230 monthly (£2,760 annualised), contradicting both the report's own statement of £230 per week (£11,960 annualised) and the cashflow input of £11,973 annualised.
      - Report page 14 cashflow states 'state pension income of £11,973 per annum' but report page 3 states '£230 per week' which is £11,960 annualised. Discrepancy of £13 per annum between these two figures in the report itself.
      - Fact Find [P6] records State Pension frequency as 'Monthly' with amount £230.00, but all other evidence (report narrative and cashflow modelling) treats it as weekly or annualised at weekly rates.
      - Report's startsOn date of 2026-08-07 for one income entry does not align with State Pension age of 66 (which for client born 07/06/1956 would be 14/06/2022, already passed) or spouse aged 65 born 07/05/1960 (state pension age 66, reaching it 07/05/2026).
    The report contains an internal inconsistency: page 3 states State Pension at £230 per week (£11,960 annualised), but page 14 cashflow inputs show £11,973 annualised—a £13 discrepancy unexplained within the report. The Fact Find [P6] records the same income as £230 monthly, not weekly, which would annualise to £2,760. This is a material difference (£9,200 annual gap). The weekly reading from the report narrative (£230/week = £11,960 pa) aligns with the cashflow modelling figure of £11,973 (within £13, which falls within rounding), but contradicts the Fact Find's monthly frequency and amount. The Guard on State Pension forecasts instructs to annualise weekly figures at 52 weeks before comparison; applying this, the report's £230/week × 52 = £11,960, matching the narrative but not the Fact Find's £230 monthly. The client is already 69 on report date (14/10/2025) and would have begun receiving State Pension at age 66 (around 14/06/2022), yet the startsOn date of 2026-08-07 appears to reference a future event (possibly spouse's State Pension age 66 on 07/05/2026, age entry being 2026-08-07 is not explained). The affordability assessment depends on State Pension income; the Fact Find shows monthly frequency (contradicting weekly), creating uncertainty about which figure grounds the cashflow. The £13 discrepancy between 11,960 and 11,973 is minor but the monthly vs. weekly ambiguity in [P6] versus the report is material. Spouse's State Pension amount is stated as 'around £230 per week' (page 3) but no separate annualised figure is provided in the cashflow; it is unclear whether the £11,973 input includes spouse's income or client's alone.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client (John) retirement on 2027-10-14 with loss of HGV driver employment income of £300/week (£1,300/month net); (2) Spouse (Kim) retirement on 2026-08-07 with loss of employment income of £1,100/month net; (3) Spouse commencing State Pension on 2026-08-07 (gain of £230/week or approximately £1,000/month net); (4) Inheritance Tax treatment change for pensions from 6 April 2027. The report also asserts an emergency fund of £6,000 in liquid capital to cover unexpected emergencies, with pension access stated as 10-15 working days for administration.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1, P14, P16) records: Total net monthly income of £1,430 comprising employment income of £1,200/month (CDH Recruitment) and State Pension of £230/month; total monthly expenditure of £1,718; monthly disposable income of -£288 (deficit). Liquid savings of £6,000 in cash account. Client stated 'no' to expected changes in income and 'no' to expected changes in expenditure (P1). The Retirement Planning Notes (P17) reference 'retirement in the next 2 years' and 'needs household income of £1,700 net per month'. The intelliflo planning scenarios (P2, P6, P12) show timeline events: Kim retires 2026 age 66, John retires 2027 age 71. Current monthly shortfall of £288 before any income changes. Liquid assets remain at approximately £5,000-£6,000 throughout the plan projection (P2).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts £300/week HGV driver income continues until retirement in 2027, but Fact Find (P1, P14) shows only £1,200/month employment income from CDH Recruitment with no breakdown by source or confirmation that HGV income of £300/week is included in this figure
      - Report projects Kim's retirement income loss of £1,100/month, but Fact Find does not record Kim's employment income separately or detail her gross/net earnings by source
      - Report states household expects income needs of approximately £1,700/month net at retirement, but current monthly deficit is £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718); retirement income need stated as £1,700 is not reconciled with current shortfall
      - Fact Find explicitly records 'no' to expected income changes and 'no' to expected expenditure changes (P1), contradicting the report's assertion of material income changes on retirement dates
      - Report projects emergency fund of £6,000 and states this is sufficient to cover unexpected emergencies, but does not quantify the period this fund is intended to cover or reconcile it against the existing monthly deficit of £288
      - Report asserts pension access will take 10-15 working days, but does not reference any forward liquidity planning for the projected ongoing monthly deficit during retirement
    The report's foreseeable changes (Kim's retirement 2026-08-07, John's retirement 2027-10-14) are corroborated by the intelliflo planning timeline (P12). However, a material inconsistency exists: the Fact Find (P1) explicitly states the client answered 'No' to 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future?' This is a direct contradiction to the report's detailed projection of two retirement events with specified income losses. The report has inferred income cessation dates and amounts from the stated retirement intentions, but the Fact Find records no changes anticipated. The current cash position is £6,000 liquid savings against a monthly deficit of £288. The report asserts this £6,000 is 'sufficient' for emergencies, but does not explain: (a) how long this fund is intended to sustain the household during retirement, (b) whether it remains adequate after both retirements, or (c) how the ongoing monthly deficit of £288 will be met post-retirement without pension drawdown. The intelliflo proposed scenario (P2) shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,411 in 2039, remaining relatively stable, which implies pension drawdown or other income sources are required to meet the shortfall—but the report does not detail the affordability of required pension withdrawals. The report cites retirement dates and income changes with precise amounts inferred from employment income and State Pension information, but the reconciliation between current deficit (£288/month) and retirement income needs (£1,700/month stated in P17) is not explicit in the suitability report. The guard 'Expenditure already inside the monthly budget must not be counted a second time' applies: the current £1,718 expenditure is already recorded; the report must explain how retirement income meets both this base expenditure and the stated need of £1,700/month net. The Fact Find's 'no changes' answer is a direct contradiction that cannot be explained by a guard.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.8, G6.9). Rationale stated in canonical model names 'consolidation' as a primary benefit (appearing in at least 3 rationale statements: 'Streamlined pension management through consolidation of remaining pensions', 'Consolidation of multiple pension arrangements into a single provider provides clearer understanding and management of pension funds', and implicit in 'Client priority is to consolidate pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement'), but the evidence does not provide the full text of these rationales verbatim to verify they are as extracted. The policy research file (P8) confirms the strategic intent ('Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together') but does not quote the specific rationale language from the suitability report. The canonical model states a rationale 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement. If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' (linked to retention of People's Pension). This is marked isClientSpecific=true. However, the evidence in the passages does not reproduce this text verbatim. The policy research file (P8) notes only 'Active workplace pension' for People's Pension recommendation, not the fuller rationale. The canonical model lists rationales including 'The funds do not meet your risk profile and there are no Multi-Asset funds and only a limited range of other options' and 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds; to achieve this profile, a blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 fund is required with no diversified multi-asset funds available within risk range'. These are specific to the client's agreed risk level 5. However, the passages do not provide evidence of the detailed fund availability analysis for each legacy scheme (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows). P8 notes for Zurich state 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' without mentioning the risk level 5 fund unavailability rationale. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report provides 18 rationale statements across 6 recommendations. The report claims 16 of these 18 rationales are client-specific (isClientSpecific=true), with 2 marked as generic ('The charges are competitive' and 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers'). Each recommendation is linked to at least one of three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3). Rationales address product fit, cost comparisons, consolidation benefits, risk alignment, fund diversification, and investment strategy simplicity.
    File:     Evidence from the case file shows: (1) The fact find (P2) records client declaration that 'my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document' but the objectives themselves are not quoted in the passages provided. (2) The risk profile report (P12, P11) documents that risk level was agreed at 5, that 'the natural risk level...is used as a starting point' and that 'we came to an agreed risk level for your specific investment objective,' but contains no additional notes recorded on investment target or agreed risk level discussion. (3) The policy research file (P8) lists objectives as 'ATR 5 CIP funds' and notes for each scheme ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' for Aviva; 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' for Zurich; 'Active workplace pension' for People's Pension; similar notes for others), but these are terse and do not elaborate on how each recommendation meets stated client goals. (4) The suitability report (P10, P14) states recommendations including 'Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension' and describes ongoing services (1% annual fee, market updates, access to advisers), but the passages do not contain the full narrative rationale linking each recommendation to the three objectives. (5) The intelliflo planning scenario (P3, P6) contains assumptions and cashflow structure but no direct rationale statements. (6) The SIPP research report (P5, P7) provides fund cost disclosure tables and performance data but not the textual rationale connecting fund choice to client objectives.
    Discrepancies:
      - Rationale stated in canonical model names 'consolidation' as a primary benefit (appearing in at least 3 rationale statements: 'Streamlined pension management through consolidation of remaining pensions', 'Consolidation of multiple pension arrangements into a single provider provides clearer understanding and management of pension funds', and implicit in 'Client priority is to consolidate pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement'), but the evidence does not provide the full text of these rationales verbatim to verify they are as extracted. The policy research file (P8) confirms the strategic intent ('Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together') but does not quote the specific rationale language from the suitability report.
      - The canonical model states a rationale 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement. If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' (linked to retention of People's Pension). This is marked isClientSpecific=true. However, the evidence in the passages does not reproduce this text verbatim. The policy research file (P8) notes only 'Active workplace pension' for People's Pension recommendation, not the fuller rationale.
      - The canonical model lists rationales including 'The funds do not meet your risk profile and there are no Multi-Asset funds and only a limited range of other options' and 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds; to achieve this profile, a blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 fund is required with no diversified multi-asset funds available within risk range'. These are specific to the client's agreed risk level 5. However, the passages do not provide evidence of the detailed fund availability analysis for each legacy scheme (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows). P8 notes for Zurich state 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' without mentioning the risk level 5 fund unavailability rationale.
      - The canonical model includes rationale 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution and has a £4.50 annual admin charge' for Aviva. The extraction process identified this as internally inconsistent: page 11 of the report shows Aviva existing charge as 0.50% vs recommended 0.44%, which is a 0.06% saving (making recommended cheaper, not more expensive). The passages retrieved do not include the suitability report's charge comparison table to verify which statement is accurate.
      - The canonical model states rationale 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life. The extraction metadata flags this as internally inconsistent: page 11 shows Standard Life 0.18% vs recommended 0.44%, making the recommended plan 0.26% MORE expensive, not 0.08% cheaper. The passages do not contain the comparison table to corroborate either figure.
      - Rationales marked as client-specific include 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and 'Multi-asset funds provide a professionally managed, diversified investment solution that aligns with financial goals and risk profile'. These reference 'your' and 'agreed level of risk' but the passages do not quote the client's specific goals verbatim or the agreed risk level discussions in detail (only P12 confirms risk level 5 was agreed, with no recorded additional notes).
      - The canonical model lists two rationales as not client-specific (isClientSpecific=false): 'The charges are competitive' and 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers'. These are generic. However, the passages include P7 (SIPP research report) which provides detailed cost disclosure tables and states funds within the Defaqto peer group achieved 'most consistent risk adjusted returns, over the preceding 5 years discrete period', suggesting some performance comparison work was done. The passages do not confirm whether these generic rationales were presented without the supporting detail or whether the detail was in the suitability report but not retrieved.
      - Rationale 'The funds are part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition), regularly reviewed by a quarterly investment committee and monitored by a leading external research team' is marked client-specific in the canonical model, but the passages do not provide evidence of CIP documentation or quarterly review records to corroborate this claim.
      - Objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 are referenced by all recommendations but the passages do not quote or define what these three objectives are, making it impossible to verify whether each recommendation is genuinely linked to a stated goal or whether the linkage is asserted without evidence.

    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the full text of the suitability report is not in the evidence pack. The canonical model contains 18 rationale statements extracted from the report, but only fragments of these appear in the retrieved passages. The most critical gap is the definition of the three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) that every recommendation claims to meet. Without knowing what these are, the requirement to link recommendations to 'client-specific objectives' cannot be assessed.
    
    The policy research file (P8) does show that the underlying strategy document records the client's core motivation ('he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement'), which aligns with the canonical model's rationale about consolidation and control. This suggests client-specific rationale does exist, at least in part. However, several specific rationale claims cannot be corroborated: (1) The detailed fund availability analysis for each legacy scheme (why Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds; why Standard Life funds do not meet the profile) is not evidenced in the passages. (2) The cost comparison rationales contain internal inconsistencies flagged in the extraction metadata (Aviva described as '0.06% more expensive' but calculation shows it is cheaper; Standard Life described as '0.08% more expensive' but calculation shows recommended is significantly more expensive). Without the source comparison table, these contradictions cannot be resolved. (3) Two rationales are explicitly marked generic ('The charges are competitive'; 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers'), and while supporting data exists (P7 shows performance and cost disclosures), the rationales as stated are boilerplate and not personalised. (4) No evidence shows alternatives were considered and rejected for each recommendation; the requirement explicitly calls for this but no alternative analysis appears in the passages.
    
    The risk profile report (P12) confirms that risk level was discussed and agreed at 5, and that projections were produced and discussed, supporting the claim that rationales are grounded in a personalised process. However, the actual content of those discussions (what was said, what alternatives were presented) is not recorded in the passages retrieved.
    
    The extraction metadata identified that rationales stating cost comparisons contradict the derived charge figures, which is a material internal inconsistency. If the suitability report states 'plan is 0.06% more expensive' but the comparison table shows 0.06% cheaper, the rationale is factually wrong and contradicts the report's own data. This is not a problem with the evidence pack's incompleteness; it is a problem within the report itself and needs to be resolved.
    
    The client agreement (P13) confirms the client agreed to advice and signed acknowledgement of financial planning objectives, and the fact find (P2) records client confirmation that objectives 'identified and prioritised in this document' are correct. This suggests objectives were discussed and recorded, but they are not quoted in the passages provided.
