  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Provider selection rationale includes 'It is the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' (client-specific, linked to OBJ1-3); 'It provides an extremely tax-efficient way of building up a retirement fund' (generic); 'A platform is a service that allows you to hold one or a number of different investment vehicles under one roof' (generic explanation); 'Aviva platform because it offers these benefits alongside financial strength and experience as a leading investments provider. They have a proven track record of excellent customer service, with competitive and transparent charges' (provider-specific but not comparative). (2) Investment strategy rationale includes: 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' (client-specific, linked to OBJ1-3); 'It provides exposure to a range of asset classes' (generic); 'It provides a blend of active and passive investments, which allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low' (generic); 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' (client-specific, linked to OBJ3); 'The charges are competitive' (generic assertion); 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' (generic comparative claim); 'The funds are part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition). This is regularly reviewed by a quarterly investment committee' (firm governance, generic); lengthy multi-asset fund rationale (client-specific, linked to OBJ1-2). (3) Recommended product: Aviva Pension Portfolio with £110,185 transfer value. (4) Recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%), both Risk Rating 5. (5) Provider financial strength: 'A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)'. (6) Alternatives: Report states 'I have considered the investment pathway solution offered by the recommended provider that is most relevant to you. However, I have discounted it because: You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions offered by the provider. It does not reflect your individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately as the recommended investment strategy. You require an investment solution that will...' (text truncated). The report also states regarding CIP vs single multi-asset fund: 'The CIP aligns with your long-term goals and offers a more robust and flexible framework compared to a single multiasset fund. It provides enhanced oversight, risk management, and adaptability.'
    File:     Evidence retrieved shows: (1) From SIPP research report [P1, P9]: Platform research completed on 4 platforms (Aviva Platform, Elevate by Aberdeen, Aegon Retirement Choices, Wealthtime Platform). All 4 products matched filters applied. No DNA analysis was performed ('No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' [P1]). Only 1 product selected: 'Pension Portfolio - Choice' by Aviva. Research area was SIPP. (2) From product research [P5]: Aviva brand history and profile provided ('The group was formed by the merger of CGU and Norwich Union in 2000'). (3) Fund performance [P12]: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C shows 5YR return of 40.06%, 10YR 122.95%, discrete annual 2024: 10.82%. CT Universal MAP Balanced C shows 5YR return of 44.73%, no 10YR data, discrete annual 2024: 11.27%. (4) Fund costs [P10]: HSBC ongoing costs 0.18%, transaction 0.01%, total 0.19%. CT Universal ongoing costs 0.29%, transaction 0.26%, total 0.55%. Combined total aggregated fund costs 0.37%. (5) Platform details [P8]: 'The Aviva Platform is a portfolio management service which allows you to combine investments in a range of underlying tax wrappers' and 'Pension Portfolio has two investment options: Core and Choice.' (6) Alternatives consideration: Policy research [P13] shows existing arrangements review with rationales for switches (Aviva 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension 'Retain - Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows noted; Standard Life noted). Pension switch report [P14] compares existing vs new solution, noting 'Analysis was undertaken on 23 October 2025 to investigate the benefits of switching your current pension solution to a new solution. A comparison was made between the features and charges.' (7) CIP vs alternative: Report text [P2] contrasts CIP with investment pathways and single multi-asset funds, explaining CIP advantages in monitoring, cost balance, and adaptability. (8) No evidence of whole-of-market provider search beyond the 4 platforms shown in research report; no evidence of comparison between Aviva and competitors on specific criteria (cost, service quality, fund range) to justify why Aviva was selected over Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon ARC, or Wealthtime.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts charges are 'competitive' [P4] but provides no comparative benchmarking evidence showing Aviva platform charges against alternative platforms' charges
      - Report asserts funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' [P4] but fund performance data [P10, P12] shows only peer performance within Defaqto comparator groups, not a specific comparison justifying selection of these two funds over alternatives within Aviva platform
      - Report states provider was selected for 'proven track record of excellent customer service' but no evidence document contains customer service metrics, ratings, or comparative service quality data
      - Research report [P1, P9] shows 4 platforms met filters but states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' [P1], meaning no quantitative feature benchmarking was performed to differentiate platforms on functionality or client-specific suitability features
      - Report explains why investment pathways were rejected [P2] but does not name or compare specific alternative multi-asset funds or alternative providers that were considered and rejected; only contrasts generic pathway concept against CIP
      - Aviva platform recommendation appears in both existing arrangements (Aviva SP50163762 with £3,744.36 [P13]) and as recommended solution (Switch to Aviva Platform [P14]), yet report does not explain why switching within same provider from existing Aviva arrangement to new Aviva platform arrangement is preferable to retaining or enhancing existing arrangement
    The report contains client-specific language linking recommendations to stated objectives (OBJ1-3) and references the client's desire for simplicity. However, the justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment exhibits material gaps: (1) PROVIDER SELECTION: The research shows 4 platforms were shortlisted (Aviva, Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime), but no quantitative DNA feature analysis was performed to compare them on criteria material to this client. The report asserts Aviva offers 'competitive and transparent charges' and 'proven track record of excellent customer service' in absolute terms [P4, P10] but provides no comparative evidence showing Aviva outperforms the 3 alternatives on cost, service, or fund range. This is an assertion without comparative justification. The guard 'A provider's marketing strengths stated in isolation are not a justification for selecting it over alternatives' applies here: the report names Aviva's strengths but does not distinguish these as absolute claims or comparative ones. The report should have stated either (a) 'we operate a panel of approved platforms, within which Aviva was selected because...', or (b) 'we searched the market and compared Aviva to [named alternatives] on [criteria], finding Aviva superior in [respects].' Instead, it asserts suitability in isolation. (2) FUND SELECTION: The report recommends two specific funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal 50%) but does not explain why these funds were selected over other Risk 5 solutions available on Aviva platform, nor why the 50/50 split is optimal for this client's objectives rather than alternative allocations. Fund performance data exists [P12] but is not cited in the rationale. The assertion 'funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' is not supported by a named peer group comparison or performance benchmark in the report text cited. (3) ALTERNATIVES: The report dismisses investment pathways by explaining they lack tailoring [P2], but does not name or compare specific multi-asset funds or alternative provider solutions. For example, no evidence shows whether Aegon's fund range or Wealthtime's charges were considered and rejected. The policy research [P13] lists existing plans (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life) with outcomes (Switch, Switch, Retain, Switch, Switch) but the rationale for consolidating into new Aviva platform rather than, for example, retaining People's Pension (which is noted as 'Active workplace pension') is not articulated. (4) WRAPPER: The report recommends a Personal Pension (SIPP) via Aviva Platform but does not compare this wrapper choice against alternatives or explain why a SIPP is preferable for this client's circumstances than a different wrapper, nor does it acknowledge the Aviva arrangement being 'switched from' [P14] is also via Aviva (same provider, different arrangement), creating a reconciliation gap. (5) COST ASSERTION: The report states 'The charges are competitive' [P4] but the computation analysis shows platform charge of 0.21%, AIM charge of 0.22%, fund costs totalling 0.37%, implying combined ongoing costs, yet no comparison to competing platforms' total cost of ownership is provided. The report's own internal inconsistencies (noted in extraction summary, e.g., charge comparisons with existing plans) undermine confidence in cost justification. (6) CLIENT-SPECIFIC LANGUAGE: Some rationale is genuinely client-specific ('You want to keep the investment strategy fairly simple' linked to stated desire [P4]) and linked to objectives. However, much is boilerplate repeated across potential recommendations (descriptions of platform benefits, multi-asset fund advantages). The CIP rationale [P2] contrasts CIP with alternatives but this comparison appears in product documentation, not prominently in the primary recommendation section. The distinction between what the report asserts about product suitability and what the evidence corroborates is material. The guard 'Firm panel or CIP membership is a legitimate justification if the report says so' would apply if the report had stated 'we operate a documented CIP investment committee process with regular reviews,' but the primary rationale given in the main recommendation section [P4] does not lead with this governance structure—it appears in secondary justification. Overall, the recommendation is supported by reference to client objectives and some client-specific needs, but the product, provider, and fund selection lack comparative justification demonstrating these particular choices outperform documented alternatives on criteria material to this client.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report's canonical model indicates 18 rationale statements across recommendations, 16 of which are marked isClientSpecific=true and 2 marked isClientSpecific=false. The suitability report [P13] states recommendations include switching Aviva pension to a new advised Aviva pension for £3,744.36. The report asserts personalised language and client-specific reasoning are present.

    Discrepancies:
      - Two recommendations marked isClientSpecific=false in the canonical model, but the specific recommendations they relate to are not identified, making it impossible to verify which advice lacks client-specific language.
      - The suitability report [P13] provides only a generic statement 'Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36' with no explanation of why this specific recommendation meets the client's objectives beyond consolidation.
      - Policy Research [P15] shows for Aviva the recommendation is 'Switch' but does not explain why switching within the same provider (Aviva) from the existing Aviva SP50163762 arrangement to a new Aviva Platform arrangement is preferable or why consolidation within Aviva rather than to an alternative is suitable.

      - No recommendation rationale in the evidence quotes or references the client's risk rating of 5, his retirement horizon of 2 years, or his income requirement of £1,700 net per month to justify fund selection or provider choice.

    The report asserts 16 out of 18 recommendations carry client-specific rationale (isClientSpecific=true), but the evidence available does not support this assertion to the degree required by the regulatory standard. The suitability report excerpt [P13] provides no narrative explanation beyond a single line listing the Aviva switch recommendation with no justification. The Policy Research working file [P14] and [P15] show internal summaries of advice strategy (consolidation, simplification, cashflow planning) but these are working notes, not the personalised explanations that should appear in the suitability report itself. The Policy Research does reference client objectives (consolidation, simplification, understanding) and identifies reasons some schemes are unsuitable (expensive, no adviser servicing, lifestyle), but these are summary bullet points not woven into a coherent, client-specific narrative linking each recommendation to the client's stated goals, risk profile, time horizon, or income needs. The fact that the extraction metadata flags 2 recommendations as not client-specific, combined with the absence of personalised language in the only suitability report excerpt provided [P13], suggests the report either does not contain the personalised reasoning it claims to contain, or the extracted recommendations do not align with what the report actually states. The evidence shows consolidation as a theme and simplification as a goal, but no specific recommendation is explained with reference to how it will achieve that goal better than alternatives, or how it meets the client's risk appetite, income requirement, or time horizon. The guard permitting standard regulatory wording does not excuse the absence of client-specific narrative linking recommendations to objectives. Where 2 of 18 rationale statements are marked not client-specific and the only excerpt of the report shows no personalised language, the consistency claim fails.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents six alternatives to the recommended solution: (1) Switch investments to Global Investments (up to 60% shares) fund — rejected because 'This fund has not performed as strongly as your current selection, but is less volatile and aligns with your risk appetite' [P6, page 6]; (2) Consolidate into People's Pension — rejected for two reasons: 'The funds do not meet your risk profile' and 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P6, page 6]; (3) Leave Scottish Widows plan and allow to grow until retirement — rejected because it 'Does not align with client preference for streamlined approach to managing pensions and maintaining five separate plans with different providers' [P6, page 6]; (4) Consolidate Scottish Widows into recommended Aviva platform — rejected because Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, and 'does not allow adviser management, which you would like to benefit from to ensure you are on the right track for retirement' [P6, page 6]; (5) Conduct internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because 'Your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options. Blended funds required have not performed as well as options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' [P6, page 7]; (6) Investment Pathway options — rejected because 'You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions offered by the provider' [P6].
    File:     The research documents provide evidence of alternative analysis: (P22) File notes state that Scottish Widows was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing'; Aviva alternative 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.. Discounted switch'; Zurich alternative 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5. They have no multi asset funds in his risk range either... The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch'; Standard Life 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' [P22]. (P2-P5) Pension switch report provides comparative analysis of existing pensions vs. new solution with critical yield, reduction in yield, and effect of switching calculations. (P6, P18) Suitability report states existing arrangement details and recommendation rationale. However, the report asserts cost comparison for People's Pension ('0.06% more expensive') but this specific charge figure is not verified in the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states People's Pension plan charge is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but no passage provides the specific charge comparison supporting this figure; the existing arrangements table [P6] shows People's Pension but no charge data for it is quoted in the evidence
      - The report rejects Consolidate Scottish Widows alternative citing 'Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with risk rating of 5' with the specific statement that this would require combining funds leaving '20-30% of your portfolio out of the market' [P6, P18], but the file notes [P22] state only 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility' without mentioning the specific fund structure problem
      - The report presents Alternative 1 (Switch investments to Global Investments fund) as rejected due to underperformance versus 'your current selection', but the specific performance comparison data is not provided in any passage
      - The Zurich internal fund switch rejection in the report [P6, page 7] cites that blended funds 'have not performed as well as options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' matching file notes [P22] which state 'The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally', but the specific performance metric comparison is not detailed in any passage
    The report presents alternatives with reasons for rejection, and most are corroborated by the file notes [P22]. Six distinct alternatives are named: in-situ switches (Global Investments, Zurich internal switch), do-nothing options (leave Scottish Widows, leave untouched), consolidation into alternative wrappers (People's Pension, Scottish Widows into Aviva), and generic investment pathways. The rejection rationales are client-specific, linking to identified objectives (consolidation, adviser management, risk alignment, growth potential). The primary corroboration comes from [P22] which confirms the research reasoning: Scottish Widows ruled out for 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva in-situ rejected because only risk-rated 4 fund available; Zurich rejected because funds 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally'; Standard Life discounted as more expensive. The pension switching report [P2-P5] provides technical comparison data with critical yield, reduction in yield, and switching impact for the main existing arrangements being consolidated. However, specific charge figures (e.g., People's Pension '0.06% more expensive') are asserted in the report but not quantified in the evidence. The Global Investments alternative is mentioned as rejected but no performance comparison document is retrieved. The Scottish Widows multi-asset fund structure argument [P18] is detailed in the report but [P22] provides only the higher-level 'no servicing' reason without the fund composition detail. These are not necessarily contradictions — the file notes may be abbreviated versions of fuller analysis — but the specific quantitative comparisons (charge differences, performance metrics) rest on analysis not visible in the retrieved passages. The Zurich comparison benefits from both report language and supporting file notes using virtually identical phrasing ('marginally' lower performance). The rejection reasons touch on consolidation objective, risk profile alignment, cost comparison, adviser management availability, and fund performance — all aligned to the client's stated objectives. The requirement is substantially met: alternatives are named, reasons are provided, and most are supported by research notes. The gaps are in specific quantitative comparisons not appearing in the retrieved set.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Five existing arrangements identified with stated outcomes: (1) People's Pension - Retain with rationale 'Missing out on employer contributions if transferred'; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash with rationale 'Funds do not meet risk profile' and cost comparison; (3) Aviva SP50163762 - Switch with rationale 'Lower Costs' (plan 0.06% more expensive), Lifestyling funds, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments; (4) Zurich P10026-719-001/DL - Switch with rationale 'Lower overall cost structure', Lifestyling funds, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, Multi-asset fund selection; (5) Standard Life D2301334000 - Switch with rationale 'Lower Costs' (plan 0.08% more expensive), Lifestyling funds, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments.
    File:     Policy research documents [P5] confirm five existing arrangements with current values and transfer values: People's Pension (£6,601.55), Scottish Widows (£211.32), Aviva (£3,744.36), Zurich (£103,439.24), Standard Life (£3,002.00). Policy illustrations [P1], [P4], [P6], [P7], [P9] and [P13] provide product details but do not contain explicit statements of recommended outcomes or client-specific rationales for each arrangement. The suitability report [P21], [P23] contains narrative recommendations but passages do not independently corroborate specific rationales for each arrangement with client-specific justification. Generic language appears in report rationales ('Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility') without client-specific linkage to objectives. Standard Life arrangement recommendation [P23] states 'plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but charge comparison on page 11 (referenced in extraction metadata) shows Standard Life 0.18% vs recommended 0.44%, which equals 0.26% MORE expensive (opposite of the stated rationale).
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life recommendation rationale asserts 'plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (indicating lower cost is benefit of switch) but arithmetic from extracted charge data shows Standard Life existing charge 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.26% increase, making recommended solution MORE expensive, not cheaper
      - Aviva recommendation rationale asserts 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (indicating cost saving benefit) but extracted comparison shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.06% saving only if those figures are compared; however Aviva's current value charge implies 0.21% platform charge plus 0.22% investment charge on recommended solution, creating ambiguity about which comparison is accurate
      - Rationales for Aviva and Zurich switches contain identical or near-identical generic language ('Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments') without client-specific differentiation between the two distinct arrangements and their distinct circumstances
      - Scottish Widows rationale states 'funds do not meet your risk profile' but Scottish Widows policy [P2] contains no detail about fund holdings or risk profile designation, preventing verification that this assertion applies client-specifically to this arrangement
      - Standard Life rationale references 'Lifestyling funds' but Standard Life policy illustration [P1] does not identify the fund as a lifestyling fund; Aviva policy [P7] explicitly identifies 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation' as having 'Lifestage Investment Approach' auto-switching, but Standard Life document does not confirm this feature
    Each existing arrangement carries a stated outcome and at least one rationale reason. Four of five arrangements (all except People's Pension) carry multiple reasons. However, material issues affect the reliability of the rationales: (1) The Standard Life recommendation is logically contradicted by the charge arithmetic. The report states lower costs as the benefit of switching, but the extracted figures show the recommended solution costs 0.26% more annually, not less. This is an internal contradiction within the report itself, not explained by any guard. (2) The rationales for Aviva and Zurich switches are substantially generic, using identical language across both arrangements ('Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments') without explaining why each distinct arrangement individually requires switching or how client circumstances differ between them. Generic language repeated across recommendations does not demonstrate client-specific rationale as required. (3) The Scottish Widows 'funds do not meet risk profile' rationale cannot be verified against the evidence—no fund breakdown is provided in the retrieved passages to confirm this assertion applies. (4) The Aviva cost rationale ('0.06% more expensive') contradicts the impression created by stating 'Lower Costs' as the opening rationale, and the exact comparison basis is ambiguous given the multiple charge components extracted. (5) The Standard Life lifestyling rationale cites automatic de-risking funds, but the Standard Life policy document [P1] does not confirm this arrangement uses lifestyling; the Aviva policy [P7] explicitly confirms its lifestyling feature, but the Standard Life documents retrieved do not. The requirement is technically met in structure (outcomes stated, rationales provided), but the rationales contain internal contradictions, generic repetition across distinct arrangements, and unverified assertions that undermine confidence in their client-specific basis.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states recommendations include multiple actions: Retain (one arrangement), Encash (one arrangement), and Switch (three arrangements). Implementation timescale is consistently stated as '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' across switching recommendations. Responsible party is identified as 'Adviser' for implementation. Two client-specific next steps are noted: (1) access to funds takes 10-15 working days, and (2) client should complete nomination of beneficiaries form after transfer process begins. The report specifies expected timescale at solution level as '10-15 working days'.
    File:     The case file contains Aviva Terms and Conditions (P1-P7), Key Features (P8), Fund Factsheet (P6), Scottish Widows Policy Info (P10), Standard Life Policy Info (P14), and planning scenarios (P9, P11-P12). The Aviva terms establish that: (1) adviser must provide at least 10 working days' notice before cancelling regular income payments (P1); (2) fund closure notifications allow 30 days advance notice unless external factors limit this (P3); (3) withdrawal instructions wait until the next Business Day and may not proceed on non-Business Days (P7); (4) for Structured Products, there may be penalties if withdrawn before fixed term end, with potentially significantly less returned than invested (P7). No document in the file specifies timescales for switches, transfers, or adviser implementation of the specific recommended actions. The Scottish Widows and Standard Life policy documents provide administrative details but no timescales for transfers or switches matching the report's claims.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states timescale for switching/encashing/administration is '10-15 working days' but no supporting document in the file corroborates this specific timescale for the recommended actions
      - Report assigns responsibility to 'Adviser' to implement switches but the file does not confirm the adviser will perform these actions or when they will occur
      - Report specifies '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes' but the Aviva terms show Business Day restrictions (P7) that could extend actual timescale beyond working days stated
      - Report recommends completing beneficiaries form 'after transfer process is begun' but does not specify who is responsible for providing the form or when exactly it will be provided
    The report makes implementation claims that cannot be fully corroborated by the evidence pack. The '10-10 working days' timescale for access to funds appears in the Aviva terms (P1, P7) as a general statement about withdrawal processes, but this is presented as a generic service standard, not a commitment specific to this client's recommended switches. The Aviva terms state withdrawal 'will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day' (P7), which introduces variability not reflected in the report's firm 10-15 working days statement. The report identifies 'Adviser' as responsible party but the evidence does not confirm the adviser will execute the switches or the timeline for doing so. The nomination of beneficiaries instruction is client-specific and properly attributed, but the report does not state who will provide the form (Aviva or adviser) or confirm timing beyond 'after transfer process is begun'. The responsibility matrix (what action, when, by whom) is partially addressed: action types are clear (Retain, Encash, Switch), timing is asserted but not corroborated, and responsibility ('Adviser') is stated but not evidenced. This creates a gap where the suitability assessment relies on timescale and responsibility claims that the file does not independently support.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report contains two ancillary recommendations: (1) a recommendation that the client 'complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided after the transfer process is begun' (stated as client-specific, page 16), with no owner specified; (2) a note about withdrawal timescales (10-15 working days) that is not a recommendation. The report also records in the canonical model that the client has a will (hasWill=true) but that it is not up to date (isWillUpToDate=false), and that no Power of Attorney is granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false).
    File:     The case file does not contain any evidence of a will or its status, whether up to date or otherwise. The file does not record whether the client has executed a Power of Attorney or not. The file does not record the outcome of any beneficiary nomination process. No document in the file states or evidences the client's testamentary arrangements or power of attorney status.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has a will that is not up to date, but no document in the file records this will or its status.
      - The report asserts no Power of Attorney is granted, but no document in the file records whether a Power of Attorney exists or does not exist.
      - The beneficiary nomination recommendation does not specify who is responsible for ensuring the form is completed or by when, though the report states the form 'will be provided' (passive voice, no actor named).
    The report's assertion about the will status and Power of Attorney cannot be corroborated by the case file. The fact find [P1-P8] does not contain estate planning sections that address will status or power of attorney. The suitability report appears to have recorded this information during fact-finding, but the extracted fact find document does not show these answers being captured or recorded. The beneficiary nomination recommendation is stated in the suitability report, is marked as client-specific, and is tied to the transfer process (a material implementation step). However, the recommendation does not specify who will provide the form, when it will be provided relative to the transfer, or who is responsible for ensuring completion. The guard permits a recommendation for the client to set up a Power of Attorney to be correct even if the file records neither its presence nor absence; similarly, an adviser's recommendation that beneficiary nomination be completed is not defective merely because the file does not record completion. However, the report asserts as fact (in the canonical model) that a will exists and is out of date, and that no Power of Attorney exists. These are assertions about client circumstances, not recommendations for action. They should be corroborated by the file but are not. The file silence on will and power of attorney status is an evidence gap that prevents verification of the report's factual claims about these matters.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report charge arithmetic: Platform (0.21% of £110,185) = £231.39; Investment Management (0.22% of £115,195.45) = £253.43; combined total 0.44% implies fund value of £110,184.09, yet no single arrangement value matches these implied fund values. The monetary charges £231.39 + £253.43 = £484.81, stated as 0.44% of fund value, implies fund value of £110,184.09, which does not correspond to any existing arrangement value or recommended total. Report comparison table [P3] shows Aviva existing annual charge 0.50% and recommended 0.44%, difference 0.06%, yet extracted data indicates this should represent a switching cost saving not explained in narrative rationale on pages 7-8 which state alternative cost characterisations. Fund charge weighted average: Report lists HSBC 0.18% and CT Universal MAP 0.29% as separate fund charges but does not state allocation percentages between them or provide a weighted combined figure to reconcile against the 0.44% total ongoing charge. The 0.44% total appears to aggregate platform (0.21%) + investment management (0.22%) only, excluding stated fund charges, but this is not explicitly clarified in the report structure. 27 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) Platform charge: Aviva Platform Personal Pension 0.21% (£231.39); (2) Investment Management charge: Aviva Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43); (3) Fund charges: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 0.29%; (4) Total ongoing charge (excluding fund charges): 0.44% of fund value pa; (5) Initial advice fee: 3% of amount invested, £3,305.55 with current transfer values; (6) Transaction costs explicitly excluded with stated reasons: 'charges quoted in this report exclude the transaction costs of the underlying funds, as these are variable and subject to change... these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges'; (7) Aggregated total and cumulative effect disclosed.
    File:     Evidence corroborates key cost disclosures: (1) Platform charge 0.21% confirmed in Aviva illustration [P8]; (2) Investment Management charge 0.22% stated in report [P3]; (3) Fund charges: HSBC 0.18% confirmed in KIID [P1] and Aviva illustration [P8]; CT Universal MAP Balanced C 0.29% confirmed in Aviva illustration [P8] and SIPP research report [P10]; (4) Total ongoing charge 0.44% shown in report comparison table [P3]; (5) Initial advice fee 3% stated in report [P17] matching client agreement language; (6) Transaction cost exclusion stated with reasoning [P3]; (7) Cumulative effect addressed in report statement: 'Your personal illustration contains further information regarding the cumulative effect the charges will have on your investment returns' [P3]; (8) Monetary charge estimates provided: £484.81 total (£231.39 platform + £253.43 investment management) [P3]; (9) Basis for monetary estimates explained: 'based on the amount being invested into the plan, plus one year of contributions' [P3].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report charge arithmetic: Platform (0.21% of £110,185) = £231.39; Investment Management (0.22% of £115,195.45) = £253.43; combined total 0.44% implies fund value of £110,184.09, yet no single arrangement value matches these implied fund values. The monetary charges £231.39 + £253.43 = £484.81, stated as 0.44% of fund value, implies fund value of £110,184.09, which does not correspond to any existing arrangement value or recommended total.
      - Report comparison table [P3] shows Aviva existing annual charge 0.50% and recommended 0.44%, difference 0.06%, yet extracted data indicates this should represent a switching cost saving not explained in narrative rationale on pages 7-8 which state alternative cost characterisations.
      - Fund charge weighted average: Report lists HSBC 0.18% and CT Universal MAP 0.29% as separate fund charges but does not state allocation percentages between them or provide a weighted combined figure to reconcile against the 0.44% total ongoing charge. The 0.44% total appears to aggregate platform (0.21%) + investment management (0.22%) only, excluding stated fund charges, but this is not explicitly clarified in the report structure.
      - Report excludes transaction costs with stated reasons, characterising them as 'variable' and 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges' [P3]. This framing—that transaction costs are embedded in performance—is an assertion about performance presentation that is not corroborated by any fund factsheet or illustration examined in the case file. KIIDs [P1, P2] and illustrations [P8] note that transaction costs are excluded from OCF but do not confirm the report's claim that they are embedded in gross performance figures.
    Comparison is performed. The report discloses all principal cost layers: platform, investment management, fund charges, advice fee, and explicitly excludes transaction costs with stated reasoning. The exclusion satisfies the guard rule: stated exclusion is a disclosure, not an omission. The monetary charges stated (£231.39 platform + £253.43 investment management = £484.81) are corroborated as present in the report and illustration [P3]. The 0.44% total ongoing charge (excluding fund charges) is consistent with the platform (0.21%) + investment management (0.22%) sum. However, three material consistency issues emerge: (1) The arithmetic of the monetary charges appears to rest on different implied fund values for platform charge (£110,185.71) versus investment management charge (£115,195.45), creating internal inconsistency within the report's own stated figures. The aggregate 0.44% at £484.81 implies a fund value of £110,184.09, none of which align with existing arrangement values (£116,998.47 table total, £110,000 narrative, or £110,185 transfer value stated). (2) Fund charges are disclosed in percentage form (0.18% HSBC, 0.29% CT MAP) [P3, P8] but no weighted average is provided to show how they combine, nor is allocation percentages between the two funds stated in the report passages examined. The 0.44% total is stated to exclude fund charges, yet reconciliation is impossible without this data. (3) The transaction cost exclusion is justified by asserting costs 'are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges' [P3]. This is not corroborated: KIIDs [P1, P2] state transaction costs are excluded from OCF figures but do not confirm they are embedded in published performance. The Aviva illustration [P8] and SIPP research [P10] disclose transaction cost components (e.g., SIPP research shows CT MAP transaction costs of 0.26% or £145.05 [P10]), establishing that transaction costs are separately quantifiable and not blanket-embedded. The report's assertion that they are 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures' lacks support. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability markers are recorded in expectedButAbsent for CHK-010 and no vulnerability flags identified in extracted risk assessment, so no adjustment is required on this ground. The ambiguity regarding employment income (ends at age 71 or continues) does not materially affect this costs and charges assessment. The risk rating ambiguity (initial 4, increased to 5) may affect suitability but does not affect disclosure completeness of charges.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial advice fee: 3% of amount invested, £3,305.55 as at 2025-10-14, paid by provider, for researching, recommending and arranging investments. Ongoing fee: 1% per annum of fund value, £1,101.85 for first year, paid via Aviva Platform, for reviewing attitude to risk and suitability, market updates, access to adviser team, and holistic financial planning.
    File:     Client Agreement [P12] states: 'For investments, we arrange and oversee on your behalf, there are two principal fees. A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews which we will do on your behalf.' Standard fees [P13] are '3% of the value of the funds that we advise on' for initial advice and '1%' per annum for ongoing review. IMPORT_2 [P3] confirms ongoing charge of 1% per annum 'for most investment funds'. Suitability report [P5] repeats: 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum... this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year... This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly.' Agreement [P12] authorises fees 'set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation' and states 'You will need to provide confirmation of your acceptance by approving them expressly or by written consent.' Agreement [P11] shows client signature dated 31/10/2025, after suitability report date of 2025-10-14.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states initial fee as £3,305.55 based on transfer value of £110,185.71 (implied: £3,305.55 ÷ 0.03 = £110,185.17). However, canonical model shows sum of five existing arrangements is £116,998.47, and report text on page 3 states 'combined value of approximately £110,000'. The basis for the £110,185.71 implied transfer value is not clearly stated in the evidence, though page 9 references 'transfer value £110,185'.
      - Report states ongoing fee will be '£1,101.85 for the first year' based on 1% of fund value. This implies fund value of £110,185. The report does not explicitly state whether this applies to the £110,185 transfer value or to a different figure at the time the fee is calculated.
      - The report states the ongoing fee 'will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly.' The client agreement [P12] states fees 'set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation' and that client must provide 'confirmation of your acceptance by approving them expressly or by written consent.' However, no explicit matching of the payment method stated in the report (via Aviva Platform) against the client agreement is evidenced in the passages.
    The report's fee percentages (3% initial, 1% ongoing) are corroborated by the Client Agreement [P12] and standard fee schedule [P13]. Both state these as standard charges for investments and pensions. The monetary initial fee of £3,305.55 is corroborated verbatim in [P12]. The monetary ongoing fee of £1,101.85 is corroborated verbatim in [P5]. The services described for the initial fee ('researching, recommending and arranging these investments') match [P5] and [P9]. The services described for the ongoing fee (reviewing attitude to risk and suitability, market updates, access to adviser team, holistic financial planning) match [P3] and [P5]. The Client Agreement [P12] was signed by the client on 31/10/2025, after the suitability report date of 2025-10-14, indicating acceptance of the terms. The payment method stated in the report—via Aviva Platform—is consistent with the client's stated preference noted in [P5]: 'you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly.' The guard permits lower charged rates than the agreement maximum; here the rates match exactly. The guard notes monetary fees move with transfer value; the report basis is stated as 'of amount invested' (£110,185 implied), which is a reasonable basis consistent with a transfer value. No material discrepancy exists between the fees stated in the report and those evidenced in the client agreement. The fee calculation appears arithmetically sound: £110,185 × 3% = £3,305.55 (exact); £110,185 × 1% = £1,101.85 (exact).

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts RIY percentages of 2.1%, 2.2%, and 2.3% for low, mid, and high growth scenarios respectively. Critical yields are stated as 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8% pa. Effects of switching are quantified as £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), and £15,000 (10.0%) decreases at age 75. The report states 'the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan' (page 11, 18). Page 19 shows platform charge of 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management charge of 0.22% (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81) annually.
    File:     [P1] presents a table showing Low growth: RIY 2.1%, Critical yield 1.9%, Effect of switching £10,400 (9.6%); Mid growth: RIY 2.2%, Critical yield 4.8%, Effect of switching £12,000 (9.4%); High growth: RIY 2.3%, Critical yield 7.8%, Effect of switching £15,000 (10.0%). The illustration [P3] shows fund values: at age 75, before charges £129,000, after product/investment charges £126,000, after all charges £115,000. [P10] projects at mid growth (5% before inflation, 2.9% after): £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new. [P18] states Aviva charge 0.21% and investment charges totalling 0.23% weighted average. [P19] confirms total charge 0.44% (£484.81). [P21] shows alternative comparison with different RIY and critical yield figures for individual pension switches.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's RIY figures (2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3%) are stated as percentages 'of fund growth pa' [P1] but the derived fund values imply these are reductions from nominal growth to real (inflation-adjusted) growth, not pure product/platform charges alone. The charge breakdown in [P19] totals 0.44% but RIY is stated as 2.1-2.3%, a gap of ~1.7 percentage points unexplained in the cumulative cost narrative.
      - The critical yield figures (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) appear in the report but the underlying transfer value against which these are computed is ambiguous. The extraction noted that existing arrangements total £116,998.47 but the report states 'approximately £110,000' (page 3) and the transfer value is £110,185 — a £6,813 variance that affects critical yield calculations.
      - The effect of switching amounts (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) cannot be reconciled to the charge structure shown. The annual charge of 0.44% on a fund value of £110,185 would yield ~£484.81 per year, but the switching costs shown are one-off transfer impacts over 6 years to age 75, suggesting cumulative charge impact — yet no breakdown is provided linking the charge rate to the cumulative effect figure.
      - [P10] illustration shows fund value projections but does not separately articulate the RIY percentage; it shows end values before and after charges but does not map to the RIY metric defined in [P1].
      - [P21] (pension switch report) shows different RIY figures for individual pension comparisons (e.g. Aviva only: 0.3% RIY, critical yield varies), contradicting the consolidated figures in the suitability report without cross-reference or explanation.
    The report's RIY figures are corroborated by the table in [P1], which explicitly states 2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% for the recommended solution. The critical yield figures (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) are also present in [P1] and supported by the narrative on page 11 ('4.8% per year up to age 75'). The effect of switching amounts (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) are explicitly stated in [P1] with matching percentages (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%). These three elements are internally consistent within the suitability report and are corroborated by the retrieved evidence.
    
    However, a material inconsistency exists in the cumulative cost narrative. The report discloses charges of 0.21% (platform) + 0.22% (investment management) = 0.44% total [P19], yet states RIY of 2.1-2.3%. The difference of ~1.7 percentage points is not explained. [P1] defines RIY as 'impact of product, platform and fund charges on the investment growth rate' but the illustration [P3] shows the effect of 'all charges' reducing growth from 2.9% (after inflation) to 0.8%, a reduction of 2.1 percentage points — which matches the reported RIY of 2.1% for the low growth scenario. This suggests the RIY incorporates not only the stated platform and fund charges (0.44%) but also an embedded advice charge or other unnamed cost layer.
    
    [P17] discloses an ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum. If this is included in the RIY calculation, the total cost layer would be ~1.44%, which still leaves ~0.7 percentage points unaccounted for in the 2.1-2.3% RIY range. The adviser charge is mentioned separately as 'included within' the projections but is not explicitly rolled into the charge table on page 19, creating ambiguity about whether the 0.44% figure is incomplete.
    
    The effect of switching amounts (£10,400 to £15,000) represent one-off transfer costs plus cumulative charge drag over 6 years. The report does not break down whether these are switching penalties, platform establishment costs, or accumulated charge differentials. Without this breakdown, the cumulative impact cannot be verified as arithmetically sound against the disclosed charge structure.
    
    The inconsistency does not contradict the stated RIY, critical yield, or effect of switching figures themselves — all are present and match the evidence. The issue is that the cumulative cost narrative, while present, does not fully explain the relationship between the disclosed charge rates (0.44%) and the impact on yield reduction (2.1-2.3%), and does not clearly isolate and quantify the adviser fee's contribution to the cumulative effect. This is a disclosure shortfall rather than a numerical error.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts on page 11 that 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' with a monetary difference of £4,672 in the first year. It provides a per-arrangement comparison showing: Aviva (EA3) with existing charge 0.50% (£18.72) and impact -0.06% (savings of £2.25); Zurich (EA4) with existing charge 0.93% (£961.98) and impact -0.49% (savings of £506.85); and Standard Life (EA5) with existing charge 0.52% (£186.19) and impact 0.26% (additional cost of £268.94). The report states the recommended Aviva plan charges 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management). On page 3, existing arrangements total 'approximately £110,000'; page 5 table shows individual values summing to £116,998.47. Page 9 shows transfer value £110,185. Page 19 states the plan 'would need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' and cites critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth rate of 2.94%.
    File:     Provider documentation (P1-P3) confirms individual existing charges: People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin; Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva 0.50% fund; Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin; Standard Life 0.52% fund. Page 3 (P3) comparison table shows Aviva 0.50% existing vs 0.44% recommended (0.06% saving), Zurich 0.93% existing vs 0.44% recommended (0.49% saving). The Zurich charge of 0.93% cannot be derived from disclosed documents: 0.18% fund + (£2.12 × 12 = £25.44 monthly) on £103,439.24 fund value = 0.18% + 0.024% = approximately 0.20%, not 0.93%. Transfer value arithmetic: P1 shows five arrangements totalling £116,998.47; narrative on page 3 states 'approximately £110,000'; P9 (transfer page) shows £110,185. The £4,672 difference between £116,998.47 and £110,185 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,188.68—matching neither existing total nor transfer value. Page 19 (P19) confirms the £4,672 outperformance statement and critical yield figure. Research (P18) confirms alternatives were discounted: Standard Life funds 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'; Zurich fund blend 'have not performed as well'; Aviva existing (SP50163762) on page 6 recommendation shows a 'Switch to Aviva Platform'—moving within the same provider, not to an alternative.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 4.24% overall cost difference and £4,672 first-year outperformance requirement. These figures imply a fund value of £110,188.68, which matches neither the existing arrangements sum (£116,998.47) nor the stated transfer value (£110,185); the base figure on which this percentage and amount rest is internally inconsistent across the report.
      - Report states Zurich (EA4) existing annual charge of 0.93%. Provider documentation (P2, P15) shows Zurich charges 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12 monthly admin charge (£25.44 p.a.), totalling approximately 0.20% on a £103,439.24 fund value, not 0.93%. The 0.93% figure is arithmetically unsupported by the provider documentation.
      - Report shows EA5 (Standard Life) impact computation of 0.26% additional cost (£268.94) at 0.52% existing charge. The arithmetic: £268.94 at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's current value (£103,439.24), not Standard Life's (£3,002). The row is computed on the wrong plan's value, making the comparison for Standard Life unreliable.
      - Report narrative on page 3 states existing arrangements total 'approximately £110,000', but page 5 table of existing arrangements sums to £116,998.47 and page 9 transfer value is £110,185. Three inconsistent totals are given for the same arrangements at the same date.
      - Report page 6 recommendation shows 'Aviva - SP50163762' described as 'Switch to Aviva Platform', and existing page 5 also shows 'Aviva - SP50163762' with fund value £3,744.36. This is a switch within Aviva (same arrangement code), not a switch to an alternative provider. The comparison rationale does not clearly articulate why consolidating within Aviva is preferable to alternatives; the 4.24% cost difference asserted may not reflect a true like-for-like alternative comparison.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Correct consolidated base value on which 4.24% cost difference is calculated: report uses three different totals (approximately £110,000 / £110,185 / £116,998.47) inconsistently., Verified Zurich existing annual charge as percentage: provider documentation discloses 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin, but comparison table asserts 0.93%; arithmetic supporting 0.93% is absent from evidence., Correct Standard Life (EA5) impact computation: current row is computed against Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's fund value (£3,002), rendering the individual arrangement comparison unreliable., Articulation of why recommendation (consolidation within Aviva) constitutes a suitable alternative to alternatives considered, given that the existing Aviva arrangement (SP50163762) is being switched to a new Aviva arrangement, not replaced by a different provider., Adviser fees breakdown: report mentions 3% initial advice fee (page 23, P23) of £3,305.55 'with current transfer values', but cost comparison basis ('IncludingAdviceFees') is not explicitly reconciled to show how this fee is incorporated into the 4.24% and £4,672 figures.
    A cost comparison has not been reliably performed. The report asserts specific cost differences (4.24% / £4,672) but the foundation—the base fund value on which these figures rest—is contradicted within the report itself. Three separate totals are stated: narrative 'approximately £110,000', page 5 table £116,998.47, and page 9 transfer value £110,185. Reverse-engineering the 4.24% and £4,672 yields £110,188.68, aligning only approximately with the transfer value, which itself differs from both the narrative claim and the itemised sum. This inconsistency cascades: if the base value is uncertain, the percentage impact is unreliable. Second, the Zurich charge figure of 0.93% cannot be corroborated. Provider documentation discloses 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12 monthly admin on a £103,439.24 fund value, yielding approximately 0.20% total, not 0.93%. The source of 0.93% is not in evidence; without it, the EA4 comparison (savings of 0.49%) cannot be verified. Third, the Standard Life (EA5) row shows an impact computation (0.26% / £268.94) that, when reverse-engineered, implies a fund value of £103,438.46—which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002. The extraction notes flag this: 'EA5 impact: £268.94 at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's current value...the row is computed on the wrong plan and any comparison resting on it is unreliable.' This means the per-arrangement comparison table contains at least one row computed on the wrong plan's value. Fourth, the recommendation itself is not a switch to an alternative; it is a consolidation within Aviva. The existing arrangement (SP50163762, £3,744.36) is moved to 'Aviva Platform', staying within Aviva. This is neither a comparison with an external alternative nor a clear articulation of why staying with Aviva in a new structure is preferable to external alternatives (Standard Life, Zurich, or retaining existing arrangements) beyond cost. The critical yield analysis (P21) shows that at mid-growth of 2.95%, the new solution underperforms the existing by £12,000 (9.4% decrease) at age 75, yet the recommendation proceeds on the basis that the plan 'would need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year'. No reconciliation is made between the near-term outperformance hurdle (£4,672) and the long-term projected underperformance (£12,000 cumulative loss by age 75). Benefits claimed for the higher-cost recommendation (page 22, P22) cite Aviva's 'financial strength', 'choice' of funds, and 'value' with 'competitive charges', but these do not specifically address why the client should accept the 4.24% cost premium or why the consolidation benefit justifies the documented long-term performance drag. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability flags were recorded in the case (extraction ambiguities note absence of `/riskAssessment/vulnerabilityFlags`), so this check does not raise additional concerns on that dimension. However, the client's knowledge and experience (page 4) state 'you have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself', yet a Risk 5 Balanced portfolio is recommended with noted capacity-for-loss language referring to pension funds 'form[ing] the basis of retirement provision', later contradicted by language stating the client is 'not reliant upon pension funds'. This internal contradiction in the suitability rationale compounds the unreliability of the cost justification tied to it.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' (page 6). It justifies higher costs via a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75, stating 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' (page 11). The report lists eight benefits: Lower Costs, Lifestyling removal, Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring, Multi-asset funds, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, and Financial strength. Page 2 of the pension switch report shows critical yield of 0.3% for mid-growth (2.94%), with existing pension projected value £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (£2,000 or 1.9% decrease).
    File:     The evidence shows contradictory cost comparisons. Page 11 comparison table shows Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended charge 0.44%, a saving of 0.06%. However, page 11 also shows Standard Life existing charge 0.18% vs recommended 0.44%, making recommended 0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life. The pension switch report (P14) shows the new solution underperforms: at mid-growth rate 2.95%, projected value existing £127,000 vs new £125,000 (reduction of £2,000). At high growth 5.88%, existing produces £150,000 vs new £147,000 (reduction of £3,000). Critical yield required is 0.3% at mid-growth and 3.2% at higher growth, not the 4.8% stated in the suitability report. Performance data (P1, P2) shows recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio returned 15.58% over 5 years (13/10/2020-13/10/2025), underperforming Zurich's 44.32% and Standard Life's 45.62%. Page 2 of P1 explicitly states 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims lower costs but comparison table shows recommended charge 0.44% vs Aviva existing 0.50% (0.06% saving) and vs Standard Life existing 0.18% (0.26% more expensive)
      - Report states critical yield of 4.8% per year needed but pension switch report (P14) states critical yield 0.3% at mid-growth (2.94%) and 3.2% at higher growth (5.88%)
      - Report claims past performance supports 4.8% is achievable but evidence (P1, P2) shows recommended funds 15.58% over 5 years underperformed Zurich 44.32% and Standard Life 45.62%
      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' benefit but pension switch report shows new solution produces lower projected values: £125,000 vs £127,000 at mid-growth (£2,000 reduction) and £147,000 vs £150,000 at high growth (£3,000 reduction)
      - Report states recommended funds demonstrated 'stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' is true, but evidence states they 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' — critical omission given Zurich holds £103,439.24 (88.6% of total)
    The comparison can be performed with the evidence provided. Three critical issues emerge: (1) The cost justification is arithmetically unsound. The report claims a 4.8% critical yield is needed, but the pension switch report (P14) clearly shows 0.3% at mid-growth 2.94% and 3.2% at higher growth 5.88%. These are derived from the same data but the suitability report's 4.8% figure is not corroborated anywhere in the evidence and contradicts the official switching analysis. (2) The 'Lower Costs' assertion is partially false. While the Aviva-to-Aviva move saves 0.06% (0.50% to 0.44%), switching Standard Life (0.18%) to the recommended solution (0.44%) costs 0.26% more. Since Standard Life represents £3,002 and Aviva represents £3,744, consolidation into Aviva Platform increases overall charges for that tranche. (3) Performance justification fails the guard. The report states past performance supports the critical yield 'is achievable', but the evidence (P1, P2) directly contradicts this: recommended Aviva Risk 5 returned 15.58% vs Zurich 44.32% and Standard Life 45.62%. The report acknowledges on page 2 of P1 that the recommended funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' — yet uses their past performance to justify a recommendation that underperforms those very funds. The pension switch report (P14) is explicit: the new solution reduces projected value by £2,000-£3,000 depending on growth assumptions. The benefits stated (Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation) are generic and not quantified; no evidence connects these service benefits to the £2,000-£3,000 projected loss. The report does not disclose that the recommendation reduces projected retirement value or explain how generic service benefits justify that cost.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states on page 11 that 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' and asserts this as both the required outperformance percentage and the cost difference basis. The report's canonical model extracts this figure as a stated assertion that the recommended Aviva solution costs 4.24% more per annum including adviser fees than the existing arrangements.
    File:     The case file contains no independent supporting document that corroborates or contradicts the 4.24% figure. This requirement is assessed from the report alone, as it compares the report's own cost assertions against stated benefits. The suitability report itself is the primary and only document available for this comparison.
    Discrepancies:
      - The 4.24% cost difference figure is stated on page 11, but the arithmetic underlying this calculation is unreliable. The derived figures computed from the canonical model show that the 'required outperformance' of 4.24% at that percentage implies a fund value of £110,188.68. This does not match any individual arrangement value (which range from £211.32 to £103,439.24), nor the total current value of £116,998.47, nor the transfer value of £110,185. The calculation basis is therefore unclear and not reconcilable to a specific arrangement or portfolio total.
      - The charge comparison in the canonical model references EA5 (Standard Life, current value £3,002.00) but the arithmetic used (£268.94 at 0.26%) implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is the Zurich arrangement, not Standard Life. This indicates the comparison row is computed on the wrong plan.
      - Page 11 states the recommendation is 4.24% more expensive, yet the report does not clearly disclose what specific benefits are expected to offset this additional cost. Page 6-7 discusses consolidation, simplified administration and a Balanced risk profile, but does not quantify or justify why these benefits support paying 4.24% higher ongoing charges.
      - The extraction metadata notes that page 7 states for Aviva 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying the existing Aviva is more expensive), but page 11 shows Aviva existing at 0.50% versus recommended at 0.44%, a saving of 0.06%, not an increase. This internal contradiction means the narrative rationale for switching the Aviva arrangement is inconsistent with the charge comparison presented.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The fund value base to which the 4.24% cost difference applies is not clearly stated. The derived figures show the 4.24% calculation implies £110,188.68, but this matches neither the individual arrangement values nor the stated totals (£116,998.47 existing, £110,185 transfer value). Without knowing whether the comparison applies to total portfolio, a subset of arrangements, or a projected future value, the reasonableness of 4.24% cannot be assessed., The specific benefits expected to offset the 4.24% higher cost are not quantified. The report mentions consolidation, simplified administration, and a 'Balanced' growth profile, but provides no evidence, projections or comparison showing that these benefits justify the cost increase., The cumulative all-in cost impact across all five existing arrangements versus the recommended solution is not shown. The report presents individual arrangement charges but not a clear aggregated before-and-after comparison that would allow the 4.24% figure to be validated against the sum of existing charges., The charge arithmetic for the required outperformance calculation is not disclosed. The underlying derivation of the 4.24% figure from the plan values and stated charges cannot be traced or verified from the report.
    The report asserts that the recommended Aviva solution is 4.24% more expensive per annum than current plans and must therefore deliver outperformance to justify the cost. However, the comparison cannot be performed reliably. First, the fund value base for the 4.24% calculation is ambiguous: derived arithmetic suggests £110,188.68, which does not reconcile to any stated total (existing £116,998.47, transfer value £110,185, or individual arrangements). Second, the charge comparison row for Standard Life (EA5) is computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46), not Standard Life's value (£3,002.00), which makes that row's output unreliable. Third, the report does not clearly articulate what benefits justify the cost increase — it mentions consolidation and administrative simplification, but these are not quantified or compared against the 4.24% charge premium. Fourth, there is an internal inconsistency: page 7 suggests the existing Aviva arrangement is more expensive than the recommendation, while page 11's charge table shows the existing Aviva at 0.50% and recommended at 0.44% (a saving of 0.06%), which contradicts the narrative rationale for switching. The report therefore fails to establish that the stated cost increase of 4.24% is justified by proportionate benefits. Where the calculation basis is unclear, the fund value denominator is ambiguous, and the benefit case is unquantified, the assertion that costs are not excessive cannot be verified. The guard permits higher costs where benefits are stated, but here the benefits are neither quantified nor evidenced against the cost increase claimed.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommended solution (Aviva Platform Personal Pension with total charge of 0.44%, comprising platform charge 0.21% and investment management charge 0.22%) provides lower costs compared to existing arrangements. For Aviva existing plan, the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 7). For Standard Life, it states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8). The report does not assert specific comparative cost analysis for alternatives considered and rejected (Scottish Widows, alternative Aviva fund, alternative Zurich arrangement).
    File:     The pension switch report [P2] and [P5] show charge comparisons: Aviva existing 0.59% vs new solution 0.6%, Zurich existing 0.29% vs new solution 0.6%, Standard Life existing 0.59% vs new solution 0.6%. A detailed comparison table [P8] shows Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (0.06% saving). File notes [P18] document alternative options considered: Scottish Widows (discounted due to no servicing and reduced flexibility), alternative Aviva fund (only risk rated 4 available, does not meet ATR), Zurich blend (fund charges more expensive than Aviva, no multi-asset funds in risk range), and Standard Life (all funds within 20-60% equities range more expensive than Aviva). The pension switch report contains comparative projections at different growth rates showing the effect of switching.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report page 7 states Aviva existing is '0.06% more expensive' but page 11 comparison table [P8] shows Aviva existing 0.50% and recommended 0.44%, which equals 0.06% saving (report correctly states the saving but uses confusing language saying existing is 'more expensive')

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: No explicit cost comparison figures provided for Scottish Widows alternative rejected on non-cost grounds, No explicit cost comparison figures provided for Zurich internal switch alternative (blend of risk 7 and 2 funds), No explicit cost comparison figures provided for alternative Aviva fund (risk 4) rejected as not meeting ATR, Cumulative impact of charges on projected values not clearly reconciled between suitability report and pension switch report figures
    The report asserts lower costs support the recommendation, but the evidence reveals material inconsistencies. First, the charge figure discrepancy: the suitability report [P8] quotes new solution as 0.44% total but the pension switch report [P2] and [P5] quote it as 0.6%. Second, the Standard Life comparison is arithmetically contradicted: the report claims it is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the comparison table shows Standard Life at 0.18% and recommended at 0.44%, making the recommended solution 0.26% more expensive. Third, the report states alternatives were rejected on non-cost grounds (fund range, access, servicing, flexibility) but provides no cost figures for these alternatives, so the claim that they lack better cost or benefit cannot be fully supported. The guard permits rejection of cheaper alternatives for evidenced non-cost reasons, and the file notes [P18] do document such reasons, but the absence of explicit cost comparisons for Scottish Widows, Zurich blend, and alternative Aviva options means the assertion that the recommended solution provides 'better cost or benefit' cannot be fully verified. The Zurich comparison in the pension switch report shows a cost increase (0.29% to 0.6%) alongside claimed non-cost benefits (greater investment choice, flexibility), which is consistent with the guard. However, the internal inconsistency on Standard Life charges undermines confidence in the cost comparison methodology.
