  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1% per annum. The services included for this charge are: (1) Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The report states 'Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year. This will vary in line with your fund value. This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly.' The report does not specify a review frequency for the ongoing service.
    File:     The client agreement [P1] states 'ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' [P8] describes the ongoing service: 'TFAS Wealth Limited aims to revisit and review your plans at least annually. The service includes: Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of this service; bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk. Statements and Performance Reports twice per year. Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial. Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children. Regular market updates. Quarterly e-magazine. On-line access for portfolio valuations. Access to your adviser should you need us in the meantime.' [P8] also confirms the charge: 'For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%'. The client agreement [P1] also notes 'The fee(s) will become payable when you agree upon advice that has been given and will be settled by from your pension/investment.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report describes five ongoing services but does not specify review frequency; the client agreement specifies 'at least annually' for review meetings and 'twice per year' for statements.
      - The suitability report lists 'Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs' as a service included in the 1% charge; the client agreement [P8] includes this as part of the service description but also mentions 'Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children', which is not mentioned in the suitability report.
      - The suitability report does not mention that statements and performance reports are provided twice per year, which is stated in [P8].
      - The suitability report does not mention quarterly e-magazine or online access for portfolio valuations as part of the 1% ongoing service, both of which are listed in [P8].
    The comparison can be performed because both the suitability report and the client agreement address the ongoing service proposition and the 1% charge. The core charge of 1% per annum is consistently stated in both documents [P1, P8, P15]. However, the suitability report provides an incomplete disclosure of services included in the 1% ongoing charge. The client agreement [P8] enumerates a comprehensive list of services: review meetings at least annually, statements and performance reports twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit (fee-free advice for direct family members), regular market updates, quarterly e-magazine, online access for portfolio valuations, and ad-hoc adviser access. The suitability report [P15] lists only five services: (1) reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss, (2) reviewing ongoing suitability of portfolio, (3) market updates and legislation updates, (4) ad-hoc advice access, and (5) holistic financial planning. The suitability report omits material components of the service delivery that the client agreement confirms are included in the 1% fee: statements and performance reports twice per year, quarterly e-magazine, online portfolio access, and the family benefit provision. The report also fails to specify the review frequency ('at least annually'), whereas the client agreement clearly states this. These are not contradictions that require a guard to explain — they are disclosure shortfalls. The report does not describe the full value proposition that supports the 1% charge. The services are not contradicted, but they are incompletely disclosed in the suitability report. Per the guard 'The client agreement and the report may name the same service level differently; compare what is included, not the label' — this applies only where the same service is named differently; here, services are present in the agreement but absent from the report's description of what the client receives.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states eight benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs - existing pensions have higher cost structure; (2) Lifestyling removal - existing funds drift outside risk range; (3) Greater Investment Choice - wider range enabling tailored strategy; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation - charges deducted directly for ongoing support; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments - adviser will actively monitor; (6) Multi-asset fund selection - Aviva offers risk 5 funds whereas Zurich requires blend of risk 7 and risk 2; (7) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility - more flexible retirement options; (8) Financial strength - Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM. The report asserts at page 11 that the recommended plan would need to grow 4.8% per year to match the existing plan value, and claims this is achievable based on past fund performance. The report also states the recommended Aviva funds have demonstrated stronger performance and reduced volatility compared to existing holdings.
    File:     Evidence from the case file shows: (1) Cost comparison at page 11 shows Zurich existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (0.06% saving), but for Standard Life shows existing 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% (0.26% MORE expensive, not a saving); (2) Zurich policy info confirms Zurich offers 60+ funds with full and partial UFPLS and FAD available, contradicting the claim that Zurich lacks risk 5 offerings - the research report shows Zurich's fund range includes risk-rated options; (3) Aviva policy info confirms enhanced retirement flexibility options (FAD, UFPLS, annuity) are already available on existing Aviva plan SP50163762, so this is not a new benefit; (4) The multi-asset benefit claim references Zurich not offering risk 5, but the recommendation itself involves switching from one Aviva arrangement (SP50163762) to another Aviva arrangement (Aviva Platform), meaning the existing Aviva already has access to the same Aviva funds and platform capabilities; (5) Adviser charging facilitation and ongoing monitoring describe the ongoing service proposition, not financial benefits; (6) Financial strength is a non-client-specific provider stability assertion; (7) The critical yield of 4.8% relies on fund values that do not reconcile - derived figures show platform charge at 0.21% implies £110,185.71 fund value, investment management charge at 0.22% implies £115,195.45, and total ongoing at 0.44% implies £110,184.09, creating arithmetic inconsistency; (8) Performance comparison claims stronger Aviva fund performance but provides no supporting evidence from research reports or performance data.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' but page 11 comparison shows Standard Life existing charge 0.18% versus recommended 0.44%, meaning recommended is 0.26% MORE expensive, not cheaper
      - Report states Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds requiring blend of risk 7 and risk 2, but Zurich policy information shows 60+ funds available and research report indicates risk-rated fund options exist
      - Report claims Enhanced Retirement Flexibility as a benefit of the new scheme, but existing Aviva plan SP50163762 already provides FAD, Full and partial UFPLS, and annuity options per Aviva policy info - this benefit already exists on the ceding plan
      - Recommendation involves switching existing Aviva SP50163762 to Aviva Platform - both are Aviva arrangements, so the Greater Investment Choice and Multi-asset fund benefits are not unique to the new arrangement; existing Aviva already has access to Aviva's fund range
      - Critical yield calculation of 4.8% per annum relies on transfer value of £110,185 but derived charge arithmetic shows this figure does not reconcile with stated charges: platform charge 0.21% on £110,185.71, investment management 0.22% on £115,195.45, and total 0.44% on £110,184.09 create inconsistent denominators
      - Report claims recommended Aviva funds have demonstrated stronger performance and reduced volatility compared to existing holdings but provides no citations to supporting performance data or comparative analysis in the evidence pack
    The requirement is to assess whether benefits relied upon to justify higher costs are supported by and consistent with evidence. The comparison can be performed because the report states benefits explicitly and cost comparisons are available. However, multiple material discrepancies emerge: (1) The primary financial benefit claimed is 'Lower Costs' but the comparison table contradicts this for the Standard Life arrangement (0.26% MORE expensive). For Aviva and Zurich the savings are modest (0.06%) and do not appear to justify the higher critical yield of 4.8% against a baseline of 2.94% mid-growth rate. (2) The Zurich multi-asset fund benefit is unsupported; evidence shows Zurich offers 60+ funds and the research report does not confirm absence of risk 5 options. The claim that Zurich requires a blend reducing diversification is stated but not evidenced. (3) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility is contradicted by existing Aviva policy information showing the ceding plan already provides FAD, UFPLS, and annuity options - applying the false-positive guard that a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify extra cost, this benefit fails. (4) The recommendation switches from Aviva SP50163762 to Aviva Platform - both within Aviva - so Greater Investment Choice and Multi-asset selection are not unique to the recommendation; the existing Aviva arrangement already provides access to Aviva's investment options. (5) Adviser Charging Facilitation and Ongoing Monitoring describe service features, not financial benefits justifying cost. (6) The critical yield of 4.8% cannot be validated because the derived figures show the transfer value and charge denominators are arithmetically inconsistent across the stated charges. (7) Fund performance claims lack supporting evidence; no performance comparisons, fact sheets, or KIIDs comparative data are cited. Per the requirement's severity guidance, the high-level issue is that cost increase to Standard Life existing is unexplained, and the critical yield required is stated without arithmetically sound cost foundation.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' (page 11). It states the recommended solution requires 4.24% per annum outperformance (including adviser fees) to match existing plan value. It projects outcomes at three growth rates: Low (0.0%) = £97,600; Mid (2.95%) = £115,000; High (5.88%) = £135,000, all at age 75. Reduction in Yield figures are 2.1%, 2.2%, and 2.3% respectively. Critical Yield requirements are 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8% respectively. The report states 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' but provides no detailed performance comparison or analysis of benefits that justify the higher cost.
    File:     The pension switch report [P10] shows a comparison table where at mid-growth (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 while the new solution projects to £115,000—a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. At low growth (0%), existing = £108,000 vs new solution = £97,600 (decrease of £10,400 or 9.6%). At high growth (5.88%), existing = £150,000 vs new solution = £135,000 (decrease of £15,000 or 10%). The report also states 'Effect of switching' showing material reductions in projected value across all growth scenarios. [P1] shows 'Existing pension: £110,185.60' vs 'New solution day one: £106,880.60' with a 'shortfall in the death benefit immediately after the switch of £3,305.00'. The switch report does not corroborate a 4.24% charge differential. [P3] and related passages show Critical Yield requirements of 0.3%, 3.2%, and 6.2% for alternative comparisons (Aviva only, Standard Life only), not the 1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8% stated in the main recommendation. The fund performance data [P13] and [P14] show combined discrete performance of 12.02% (2021), -9.83% (2022), 10.68% (2023), 11.05% (2024), YTD 7.87%, but these do not isolate whether the recommended funds will achieve the required 4.8% or 7.8% outperformance to offset the stated cost differential.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 4.24% cost differential; pension switch report shows projected value reductions of 9.4–10% under all growth scenarios, inconsistent with a 4.24% charge-driven outperformance requirement.
      - Report projects Low growth outcome of £97,600; switch report [P10] shows existing pension at low growth (0%) = £108,000, a £10,400 shortfall in the recommended solution.
      - Report projects Mid growth outcome of £115,000; switch report [P10] shows existing pension at mid-growth (2.95%) = £127,000, a £12,000 shortfall.
      - Report projects High growth outcome of £135,000; switch report [P10] shows existing pension at high growth (5.88%) = £150,000, a £15,000 shortfall.
      - Report states Critical Yield of 4.8% required at mid-growth (2.95%); this is substantially higher than the existing arrangement's Reduction in Yield of 0.3%, creating a 4.5 percentage point incremental requirement not clearly explained by the 4.24% charge differential alone.
      - Report does not disclose that switching results in an immediate £3,305 death benefit shortfall [P1], which is material to the overall value proposition.
      - Report cites past fund performance to support achievability of 4.8% outperformance but does not quantify the specific historical performance of the recommended fund combination (CT Universal MAP Balanced + HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) net of the stated charges to demonstrate the differential is recoverable.
    The report asserts a 4.24% annual cost differential and requires the recommended plan to grow at 4.8% (mid-growth scenario) to match the existing plan's projected value of £127,000. However, the evidence in the pension switch report [P10] directly contradicts this: at the same mid-growth rate (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 while the recommended solution projects to £115,000, an absolute shortfall of £12,000 or 9.4%. This is not a reflection of a 4.24% outperformance requirement—it is a lower absolute outcome. The same pattern holds at low growth (9.6% shortfall) and high growth (10% shortfall). The switch report table [P10] explicitly labels these as 'Effect of switching' with negative arrows, confirming the recommendation produces lower projected maturity values, not higher. The report's statement that the funds' 'past performance' supports achievability of the 4.8% outperformance is unsupported: while [P13] and [P14] show the funds have delivered returns of ~11% annually in recent years, these gross returns do not isolate net-of-charges performance or demonstrate that the specific funds selected, after the 4.24% cost differential, will achieve the required 4.8% net outperformance. Additionally, the report does not address the immediate impact: [P1] shows a £3,305 death benefit shortfall on day one post-switch, which is material context for evaluating value. The report's disclosure of charges is incomplete—it asserts the cost differential but does not compare the all-in ongoing charges (0.6% platform + product + fund + 1% adviser fee = 1.6% stated total) against the existing arrangements' charges (0.3% to 0.59% disclosed in the switch report), nor does it reconcile the £4,672 annual requirement stated with the 4.24% differential on a £110,185 base (which would be £4,673, arithmetically consistent but not compared to the alternative scenarios in [P3], [P5], [P6] showing different critical yields of 0.3%, 3.2%, 6.2%, 6.0%, etc.). The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerabilities are recorded in the evidence (expectedButAbsent includes vulnerabilityFlags), so this dimension does not materially affect the assessment. However, the report's assertion that higher costs are justified by 'past performance' without quantifying the net outperformance needed or achievable is a weak justification that contradicts the switch report's explicit projections showing lower outcomes across all scenarios.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report asserts the Aviva plan is '4.24% more expensive than your current plans' requiring '£4,672 in the first year (in monetary terms)' outperformance, but the underlying charge arithmetic from the canonical model shows this comparison contains errors: EA5 existing annual charge is computed on the wrong plan (Standard Life at 0.52% rather than its actual position), and the £4,672 figure is derived from a fund value (£110,188.68 at 4.24%) that matches none of the actual arrangement values or totals, creating unreliability in the material disadvantage figure presented to the client. The report states that the projected 'combined value of approximately £110,000' [page 3], but the Pension Switch Report tables [P1, P2] show individual arrangement values summing to £116,998.47, a difference of £6,998.47, yet the comparison of losses uses a £110,185 transfer value figure—creating ambiguity about which baseline the loss scenarios are calculated against. The report presents as a disadvantage that 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' but the Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] shows quantified loss scenarios (Effect of switching £10 or 0.3% decrease) that are material disadvantages not explicitly stated as monetary loss amounts in the main report disadvantages section—only referenced obliquely in the comparative analysis. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states six material disadvantages: (1) 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds'; (2) 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider'; (3) 'Including Adviser Fee, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans. This means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year (in monetary terms)'; (4) 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds'; (5) 'Your Zurich investments are allocated to higher-risk funds, which are more susceptible to market volatility, resulting in greater fluctuations in value'; (6) 'The chart does not consider any potential long-term care costs which could occur in later life'. The report also identifies limitations including: restricted pension withdrawal flexibility; Scottish Widows fund range limitations requiring cash holdings that leave 20-30% out of market; and generic stakeholder pension limitations. Investment risks are stated including that 'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee of return of original capital'. Market timing risks are disclosed. The adviser fee is stated as 3% (£3,305.55) with explicit disclosure that this 'may fluctuate depending on transfer values' and will be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer'.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] provides quantified loss scenarios showing 'Effect of switching' for the Aviva arrangement at multiple growth rates, with specific figures like '£10 or 0.3% decrease' and '£10 or 0.2% decrease' under different scenarios. The report shows Critical Yield calculations. The Key Features document [P4, P7, P13] discloses generic investment risks stating 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested.' It notes risks of foreign exchange exposure, property investment illiquidity, and fixed-term product access restrictions. Income drawdown risks are detailed including that 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income.' The Terms and Conditions [P6, P9] disclose switching mechanics: 'There are no additional Aviva charges levied for switching. There may be transactional costs where applicable' and note that 'where You are disinvested for a period and subject to potentially adverse market movements, We will not be liable for any losses arising during this period.' The Suitability Report itself [P15, P20] states tax treatment is 'based on our understanding of current tax law and HMRC practice' and notes 'Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change.' Product literature is referenced as containing 'information regarding the products which I have recommended, particularly with regards to how the product works, its aims, risks and charges'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the Aviva plan is '4.24% more expensive than your current plans' requiring '£4,672 in the first year (in monetary terms)' outperformance, but the underlying charge arithmetic from the canonical model shows this comparison contains errors: EA5 existing annual charge is computed on the wrong plan (Standard Life at 0.52% rather than its actual position), and the £4,672 figure is derived from a fund value (£110,188.68 at 4.24%) that matches none of the actual arrangement values or totals, creating unreliability in the material disadvantage figure presented to the client.
      - The report states that the projected 'combined value of approximately £110,000' [page 3], but the Pension Switch Report tables [P1, P2] show individual arrangement values summing to £116,998.47, a difference of £6,998.47, yet the comparison of losses uses a £110,185 transfer value figure—creating ambiguity about which baseline the loss scenarios are calculated against.
      - The report presents as a disadvantage that 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' but the Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] shows quantified loss scenarios (Effect of switching £10 or 0.3% decrease) that are material disadvantages not explicitly stated as monetary loss amounts in the main report disadvantages section—only referenced obliquely in the comparative analysis.
      - The report states risk rating is 5 and describes it as chosen, but the extraction notes flag an ambiguity that 'Adviser and client agreed to increase to risk rating 5' and that the questionnaire indicated rating 4—this ambiguity about whether the risk rating itself is suitable (and therefore whether the resulting recommendation's risk alignment is as claimed) is not resolved in the report's presentation of disadvantages.
      - The report identifies that Standard Life and Aviva arrangements are being consolidated into one Aviva platform [existing Aviva SP50163762 switched to 'Aviva Platform'], but does not clearly explain in the disadvantages section whether switching from an existing Aviva arrangement into a different Aviva arrangement within the same provider carries specific disadvantages compared to retaining the existing Aviva holding—only generic switch disadvantages are stated.
      - The report does not quantify or explain as a disadvantage the employment income ambiguity noted in extraction: client is 69, expects to retire at 71, currently receiving £300/week HGV driver income and £20/week volunteer income, but the modelling assumptions for whether this income continues beyond retirement are not disclosed, which affects the adequacy of the projection basis and is relevant to whether the recommendation maintains adequate retirement provision.
    The report does provide material disadvantages, risks and limitations as required. However, there are significant consistency issues between what the report asserts as disadvantages and what the supporting evidence quantifies. First, the charge comparison calculation (4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required) is undermined by errors in the underlying charge arithmetic—specifically the EA5 row being computed on the wrong arrangement, making this material disadvantage figure unreliable and potentially misleading. Second, the Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] contains quantified loss scenarios ('Effect of switching' showing specific percentage and monetary losses at different growth rates) that are far more concrete disadvantages than the generic statements in the report. These specific loss figures should have been highlighted prominently as they directly demonstrate the material disadvantage of the recommended switch. Third, the baseline fund values are inconsistent across the report (£110,000 narrative vs £116,998.47 table total vs £110,185 transfer value), creating ambiguity about whether the disadvantages (including the loss scenarios) are calculated on the correct starting position. Fourth, the risk rating ambiguity (questionnaire result 4 vs agreed rating 5) is not resolved in the disadvantages disclosure, yet if the client's true suitable risk rating was actually 4 rather than 5, this would be a material disadvantage of the recommendation that is not disclosed. Fifth, the fact that this involves consolidating from an existing Aviva arrangement into a different Aviva arrangement is not clearly explained as carrying specific disadvantages. Sixth, the employment income modelling assumptions are not disclosed, affecting whether the retirement income projections (which underpin the suitability of the recommendation) are based on assumptions disclosed to the client. The guards allow that disadvantages may be spread across sections and that a reduction in projected value should be listed—however, the quantified loss scenarios in the Switch Report (£10 decrease scenarios) should have been prominently disclosed in the main report, not left to supporting documentation. The report does disclose investment risks generically and does address long-term care as a limitation; these are adequate. Tax treatment is properly qualified as subject to change. However, the material disadvantage of the adverse charge comparison and the failure to prominently state the quantified loss scenarios from the Switch Report represent disclosure shortfalls.

      · Pension switch report - updated.md [G] (P1)
        "Effect of switching is the change in the maturity value of the new solution assuming the fund experiences the same investment growth."
      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P20)

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P15)

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts product-specific risks including: (1) Lifestyling drift — 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' (page 7); (2) Access delay — 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8). Investment-specific risks include: (1) Market timing risk during transition — 'Whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market...If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' (page 8); (2) General investment risk — 'Unit prices and the value of pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee of return of original capital, especially in early years' (page 12); (3) No switch guarantee — 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' (page 12); (4) Stress test outcome — 'A 30% market crash with continued 6% inflation would require pension and investment funds to be used, though analysis shows no shortfall would occur under these stress test conditions' (pages 14-15).
    File:     Product literature (KIIDs, Key Features, Fund Factsheets) for the recommended Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and Pension Portfolio identifies multiple risks: (1) From HSBC KIID [P1], [P3]: The Fund is rated category 3 for risk, with material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, and liquidity risk — 'the ability to buy and sell assets at a favourable price may be affected by a low level of counterparties willing to enter into a transaction with the Fund' [P11]. The Fund invests minimum 70% in other funds across global markets with asset allocation 'Equities: 45-70%, Bonds: 20-55%, Other asset types, including cash: 0-25%' [P3]. (2) From Aviva Key Features [P7], [P14]: General pension risks include 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested' [P14]. Specific withdrawal risks are noted: 'The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving' and 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income' [P4]. (3) From Zurich policy information [P16], [P17]: Property funds 'could fall quite sharply' and 'we may need to delay your transaction...by up to 12 months' [P16]. Generic fund risks include that derivatives-based funds carry risk 'if the financial institution isn't able to meet its obligations, the derivative may be worthless' [P17]. (4) From SIPP research report [P15]: Funds recommended are 'CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (GBP)' and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (GBP)' at 50% each allocation. (5) From Fund Factsheet [P5]: Main risks identified include 'Where overseas investments are held the rate of currency exchange may cause the value of such investments to go down as well as up. The Fund invests in bonds whose value generally falls when interest rates rise' and warnings on illiquidity of certain assets like property [P5].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report identifies lifestyling drift as a client-specific product risk but the evidence does not contain this risk warning in the product literature for the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio or Aviva Pension Portfolio). No document evidences that the recommended funds employ lifestyling strategies or automatically reduce risk as the client approaches retirement.
      - The report emphasizes access delay of '10-15 working days' as a specific risk for the recommendation, but this language does not appear in the evidence provided. The Aviva Key Features [P7] state 'You may sell your investment on any business day by contacting the administrator before the dealing deadline at 12pm' [P9] with no mention of 10-15 working days. The Zurich policy notes delays 'up to 12 months' for property fund transactions in uncertain conditions [P16], but this applies to property holdings, not the recommended balanced funds.
      - The report does not specifically address currency risk, despite the evidence clearly identifying it: the HSBC KIID states 'Where overseas investments are held the rate of currency exchange may cause the value of such investments to go down as well as up' [P5], and the Fund Factsheet repeats this [P5]. The Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio invests across global markets [P1] making currency exposure material, yet the report's risk disclosures do not mention this.
      - The report does not specifically address interest rate risk for bond holdings, despite the Fund Factsheet stating 'The Fund invests in bonds whose value generally falls when interest rates rise' [P5] and this being a material component of the Balanced Portfolio's 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds allocation [P3].
      - The report does not address derivatives risk for the HSBC fund, despite the KIID warning 'Derivatives Risk: derivative values rise and fall at a greater rate than equities and debt instruments. Losses can be greater than the initial investment' [P11] and the Factsheet noting the Fund 'may invest in different types of derivatives...to help the Fund meet its objective' [P3]. The report does not inform the client that derivatives are used in their recommended fund.
      - The report does not address liquidity risk, despite the evidence identifying it: the KIID states 'Liquidity Risk: the ability to buy and sell assets at a favourable price may be affected by a low level of counterparties willing to enter into a transaction with the Fund' [P11], and recommends that the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' [P9]. The recommendation applies to a 69-year-old client with a 2-year horizon to retirement, potentially contradicting the fund's suitability window.
      - The report does not address counterparty risk or default risk for bond investments, both of which the KIID identifies: 'Counterparty Risk The possibility that the counterparty to a transaction may be unwilling or unable to meet its obligations. Credit Risk...Default Risk The issuers of certain bonds could become unwilling or unable to make payments on their bonds' [P3].
      - The report does not address withdrawal/drawdown-specific risks identified in the Aviva Key Features [P4], [P14]: that withdrawals can deplete the pension fund faster than growth, impact means-tested benefits, or leave less for dependants or annuity purchase. These are material to a client considering drawdown.
      - The stress test risk disclosure in the report is generic (market crash, inflation assumption) and does not reference product-specific consequences or limitations of the HSBC or Aviva platforms in a stress scenario.
    The report contains several product-specific and investment-specific risk disclosures (lifestyling drift, access delay, market timing, general investment risk, no switch guarantee, stress test), but comparison against the evidence pack reveals material omissions and inaccuracies. The lifestyling drift warning does not appear in any product literature; no document evidences that the recommended HSBC or Aviva arrangements employ lifestyling. The 10-15 working day access delay is not corroborated by the evidence: Aviva documents indicate business-day dealing, while Zurich (not part of the recommendation) mentions 12-month delays for property transactions. Critically, multiple material risks documented in the fund literature are entirely absent from the report: currency risk (explicitly stated in HSBC materials as relevant to global investing), interest rate risk for bond holdings (material given 20-55% bond allocation), derivatives risk (fund uses derivatives per KIID), liquidity risk (fund unsuitable for 5-year withdrawal horizons, client is 2 years from retirement), counterparty/default risk for bonds, and withdrawal-specific drawdown risks from the Aviva platform. The report discloses general investment risk ('unit prices can fall') and a generic stress test, but these are boilerplate rather than product-specific. The guard notes that 'standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies the general warning but not product-specific risks'; the report's generic statements do not substitute for specific product risks documented in the literature. The guard also notes that deferring risk disclosure to product literature is a limitation; however, the report does not state that risks are in the literature — it simply omits them. The evidence identifies a suitability caution in the KIID that the HSBC fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' [P9]; the client is 69, expects to retire in 2 years, and may require drawdown access, creating a potential mismatch not addressed in the report's risk explanation. No vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence (extraction notes show riskAssessment.vulnerabilityFlags 'NotPresentInDocument'), so the vulnerability overlay does not apply to this finding.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states several tax consequences: (1) Under small pot rules, a £200 withdrawal would have 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate, with estimated net receipt of £170. (2) If the small pot is left to grow until retirement, it could potentially be taken within personal allowance with no tax due. (3) Death benefits before age 75 are tax-free to beneficiaries; after 75, taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate. (4) Personal contributions benefit from tax relief (£80 net becomes £100 gross). (5) Drawdown withdrawals are taxed at marginal rate of income tax. (6) No immediate tax implication on transfer. (7) General caveat that tax statements are based on current law and subject to change. (8) IHT liability stated as £0.
    File:     Product literature [P2] and [P4] state: individuals can normally withdraw up to 25% tax-free, with income tax on remaining amounts depending on personal circumstances; HMRC limits apply (lump sum allowance £268,275 and lump sum & death benefit allowance £1,073,100) with income tax payable above these amounts; benefits before 75 are normally tax-free to nominated beneficiaries unless lump sum & death benefit allowance exceeded; benefits after 75 taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; tax information based on current UK legislation with warning that rules may change. [P6] confirms: flexi-access drawdown allows 25% tax-free lump sum (subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275), with remainder taxed at marginal rate; no upper limit on withdrawals; unused pension funds can pass to beneficiaries free of Inheritance Tax. Terms [P1] describe charging mechanics but not tax consequences. SIPP research [P8], [P17], [P18], [P19] disclose MiFID II cost data and fund charges (0.37% total aggregated costs shown) but do not address tax consequences specific to this client's position.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states small pot withdrawal of £200 would yield £170 net (25% tax-free = £50, 75% × presumed marginal rate = estimated £30 net on remaining £150), but the exact marginal tax rate applied to reach £170 net is not disclosed in the report or evidenced in the file. The calculation assumes a specific tax band (approximately 40% on £150 = £60 tax, leaving £90, plus £50 tax-free = £140 total, not £170; or implies 45% rate on remaining portion, or different calculation basis not explained). The precise tax rate assumption is undisclosed.
      - Report states small pot alternative: 'If you were to leave this pot to grow until you retire, you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due.' Product literature [P2] and [P6] reference the Lump Sum Allowance (£268,275) and Lump Sum & Death Benefit Allowance (£1,073,100) as HMRC limits, but do not confirm that a small pot left to grow would remain within personal allowance at retirement. The statement assumes no other pension benefits or income will exceed personal allowance at retirement, an assumption not verified against the client's stated retirement income objective of £1,700 net per month (approximately £20,400 net annually, which when grossed up for income tax may exceed personal allowance thresholds depending on tax position). The feasibility of the tax-free outcome is conditional on unverified assumptions.
      - Report discloses transfer as having 'no immediate tax implication' but does not address whether a £3,305.55 initial advice fee (stated in [P7]) is deducted before or after transfer, or what tax treatment applies to that fee. Product literature [P4] states 'We don't charge for transferring, but there may be charges associated with the pension or re-registering underlying investments,' but the interaction between the adviser fee and tax treatment at point of transfer is not clarified.
      - Report states drawdown income 'is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax' ([P6] quote in report) but does not establish what the client's marginal rate is. The client's current employment income (£300/week HGV + £20/week volunteer = £320 net/week ≈ £16,640 net annually) plus state pension (£230/week = £11,960 annually) totals approximately £28,600 before Kim's income, but the report does not confirm: (a) whether this is above or below 40% threshold in retirement; (b) whether employment income continues in retirement; (c) whether withdrawal strategy is modelled to stay within basic rate band. Without establishing tax band, the drawdown tax consequence is stated generically, not client-specifically.
      - Report discloses 'All statements concerning the tax treatment... are based on our understanding of current tax law and HMRC practice. Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change' ([P7]) as a blanket caveat but does not identify which specific tax consequences (small pot encashment, drawdown, death benefits, contributions relief) are subject to which risks or uncertainties.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Client's marginal tax rate in retirement (required to verify small pot net calculation of £170 and drawdown tax treatment), Confirmation of whether employment income (£320/week net) continues beyond retirement age 71 and its effect on tax band in retirement, Precise calculation methodology for small pot net receipt of £170 (tax rate assumed), Verification that small pot could be taken within personal allowance at retirement given retirement income objective of £1,700/month net and state pension income, Treatment of £3,305.55 initial advice fee for tax purposes (deducted pre- or post-transfer), Whether withdrawal strategy is modelled to optimize tax efficiency within basic rate band
    The report states multiple tax consequences, but three material gaps prevent full verification: (1) The small pot encashment calculation (£200 → £170 net) depends on the client's marginal tax rate, which is not established in the report or evidence. The file does not confirm the client will be a basic rate (20%) or higher rate (40%+) taxpayer in retirement. Without this, the £170 figure cannot be verified as accurate to the client's position. (2) The alternative small pot strategy (leaving to grow, taken tax-free from personal allowance) assumes the client's total income at retirement will not exceed personal allowance thresholds. The report states retirement objective of £1,700 net/month (about £20,400 net pa), but does not confirm gross equivalent or verify it fits within personal allowance once state pension (£11,960 pa) is added. The assumption is unverified. (3) The generic statement that drawdown is 'taxed at marginal rate' is not client-specific — it restates product mechanics (supported by [P6]) but does not establish what that rate is for this client or confirm whether the withdrawal strategy stays within basic rate. These are not errors in the report's recitation of tax rules; they are absences of client-specific consequence analysis that the regulation (COBS 9.4.7R) requires. The report does cite statutory limits and product features accurately ([P2], [P4], [P6] all match the report's statements on lump sum allowances, death benefit taxation, and drawdown mechanics). However, the guard 'A tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong' applies: these consequences are stated, but their application to this client is not demonstrated. The £0 IHT liability stated matches the personal circumstances disclosed ([P3] and [P5]) and is consistent with the IHT analysis provided. The initial advice fee tax treatment is not addressed in the evidence, creating uncertainty about the net transfer value available for investment.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states one loss scenario: 'Immediate market crash of 30% combined with high inflation of 6%'. The report's stress test conclusion is: 'if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' This is presented on page 15 of the suitability report.
    File:     The case file contains multiple stress tests and risk illustrations. Page 15 of the suitability report (P22) confirms the stress test scenario: a -30% market crash combined with 6% inflation, with the same conclusion about no shortfall. The illustration document (P15) shows investment growth scenarios at lower (1.9%), medium (5%), and higher (8%) rates, all after inflation adjustment. The Key Features document (P2, P7) discloses general pension risks including that 'the value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'You could get back less than has been invested.' However, no specific loss scenario quantifying a percentage loss or pound amount is illustrated in the accompanying documents beyond the stress test mentioned in the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report identifies one loss scenario (30% market crash with 6% inflation) but this scenario concludes with 'no shortfall', meaning the modelled outcome shows no loss to the client's lifestyle or liquidity—this is a stress test result rather than an illustrated loss scenario where the client experiences a defined loss. The report does not illustrate a scenario showing actual fund depletion or reduced retirement income as a consequence of market loss.
      - The illustration document (P15) shows projections at different growth rates (lower 1.9%, medium 5%, higher 8%) but does not present these as explicit loss scenarios; they are presented as 'example' growth rates for accumulation planning, not as demonstrated loss outcomes.
      - No loss scenario quantifies a specific pound amount of loss to the client's fund value or retirement income.
    The report asserts that one loss scenario has been illustrated: an immediate 30% market crash combined with 6% inflation. The guard states that 'a stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one in the risk section,' which permits recognition of the stress test as a loss scenario. The stress test is present in the evidence (P22, P15 reference the same stress testing). However, the critical issue is the nature of the scenario: the report's stress test is designed to show resilience (no shortfall results), not to illustrate a loss scenario as a warning. The report does not present a scenario where the client would experience material loss of fund value or reduction in retirement income. The accompanying illustration (P15) shows growth scenarios but frames them as example projections rather than loss scenarios. The Key Features documentation (P2, P7) contains generic risk warnings but no product-specific or client-specific loss scenario. The report's single identified loss scenario is presented as a resilience test (concluding no shortfall) rather than as a warning illustration of potential harm. For a requirement to 'illustrate' loss scenarios and 'potential consumer harms,' the report needs to show what happens when losses occur, not simply demonstrate that the plan survives a stress. The scenario described in the report is present in the evidence but its framing and prominence do not meet the requirement to illustrate losses as material disadvantages or potential harms.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that risk warnings and disadvantages are dispersed throughout the document under various section headings (Other Considerations of Switching, Fund recommendation, Performance Assessment, Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests) rather than gathered in a dedicated section. The extraction notes that hasDedicatedSection = false, sectionHeading = null, appearsOnlyInAppendix = false, with content on pages 8, 12, 13, 15.
    File:     The evidence contains multiple passages showing disadvantages and risks are presented in various locations: (P1) shows loss scenarios in a table format across low, mid and high growth rates with 'Effect of switching' showing decreases of 9.6%-10.0%; (P2) discusses Critical Yield requirements of 4.8% needed to match existing plan value; (P5) contains explicit risk warnings stating 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider'; (P6) discusses charges with a statement that 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life; (P7) addresses attitude to risk and capacity for loss including acknowledgment that 'your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value'; (P8) describes ongoing service costs of 1% per annum; (P9-P15) contain detailed pension switch reports with comparative analyses, projected values, reduction in yield figures, and critical yield calculations for multiple scenarios.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts disadvantages appear on pages 8, 12, 13, 15 but evidence shows similar content spans pages 3-15 with multiple overlapping sections
      - Report states there is no dedicated section but evidence shows 'Other Considerations of Switching' (P6) and explicit risk warning in (P5) form recognizable risk sections
      - Disadvantage disclosure for Standard Life (P6) states plan is '0.08% more expensive' but this conflicts with internal report inconsistency noted in extraction metadata showing Standard Life at 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% (0.26% more expensive, not cheaper)
      - Loss scenarios presented in tables (P1, P11) show switching effects of £10,400-£15,000 or 9.6%-10.0% decreases at mid-to-high growth rates, but report narrative in (P2) emphasizes achievability of 4.8% critical yield based on 'past performance' without proportionate prominence given to these losses
    The report's prominence claim requires examining where disadvantages appear relative to benefits. Evidence shows: (1) explicit risk warnings are present in dedicated subsections like 'Other Considerations of Switching' (P6) and as a standalone paragraph in 'Performance Assessment' (P5); (2) loss scenarios are quantified in table format across pages 1 and 11 of the evidence, showing projected decreases of £10,400-£15,000 at mid-to-high growth rates; (3) Critical Yield requirements (4.8% to match existing, 7.8% at high growth) are disclosed in (P2); (4) capacity for loss and risk acknowledgment appear in (P7). However, there is a structural prominence issue: the main narrative in (P2) states the new solution 'would need to grow by 4.8% per year... I do believe that this is achievable' based on past performance, while the quantified disadvantage tables showing these same scenarios result in £12,000 decreases (9.4% at mid-growth) appear in appendix-like material (P1, P11). The guard permits distributed risk content to be adequate if clearly stated; however, the juxtaposition of an affirmative achievability statement in the body against loss quantification in tabular appendix form suggests risks may not be presented with equal prominence to benefits. The charge discrepancy in P6 (stating Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive when calculation shows it is 0.26% more expensive) represents a material misstatement of a disadvantage. The evidence does not show a false-positive guard applicable here—the comparison can be made and prominence can be assessed against retrieved content. No vulnerability flags were identified in the extraction metadata, so the vulnerability overlay does not apply to this finding.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report states all 5 existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. For other features being given up: (1) Scottish Widows - ongoing workplace contributions will cease, no multi-asset Risk 5 fund available, no adviser management available, limited withdrawal flexibility. (2) Aviva - limited retirement flexibility. (3) Zurich - lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk as retirement approaches, moving outside agreed risk range. (4) Standard Life - lifestyling funds with same issue as Zurich.
    File:     Policy literature confirms: (1) People's Pension - no life cover, no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no guaranteed growth rates (P7). (2) Scottish Widows - no life cover, no guaranteed minimum pension, no guaranteed minimum fund, no guaranteed growth rates, no waiver of premium (P5). (3) Aviva - no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no guaranteed growth rates, allows adviser charging on other plans but not this one, charges 0.50% annually (P14). (4) Standard Life - no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no guaranteed fund at retirement, does not permit partial transfers for investment purposes, differential fund discount being applied which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' (P10, P17). (5) Zurich - life cover and waiver benefits 'are no longer available on new pension plans', would require separate medical questionnaire if sought (P3). No retirement options comparison found between existing Zurich arrangement and new solution. Switching report shows immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (P13).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report does not explicitly disclose that Zurich and Standard Life arrangements currently hold life cover and waiver benefits that are being given up, though the Zurich policy literature states these 'are no longer available on new pension plans' and 'would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan' (P3)
      - Report states for Zurich that funds 'will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' due to lifestyling, but does not explain what retirement options the current Zurich plan offers versus the new solution in terms of withdrawal flexibility
      - Report does not disclose the Standard Life differential fund discount detail: 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' (P10, P17)
      - Report does not compare retirement option availability between existing arrangements and new solution; evidence shows Aviva has FAD and UFPLS available (P8), Scottish Widows has UFPLS and partial UFPLS only (P8), but new solution shows 50% Columbia Threadneedle and 50% HSBC balanced funds with no explicit retirement options comparison (P13)
      - Death benefit shortfall of £3,305 immediately post-switch is disclosed in switching report (P13) but not mentioned in the suitability report itself
      - Report does not disclose that People's Pension 'does not offer an option to purchase an annuity' whereas some existing plans may have had annuity options (P16)
    The requirement tests whether the report fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explains all material disadvantages and features being given up. The report identifies several features lost: workplace contributions ending for Scottish Widows, lack of multi-asset Risk 5 fund, no adviser management, limited withdrawal flexibility, and lifestyling funds moving outside risk range. However, material features present in the policy literature are not disclosed in the report. Most significantly: (1) Zurich and Standard Life both held life cover and waiver benefits. The Zurich literature explicitly states these 'are no longer available on new pension plans' and require separate medical questionnaire to obtain independently (P3). This is a material protection being given up that is entirely absent from the report's discussion of what is lost. (2) The Standard Life policy reveals a 'Differential Fund Discount from the Total Expense Ratio (TER)' being applied as 'low charges' that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' (P10, P17). This material advantage is not disclosed in the report. (3) The switching report documents an immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (P13), which is a material loss scenario that does not appear in the suitability report. (4) Retirement option availability differs between arrangements—Aviva offers FAD, UFPLS and partial UFPLS (P8); Scottish Widows offers only UFPLS and partial UFPLS (P8)—but the report does not compare these options against the new solution's options. (5) People's Pension 'does not offer an option to purchase an annuity' (P16), which is a material feature unavailable in the new solution that is not disclosed. The report contains the required identification of disadvantages but material features from the evidence pack are absent from the report. This is a disclosure gap on features the client is giving up.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended plan requires 4.8% annual growth to age 75 to match the existing plan value at a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, and states 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' [P23, page 11]. The report also claims 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' [P23, page 13] and 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility' [P23, page 13]. The report discloses that switching results in lower costs and greater investment flexibility, with the new scheme offering 'a wider range of investment options' and 'Multi-asset fund selection' [P20]. The report states disadvantages include market timing risk, no performance guarantee, and general investment risks [P23, pages 8 and 12]. The direction of cost comparison is stated as 'RecommendedMoreExpensive'.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows that at mid-growth (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 while the new solution projects to £125,000—a reduction of £2,000 or 1.6%. The critical yield required is 3.2%, not 4.8%. At high growth (5.88%), the existing pension projects to £150,000 versus £147,000 for new solution—a reduction of £3,000 or 2.0%. The research report [P12] shows the combined performance of recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) over 5 years is 42.40%, with individual fund costs of 0.55% (CT fund) and ongoing costs of 0.24% combined [P13]. The existing Aviva arrangement has an ongoing charge of 0.50% [P19], while the new solution has 0.44% ongoing charges [P19]. The comparison table [P1] shows the new solution is more expensive in terms of critical yield (3.2% vs baseline) and results in projected value reduction at all growth rates. The SIPP research [P12] indicates the recommended portfolio aligns with risk level 5 [P14]. The initial adviser charge of £3,305 is taken from the new solution [P1, P17], creating an immediate shortfall in death benefit of £3,305 [P17].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states required growth of 4.8% per year; switch report shows critical yield of 3.2% for new solution
      - Report claims 'past performance' supports achievability; switch report projects new solution underperforms existing at all growth rates (mid: £125,000 vs £127,000; high: £147,000 vs £150,000)
      - Report claims recommended funds 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings'; no evidence provided comparing historical performance of new recommended funds (CT Universal MAP, HSBC Global Strategy) against the existing Aviva holdings that are being switched
      - Report describes 'comparable performance with reduced volatility'; projections show reduced maturity values, not comparable performance
      - Report identifies costs as 'RecommendedMoreExpensive' but does not explain why cost increase is justified when projections are lower
      - Report states new solution offers 'lower costs' [P20] but charge comparison shows recommended 0.44% vs existing Aviva 0.50%, representing only 0.06% saving, while initial advice charge of £3,305 creates immediate shortfall [P17]
    The report provides justification for increased costs by appealing to past fund performance and achievability of critical yield, but this justification is contradicted by the supporting evidence. The switch report [P1] explicitly shows that even at the advised mid-growth rate of 2.94-2.95%, the new solution underperforms: £125,000 projected versus £127,000 existing, requiring 3.2% critical yield rather than the 4.8% stated in the report. The claim that recommended funds demonstrated 'stronger performance' compared to existing Aviva holdings cannot be verified from the evidence—no historical performance comparison is provided. The guard permits weak justifications that repeat benefits without connecting them to costs, but this case goes further: the justification contradicts the projections. The report frames the switch positively ('lower costs, greater investment flexibility') [P20] but the underlying switch analysis shows the new solution is less attractive on maturity value basis. The initial advice charge of £3,305 immediately reduces the invested amount, creating a £3,305 death benefit shortfall [P17], which is disclosed but not integrated into the cost-benefit analysis. The critical yield discrepancy (4.8% vs 3.2%) is material: at 4.8% growth, the client would need significantly higher market returns than the analysis supports. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability factors are noted in the evidence that would affect assessment of this explanation, though the client's age (69, expecting to retire at 71) and reliance on pension funds for retirement (as noted on page 4 of report regarding capacity for loss) may affect materiality of the £2,000-£3,000 projected reduction.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses six material disadvantages: (1) no guarantee return on new investment will exceed existing arrangement; (2) switching between pension providers does not guarantee greater fund or outperformance; (3) adviser fee makes plan 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (4) recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed Zurich funds; (5) Zurich investments in higher-risk funds are more susceptible to market volatility; (6) chart does not consider potential long-term care costs. The report also discloses three limitations: (1) transferring all pensions would limit withdrawal flexibility; (2) Scottish Widows lacks single multi-asset fund for risk rating 5, requiring two funds with 20-30% in cash; (3) stakeholder pensions do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges. The report discloses two product-specific risks: (1) lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; (2) access to funds takes 10-15 working days.
    File:     The pension switch report [P7] contains extensive material disadvantages and risks not mentioned in the suitability report: (1) Effect of switching shows projected losses at age 75 of £3,000 (2.1% decrease) at mid growth, £2,000 (1.9% decrease) at low growth, and £3,000 (2.0% decrease) at high growth for overall position [P1]; (2) When Zurich is retained alone [P10], effect of switching shows £10,400 (9.6% decrease) at low growth, £12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid growth, and £15,000 (10.0% decrease) at high growth; (3) Critical yield requirements disclosed: 6.3% overall [P1], 6.2% high growth [P3], 7.8% for Zurich only [P10]; (4) Reduction in Yield for new solution ranges from 0.6% to 2.3% depending on scenario and existing plan [P1, P3, P10]; (5) The switching report shows the new solution requires substantially higher returns to match existing arrangements, with effect of switching consistently negative across all growth scenarios. The Zurich Policy Info [P8] discloses: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits' [P8]. Standard Life Policy Info [P15] discloses differential fund discount: 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The Key Features document [P20] discloses: 'You should retain the services of a financial adviser. You'll need an adviser if you want to make certain changes to your Pension Portfolio' and multiple withdrawal options with tax consequences including tax at marginal rate on lump sums beyond 25% tax-free element [P20].
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states adviser fee impact as 4.24% requiring £4,672 outperformance but does not cite the pension switch report's detailed effect-of-switching analysis showing consistent projected losses across all growth scenarios (£2,000-£3,000 overall, £10,400-£15,000 for Zurich alone)
      - The report does not disclose that life cover and waiver benefits attached to existing Zurich plan cannot be replicated on new arrangement without medical re-underwriting [P8]
      - The report does not disclose the loss of differential fund discount available on Standard Life arrangement which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' [P15]
      - The report does not quantify the critical yield requirements (6.3% overall, up to 7.8% for Zurich retention) necessary for new solution to match existing arrangement maturity values [P1, P10]
      - The report does not disclose the Reduction in Yield impact ranging from 0.6% to 2.3% depending on which existing arrangements are transferred [P1, P3, P10]
      - The report does not disclose structured loss scenarios showing negative effect of switching across low, mid and high growth rates for different retention combinations [P1, P3, P10]
      - The report does not disclose tax consequences of withdrawal options (tax at marginal rate on lump sums above 25% tax-free element) disclosed in Pension Portfolio Key Features [P20]
      - The report does not disclose the ongoing adviser requirement: 'You'll need an adviser if you want to make certain changes to your Pension Portfolio' [P20]
    The pension switch report [P7] provides comprehensive quantitative analysis of switching impact that directly underpins the suitability assessment but is not adequately referenced or integrated into the suitability report's risk disclosure. The report acknowledges in generic terms that 'there is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater' and 'the plan is 4.24% more expensive' but does not present the concrete loss scenarios from the switch report: across all growth rates and retention scenarios, the new solution shows projected losses ranging from £10 to £15,000 at retirement (in some cases representing 10% reduction in fund value). The Critical Yield analysis shows the new solution must deliver 6.3% to 7.8% returns (depending on scenario) versus lower historical performance, creating material underperformance risk not quantified in the main report. The loss of life cover and waiver benefits on Zurich transfer [P8] is a material feature being surrendered that is not addressed in the suitability report despite being 'particularly important if your health has deteriorated.' The loss of Standard Life's differential fund discount [P15] represents ongoing cost disadvantage that may not be recoverable. The tax consequences of flexible withdrawal options [P20] showing marginal rate taxation on lump sums above 25% are material to a client aged 69 approaching retirement but are absent from risk disclosure. The structured effect-of-switching analysis in the pension switch report demonstrates that the recommendation imposes measurable projected losses compared to existing arrangements across all modelled growth scenarios, which constitutes a material disadvantage not adequately disclosed in prominence or specificity in the main suitability report. The switching report treats these losses as inherent to consolidation, but the suitability report does not transparently present this trade-off to the client. The guards acknowledge that research findings undercutting the recommendation are material information; here, the switch report's consistent finding of negative effect across scenarios is such material information.
