CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report states Aviva charge 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) but page 11 comparison shows Aviva 0.50% existing vs 0.44% recommended — the arithmetic (0.21 + 0.22 = 0.43%, not 0.44%) and fund value calculation mismatch: 0.21% and 0.22% charges imply fund values of £110,185.71 and £115,195.45 respectively, neither matching actual portfolio values. Report states Aviva existing 15.58% 5-year performance but evidence (P6, P14) shows 15.85% over period 30/09/2020-30/09/2025 and 13/10/2020-13/10/2025 period referenced differently in chart. Report asserts Zurich charge 0.93% existing vs 0.44% recommended as 'Better' but charge arithmetic (P8 extraction notes) shows EA5 row computed on wrong plan (fund value £35,805.77 for 0.52% charge does not match Zurich £103,439.24), making the comparison unreliable. 32 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts 10 comparisons across multiple dimensions: (1) Aviva existing 0.50% fund charge vs recommended 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management), assessed as 'Better'; (2) Aviva existing Risk level 2 lifestyling funds vs recommended Risk level 5 Balanced portfolio, assessed as 'Better'; (3) Aviva existing limited fund range vs recommended 80+ funds, assessed as 'Better'; (4) Aviva existing no adviser servicing vs recommended adviser management with direct charge deduction, assessed as 'Better'; (5) Aviva existing FAD/Full/partial UFPLS vs recommended Full/partial UFPLS with Flexi Access Drawdown, assessed as 'Equivalent'; (6) Aviva existing 15.58% 5-year performance vs recommended 40.02%, assessed as 'Better'; (7) Scottish Widows existing Risk level 4 vs recommended cash encashment, assessed as 'NotAssessed'; (8) Scottish Widows existing no adviser servicing vs encashment, assessed as 'NotAssessed'; (9) Zurich existing 0.93% annual recurring charges vs recommended 0.44% total, assessed as 'Better'; (10) Zurich existing Risk level 7 Managed AP fund blended with risk level 2 vs recommended Risk level 5 Balanced portfolio, assessed as 'Better'. The report also states performance figures: Aviva existing 15.58% (13/10/2020-13/10/2025), Zurich existing 44.32% (13/10/2020-13/10/2025), Standard Life existing 45.62% (Since Launch).
    File:     The evidence shows: (P18) Aviva SP50163762 exists with fund value £3,744.36, holds Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 with auto-switching lifestage investment approach, no guarantees or GMP. (P16) Existing arrangements table confirms five pensions: People's Pension £6,601.55 (0.50% + £4.50 admin), Scottish Widows £211.32 (0.19% + 0.10% platform), Aviva £3,744.36 (0.50% fund charge), Zurich £103,439.24 (0.93% charge), Standard Life £3,002.00. (P6, P14) Performance data shows: Aviva existing 15.85% over 5 years (30/09/2020-30/09/2025), Zurich Managed 46.14%, Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 42.40%, Standard Life At Retirement Universal 45.62% since launch. (P1, P7) Retirement options evidence confirms FAD available for some arrangements, full/partial UFPLS available, annuity available. (P12) Pension switch report shows new solution charges at 0.6% overall with initial adviser charge of £3,305 and ongoing 1.00% annual adviser charge. (P3, P9) Pension switch report shows alternative comparisons considered: Stakeholder pension comparison, individual plan-only comparisons (Aviva only, Zurich only, Standard Life only), with maturity values at retirement age 75. (P4, P5) Switch reports show projected values comparing existing to new solution across low, mid and high growth scenarios. (P16, P17) Comparisons in switch report show effect of switching calculated for each scenario.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva charge 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) but page 11 comparison shows Aviva 0.50% existing vs 0.44% recommended — the arithmetic (0.21 + 0.22 = 0.43%, not 0.44%) and fund value calculation mismatch: 0.21% and 0.22% charges imply fund values of £110,185.71 and £115,195.45 respectively, neither matching actual portfolio values
      - Report states Aviva existing 15.58% 5-year performance but evidence (P6, P14) shows 15.85% over period 30/09/2020-30/09/2025 and 13/10/2020-13/10/2025 period referenced differently in chart
      - Report asserts Zurich charge 0.93% existing vs 0.44% recommended as 'Better' but charge arithmetic (P8 extraction notes) shows EA5 row computed on wrong plan (fund value £35,805.77 for 0.52% charge does not match Zurich £103,439.24), making the comparison unreliable
      - Report states Zurich existing 44.32% 5-year performance but evidence (P6) shows Zurich Managed 46.14% (30/09/2020-30/09/2025)
      - Report recommends Aviva Risk 5 portfolio assessed as 'Better' but provides no evidence that existing Aviva lifestyling fund currently delivers worse alignment than Risk 5 at client's current age 69 approaching retirement at 71; lifestyling automatically reduces risk approaching retirement which aligns with client's lifecycle
      - Report compares existing to recommended but charge analysis in report page 11 contains internal inconsistency: states Aviva '0.06% more expensive' but table shows 0.50% vs 0.44% (correct 0.06% difference); contradicts page 8 statement that Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive' when table shows Standard Life 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% (0.26% MORE expensive for recommended)
      - Report states recommended solution provides 'greater investment choice — 80+ funds' but evidence (P1) shows existing Aviva arrangement already offers 80+ funds in fund range table
      - Report shows Scottish Widows comparison as 'NotAssessed' but does not disclose that this arrangement will be encashed rather than transferred, losing any accumulated benefits or options without analysis
      - Report compares Zurich existing risk level 7 blended with level 2 vs recommended risk 5, but evidence (P11) shows Zurich existing risk level is not stated as 7; client agreed risk rating 5 (ambiguity noted in extraction about whether initial questionnaire was 4, increased to 5)
      - Report states advisor charges will be 'deducted directly' under recommendation but evidence (P12) shows initial charge of £3,305 taken from new solution reducing initial investment, creating immediate shortfall of £3,305 in death benefit (P12)
      - Report does not disclose that switching involves transfer from People's Pension (workplace scheme with employer contributions £897.13 PA per P16) to recommended Aviva Platform, but no analysis provided of loss of employer contributions
    The report compares existing versus recommended across multiple dimensions as required. However, the comparison has material defects: (1) Charge calculations are arithmetically inconsistent — the stated 0.44% total does not equal 0.21% + 0.22% and the fund values implied by these percentages do not match the actual portfolio values; (2) Performance data dates vary: report uses 13/10/2020-13/10/2025 but evidence shows 30/09/2020-30/09/2025 for comparable figures, with numerical discrepancies (15.58% vs 15.85% for Aviva, 44.32% vs 46.14% for Zurich); (3) The charge comparison for Standard Life contradicts itself — page 8 claims 0.08% saving but page 11 table shows recommended is 0.26% MORE expensive, not cheaper; (4) The Zurich charge comparison relies on row EA5 which the extraction notes identifies as computed on the wrong plan (£35,805.77 fund value does not match Zurich £103,439.24), making that comparison unreliable; (5) The recommendation of Risk 5 for existing Aviva lifestyling fund does not address that lifestyling automatically reduces risk as client approaches retirement (at 71), meaning current Risk 2 positioning may already be appropriate for lifecycle; (6) Fund range comparison claim of 'greater choice' via 80+ funds on new platform contradicts evidence (P1) showing existing Aviva already offers 80+ funds; (7) Scottish Widows encashment is assessed as 'NotAssessed' but no analysis provided of loss of any accumulated benefits; (8) People's Pension workplace scheme recommendation does not disclose analysis of loss of employer contribution (£897.13 PA); (9) Adviser charging structure shows £3,305 initial charge reducing fund value immediately, creating stated shortfall in death benefit, but report frames this as benefit ('direct charge deduction') without clear disclosure. The pension switch report (P3-P20) does contain more comprehensive alternative comparisons (stakeholder, individual plans only) and effect of switching analysis, but the main suitability report's comparison as presented has internal inconsistencies and unsupported claims.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts multiple benefits of switching: lower overall cost structure (allowing more savings to remain invested for growth); funds not lifestyling (portfolio remains aligned with agreed risk range); greater investment choice (more tailored strategy); adviser charging facilitation; ongoing monitoring; encashment of small pots not detracting from retirement; and simplification through consolidation. For costs, the report states: no exit penalties (£0); for Aviva existing arrangement an annual charge saving of -0.06% (£-2.25); for Zurich a charge difference of -0.49% (£-506.85) with market risk during 10-15 working day transition; for Standard Life a charge difference of 0.26% (£268.94) with market risk during transition. The report further asserts a 'Critical Yield' requiring 4.8% annual growth to match the current plan value, and that this is 'achievable' based on past fund performance.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report provides comparative analysis. For the three-plan consolidation (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life switching to new Aviva solution), the report shows: at mid-growth (2.95%), existing pension projected value £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (Effect of switching: £2,000 or 1.6% decrease). For Zurich alone switching: existing £102,000 vs new £100,000 (Effect of switching: £2,000 or 2.0% decrease; Critical yield 0.3%). For Standard Life alone switching: existing £108,000 vs new solution £97,600 at low growth (Effect of switching: £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; Critical yield at mid-growth 4.8%). The switching report identifies a death benefit shortfall immediately post-switch of £3,305 (fund value post-switch £106,880.60 vs pre-switch £110,185.60). Charge data in Pension Switch Report shows Aviva existing 0.59%, Zurich existing 0.29%, Standard Life existing 0.59%; new solution 0.6%. No explicit comparison table reconciling these percentages with the annual differences claimed in the suitability report is present for all plans together. The switching report notes 'any fees applied at the point of switching may reduce the capital value' but does not cross-reference the £3,305 death benefit loss as a switching cost.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Aviva annual charge saving of -0.06% but switching report shows Aviva existing at 0.59% and new solution at 0.6%, implying a charge increase of 0.01%, not a saving
      - Report claims Aviva annual charge saving of £-2.25 but this figure does not reconcile with either arrangement value or the stated percentages
      - Report claims Zurich annual charge saving of -0.49% but switching report shows Zurich existing 0.29% and new solution 0.6%, implying a charge increase of 0.31%, not a saving
      - Report claims Zurich annual charge saving of £-506.85 but this contradicts the direction and magnitude of the switching report's cost comparison
      - Report claims Standard Life annual charge increase of 0.26% (£268.94) but switching report shows Standard Life existing 0.59% and new solution 0.6%, implying only 0.01% increase, and the pound amount does not reconcile with Standard Life's current fund value of £3,002
      - Report asserts 4.8% critical yield is 'achievable' based on past performance but switching report for Standard Life shows critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth with 'Effect of switching' showing £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected value at mid-growth
      - Report states benefits outweigh any higher costs but for Standard Life the switching report shows switching results in a 9.4-10.0% decrease in projected maturity value across growth scenarios
      - Report does not disclose the £3,305 immediate death benefit shortfall as a cost of switching, treating it as a separate 'impact' rather than a switching cost
      - Report asserts lower overall cost structure but aggregate switching report shows net projected value decrease of £2,000 at mid-growth (1.6% reduction) when three plans consolidated
    The requirement demands evidence that benefits outweigh costs of switching, with charges and switching costs fully disclosed. The report makes clear assertions about cost savings (Aviva -0.06%, Zurich -0.49%) and portrays these as justifying the switch. However, the Pension Switch Report's charge data contradicts these assertions. The Aviva comparison in the suitability report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' (Page 7) implying current is more expensive, but the switching report shows existing Aviva at 0.59% and new solution at 0.6%, meaning the new is more expensive. The same logical inversion appears for Zurich. The critical mismatch is material: the suitability report's cost-benefit case rests on these charge savings, yet the switching report shows no such savings exist. For Standard Life, the charge difference stated in the report (0.26% or £268.94) does not reconcile with Standard Life's actual fund value of £3,002 (0.26% of £3,002 = £7.81, not £268.94). The figure £268.94 at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's value, suggesting the charge comparison row was computed on the wrong plan. The report asserts that the 4.8% critical yield required is 'achievable' based on past fund performance, but the switching report for Standard Life shows that even at this growth rate, switching produces a 9.4% decrease in projected maturity value (£127,000 to £115,000), meaning the critical yield does not deliver the client to 'the same value as your current plan' as stated in the main report. The death benefit shortfall of £3,305 post-switch is identified in the switching report but not disclosed as a switching cost in the suitability report's cost analysis. The consolidation of three plans shows a net decrease in projected value of £2,000 at mid-growth, contradicting the narrative that lower costs mean more savings remain invested for growth. These are not minor disclosure gaps; they are contradictions at the core of the switching justification.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states on page 5 that all five existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. The canonical model shows five entries, each asserting 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'. The extraction notes that /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent from the extracted model, indicating the report does not address features or guarantees that may be lost through the recommended switches. The report recommends four actions: retain People's Pension; encash Scottish Widows (£211.32); switch Aviva SP50163762 to Aviva Platform; and switch Standard Life and Zurich to consolidated arrangements. For none of these switches does the report disclose specific analysis of lost features, guarantees, life cover, waiver benefits, or other valuable characteristics beyond the blanket statement that no safeguarded benefits exist.
    File:     Evidence from provider documentation confirms: Aviva Policy Info [P17] states 'Life cover: None' and 'Waiver of payment: None - it's no longer possible to add this.' It also confirms 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension'. Zurich Policy Info [P1] states 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' Zurich Policy Info [P15] confirms 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Scottish Widows Policy Info [P2] and [P10] table confirms 'Guaranteed Minimum Pension: No', 'Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR)?: No', 'Waiver of premium: No', 'Life cover: No'. Standard Life Policy Info [P4] states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', and 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' People's Pension Policy Info [P8] contains no reference to life cover, waiver, or guarantees. All five arrangements lack life cover and waiver benefits as standard features on modern pension products.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts all five arrangements contain 'no safeguarded benefits' but does not disclose that some arrangements (particularly Zurich) previously offered life cover and waiver benefits that are no longer available on new plans, creating a potential loss even if not technically 'safeguarded'. The report does not explain this distinction or address the client's ability to replicate these features separately.
      - The report does not analyse or compare life cover and waiver features between existing and recommended arrangements. Provider documentation from Zurich [P1] notes these benefits 'will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits' and that medical questionnaires are now required, but the report makes no reference to health status, medical underwriting, or the practical consequences of losing existing cover.
      - The report does not address whether any existing arrangement holds guaranteed annuity rates (GAR), guaranteed minimum pension (GMP), or other valuable guarantees that may be lost. Although provider documentation confirms none exist, the report's silence means no affirmative analysis was performed.
      - The report does not specifically address the Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32): no analysis of whether features are lost, whether the small amount justifies immediate encashment, or what tax treatment is applied beyond a general statement on another arrangement.
      - The report's assertion of 'no safeguarded benefits' across all arrangements is stated uniformly without plan-specific analysis, yet the provider documents show different feature sets (e.g., some plans mention auto-switching, others do not; some hold investment choice constraints the report does not address).
    The extraction report indicates that /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent ('NotPresentInDocument'), meaning the report does not contain explicit analysis of features or guarantees lost through the recommended switches. However, the comparison can be performed because the provider documents (category E) corroborate the report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits exist and confirm the absence of life cover, waiver of premium, GAR, GMP, and guaranteed funds on all five arrangements. The guard states 'An explicit no safeguarded benefits statement in the report must still be corroborated by the policy documentation.' The report's statement that each arrangement contains 'no safeguarded benefits' (quoted verbatim from page 5) is corroborated by the supporting provider documents. Each provider confirms no safeguarded benefits, no GAR, no GMP, no life cover, and no waiver. However, the absence of a disclosure requirement being met is distinct from an affirmative disclosure being made. The requirement asks that the report disclose what may be lost; the report asserts nothing is lost (no safeguarded benefits exist). This is consistent with the evidence. The report does not fail because it correctly states that no safeguarded benefits exist on any arrangement. However, the report does not provide the full context Zurich's policy information reveals: that life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans and that medical underwriting is now required if the client wishes to obtain similar cover. The report's analysis is not detailed or comparative; it states the minimum fact (no safeguarded benefits) without addressing the practical implications of moving from plans that historically held such features (even if no longer available on replacement products) or the loss of optionality (ability to add waiver in future). The report makes no disclosure of this context, and the evidence shows this context exists and may be material to the client's interests, particularly given the reference in [P1] to health deterioration. The requirement to address 'safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features' is not fully met by stating only that no safeguarded benefits exist; valuable features (life cover, waiver, optionality) should be addressed where they previously existed or were previously available on plans being replaced. The discrepancy is not between assertion and evidence but between the depth of analysis required and what was performed.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three alternatives were considered and rejected: (1) Remain in existing Aviva pension with internal fund switch — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile (lifestyling funds reduce risk outside agreed range) and plan is 0.06% more expensive with £4.50 admin charge; (2) Transfer Scottish Widows to Aviva platform — rejected because Scottish Widows does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned with risk 5 (would require two funds, one entirely cash, leaving 20-30% out of market), does not allow adviser servicing, and does not offer flexible access; (3) Remain in Zurich with internal fund switch — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds (blend of level 7 and level 2 required), blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options, and consolidation is priority.
    File:     Evidence confirms alternatives were considered: P1 and P2 contain the exact rejection rationales for all three alternatives. P9-P20 (Pension switch report) provides detailed charge comparison analysis and projections showing effect of switching. P6 confirms Zurich alternative discussed with rationale about multi-asset fund availability and performance. The evidence supports that comparisons were made between existing and new solutions with charge analysis provided.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' with £4.50 annual admin charge, but derived figures show EA3 existing annual charge of £18.72 at 0.50% on Aviva's £3,744 current value, against NP1 recommended charge of £484.81 at 0.44% on an implied £110,185 fund value — these are fundamentally different fund values being compared and the charge comparison is not reliable across different scales
      - Report describes Standard Life existing arrangement charge comparison but derived figures show EA5 impact £268.94 at 0.26% is computed on Zurich's £103,438 value (wrong plan), making any Standard Life specific charge analysis unreliable
      - The pension switch report (P9-P20) shows comparison analyses but with projections to age 75, while suitability report does not specify retirement age assumptions for its alternative rejections
      - Report states Scottish Widows alternative 'would require combining two funds, one entirely in cash, leaving 20-30% of portfolio out of the market' but evidence does not contain Scottish Widows fund listing to corroborate this specific claim about available funds
    Three alternatives were considered and explicitly rejected with client-specific reasoning stated in the report. The evidence in P1, P2, and P6 corroborates that each alternative was assessed with named reasons: risk alignment issues, cost comparisons, adviser servicing availability, and retirement flexibility options. The pension switch report (P9-P20) provides charge-based analysis supporting the comparison framework. However, a material problem emerges: the report's charge comparisons for alternatives lack reliable corroboration. The derived figures reveal that comparisons of ongoing charges between existing plans and the recommended solution rest on different fund values (Aviva comparison at £3,744 existing vs £110,185 recommended; Standard Life comparison appears to use wrong plan entirely per EA5 row). This means the stated reason that Aviva existing is '0.06% more expensive' cannot be verified as accurate — it may be a percentage point difference calculated on mismatched scales. While alternatives were considered (requirement met on that limb), the cost-justification dimension of the rejection reasoning is unreliable. Additionally, the report does not provide evidence that Scottish Widows fund range was checked — P1 contains the rejection reasoning but no supporting documentation listing Scottish Widows funds available at risk level 5. The recommendation of consolidation is stated as a client priority (P6) but the report does not evidence that alternatives maintaining separate schemes were explicitly considered and rejected on grounds other than fund availability and adviser servicing. The comparison framework exists but key numerical justifications (charge differentials) cannot be corroborated.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts four switching cost scenarios: (1) Aviva arrangement with -0.06% annual charge difference (-£2.25) and zero exit penalty; (2) Scottish Widows arrangement with zero exit penalty only; (3) Zurich arrangement with -0.49% annual charge difference (-£506.85), zero exit penalty, and out-of-market exposure risk during 10-15 working days; (4) Standard Life arrangement with +0.26% annual charge difference (+£268.94), zero exit penalty, and out-of-market exposure risk. The report claims benefits including lower costs, removal of lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and pension simplification. For Zurich specifically, the report states a critical yield of 4.8% pa is required to match the existing plan value at mid-growth assumptions, and a reduction in yield of 2.2% (existing 0.3%, new 2.1%).
    File:     The pension switch report (P4, P7) shows that for the Zurich pension only comparison, the existing pension has a reduction in yield of 0.3% pa and projected maturity value of £127,000 at mid-growth (2.95%), while the new solution has a reduction in yield of 2.2% pa and projected maturity value of £115,000 at mid-growth, representing a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in effect of switching. The critical yield for the new solution is stated as 4.8%. For the Standard Life pension only comparison (P6, P7), the existing pension projects to £2,910 at zero growth and the new solution to £2,900, a difference of £30 or 0.6% decrease. The switch report shows no comparable charge differential of -0.06% for Aviva or -0.49% for Zurich or +0.26% for Standard Life. The report does not evidence any annual charge difference figures of -£2.25, -£506.85, or +£268.94. The overall pension values total £116,998.47 (from five arrangements), but the suitability report states approximately £110,000 and the switch report shows £110,185.60 as a transfer value.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts an annual charge difference for Aviva of -0.06% (-£2.25), but the switch report evidence does not display this figure or calculate such a saving for the Aviva existing to new solution transition.
      - The report asserts an annual charge difference for Zurich of -0.49% (-£506.85), but the switch report shows Zurich existing charge of 0.3% (reduction in yield) and new solution 2.1% (reduction in yield), a difference of 1.8 percentage points representing additional cost, not savings, inconsistent with the claimed -0.49% saving.
      - The report asserts an annual charge difference for Standard Life of +0.26% (+£268.94), but the switch report shows Standard Life existing charge of 0.6% (reduction in yield) and new solution 0.6% (reduction in yield), indicating no charge difference, not +0.26%.
      - The report states annual charge difference amounts of -£2.25 for Aviva, -£506.85 for Zurich, and +£268.94 for Standard Life, but none of these specific figures appear in the switch report evidence.
      - The report's derived calculation shows the £268.94 annual cost at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's current value (not Standard Life's £3,002), indicating the charge row is computed on the wrong plan.
      - The report asserts the benefit of lower costs and savings remaining invested for growth, but for the Standard Life arrangement the report's own figures show costs increasing by £268.94 annually, contradicting the lower cost benefit claim.
      - The report asserts that the Zurich arrangement will require 4.8% critical yield to match existing value, consistent with the evidence at mid-growth (P7), but the reduction in yield increases from 0.3% to 2.1%, a net increase in drag of 1.8%, which is not justified by the stated benefits of lower costs.
      - The report does not disclose that switching the Zurich arrangement (largest by value at £103,439.24) produces a 9.4% decrease in projected maturity value (£12,000 reduction) at mid-growth assumptions, despite claiming benefits of lower costs justify the switch.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Independent comparison of the stated annual charge differences (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%) against the switch report's reduction in yield figures, which are the only charge metrics available in the evidence, Verification of the specific cost impact figures (-£2.25, -£506.85, +£268.94) against calculated amounts derived from the switch report, Clarification of which pension arrangements correspond to which cost analysis rows, as the Standard Life row's calculations appear to reference Zurich's fund value, Disclosure and analysis of why the Zurich arrangement (largest at £103,439.24) shows a projected decrease of £12,000 (9.4%) in maturity value despite being recommended for switching on cost grounds, Evidence supporting the claim that non-financial benefits (pension simplification, greater investment choice, ongoing monitoring) justify the net increase in reduction in yield for Zurich (0.3% to 2.1%) and the projected £12,000 maturity value reduction
    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the annual charge difference figures asserted by the report (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%) and their corresponding monetary amounts (-£2.25, -£506.85, +£268.94) do not appear anywhere in the switch report evidence. The only charge data available is the reduction in yield figures in the comparative tables (P7, P6). For Zurich, the suitability report claims benefits justify a -0.49% charge saving, but the switch report evidence shows the reduction in yield worsens from 0.3% to 2.1%, a net 1.8% increase in annual drag. The report states this requires a 4.8% critical yield to match the existing plan, which is corroborated by the evidence (P7), but the effect of switching is -£12,000 or -9.4% in projected maturity value at mid-growth. For Standard Life, the report asserts +0.26% higher costs (£268.94), but the switch report shows both existing and new solution at 0.6% reduction in yield with only £30 difference in maturity value. The charge arithmetic check reveals the £268.94 annual cost at 0.26% correlates to a £103,438 fund value (Zurich, not Standard Life at £3,002), indicating a calculation error or mislabelling. The report frames the recommendation as cost-justified (lower overall cost structure), but this is directly contradicted by the Zurich analysis showing increased drag and reduced projected value. The guard 'non-financial benefits can justify a net cost increase where argued explicitly' applies, but the report does not make this argument for Zurich; instead it explicitly asserts cost savings (-0.49%) as the justification. For Standard Life, the alleged cost increase (+0.26%) is not supported by the evidence, which shows equivalent charges. The report discloses alternatives considered (existing arrangements retained in some comparisons), but does not explain why the Zurich arrangement specifically, representing 88% of the consolidated portfolio by value, should be switched despite showing material projected underperformance.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that all five existing arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' as quoted on page 5. The extracted model shows no data on safeguarded benefits being given up (the path /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent from the canonical model).
    File:     The Scottish Widows Master Trust information [P13] confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' The Fact Find [P14] contains a template section asking about guarantees, annuity rates, bonuses and material features, but entries are blank, suggesting no safeguarded benefits were identified. The pension switch report [P1, P2] contains comparisons of existing and new solutions but does not explicitly address whether safeguarded benefits or guarantees are being surrendered. The Standard Life policy illustration [P8] and value statement [P11] do not mention any guaranteed benefits, annuities, or terminal bonuses.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report extracted no data on whether safeguarded benefits exist on the existing arrangements or are being given up, despite this being a material element of the replacement analysis. The canonical model path /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is entirely absent.
      - The Scottish Widows statement [P13] explicitly states 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan' for the Money4Life workplace plan, which contradicts any implicit assumption that safeguarded benefits require disclosure.
      - The pension switch report [P1, P2, P4, P7, P9, P10] compares existing and new solutions extensively but makes no mention of safeguarded benefits, guaranteed rates, terminal bonuses, or annuity options being surrendered.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Whether any of the five existing arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) contain safeguarded benefits, guarantees, enhanced annuity rates, terminal bonuses, or other valuable features being given up on transfer., Explicit confirmation from provider documentation for each arrangement that no such benefits exist, or alternatively, evidence that such benefits have been identified and addressed in the suitability analysis., Any analysis in the suitability report of the implications of losing any guarantees or valuable features.
    The requirement demands that where a replacement involves giving up safeguarded benefits or guarantees, the report must address whether this disadvantages the client and must be supported by evidence. The extraction explicitly reports that /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is 'NotPresentInDocument', meaning the report itself did not capture or disclose whether any existing arrangements contain safeguarded benefits or whether any are being given up. The Scottish Widows workplace plan [P13] confirms it has no guarantees or bonuses. However, for the other four arrangements—People's Pension, Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life—there is no provider documentation in the pack confirming the presence or absence of safeguarded benefits. The Fact Find template [P14] includes fields for 'Guaranteed Annuity Rate / Pension (or similar)', 'EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus', and 'Other Benefits and/or Material Features', but these fields are blank, which could mean (a) no such features exist, or (b) the form was not fully completed. The pension switch report [P1, P2] does not address this at all. Without explicit confirmation from provider documents or the adviser's analysis for each arrangement, it cannot be established whether any safeguarded benefits are being surrendered. This is a material omission because the FCA rule COBS 9.2.2R requires advisers to identify and address benefits that would be lost. The comparison cannot be properly performed because the foundational input—whether safeguarded benefits exist—is not evidenced in the pack.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that four alternative options were considered and rejected: (1) Remain in existing Aviva with internal fund switch — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile and plan is 0.06% more expensive with £4.50 admin charge; (2) Transfer Scottish Widows to Aviva platform — rejected because Scottish Widows does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned with risk 5, would require combining two funds with 20-30% in cash, does not allow adviser servicing, and does not offer flexible access; (3) Remain in Zurich with internal fund switch — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk 5 funds, blended approach problematic, and blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options; (4) Switch into employer's People's Pension — rejected because transfer would forfeit employer contributions, funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive, and limited fund range. The report recommends consolidating all five pensions into a new Aviva Platform arrangement. The report states the Aviva existing arrangement 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' and the Standard Life existing arrangement 'is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'.
    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P5, P6, P8, P13, P14, P16) provides multiple plan-specific comparisons. For Aviva only (P5): existing projected value £4,280 at mid-growth, new solution £4,260, effect of switching £10 decrease or 0.3% decrease. For Zurich only (P3): existing projected value £102,000-£150,000 depending on growth rate, new solution £100,000-£135,000, showing decreases of £2,000-£15,000 or 1.9%-10.0%. For Standard Life only (P8): existing projected value ranges from £2,910-£4,020, new solution £2,900-£4,010, decreases of £10 or 0.2%-0.3%. For all pensions consolidated (P13): existing £127,000 at mid-growth, new solution £115,000, decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. Charge analysis (P16, P1): Aviva existing annual charge 0.50% implies fund value £3,744, new solution 0.44% implies £110,185; Zurich existing 0.93% implies £103,438; Standard Life existing 0.52% implies £35,805. The report's stated cost comparisons cannot be reconciled with the switch report tables. P1 shows existing pensions total projected value £141,000 vs new solution £138,000, effect of switching £3,000 or 2.1% decrease. P9, P20, P21, P22 confirm Standard Life does not facilitate UFPLS; Scottish Widows does not offer flexible access; Zurich drawdown options not available without transfer; Aviva and People's Pension both offer FAD. P19 table confirms: People's Pension offers FAD and UFPLS with 7 funds; Scottish Widows offers UFPLS only with 29 funds; Aviva offers FAD and UFPLS with 80+ funds; Zurich offers UFPLS only with 60+ funds; Standard Life offers FAD only with 300 funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva existing is '0.06% more expensive' but P1 and P11 charge tables show Aviva existing at 0.50% and new solution at 0.44%, indicating new solution is 0.06% CHEAPER, not expensive — the direction is inverted
      - Report states Standard Life existing is '0.08% more expensive' but P16 shows Standard Life existing charge 0.52% and new solution 0.44%, indicating new solution is actually 0.08% CHEAPER, not expensive — the direction is inverted
      - Report rejects Aviva internal fund switch citing 0.06% cost disadvantage and £4.50 admin charge, but P5 shows switching would result in only £10 decrease (0.3% of £3,744), not a material disadvantage
      - Report rejects Zurich internal fund switch, but P3 shows switching from Zurich to new solution results in £2,000-£15,000 decrease (1.9%-10.0%) depending on growth rate — the new solution performs worse, contradicting the recommendation
      - Report claims Zurich blended funds 'have not performed as well as options available through Aviva', but provides no performance data to support this assertion in the alternatives section; P15 shows historical performance comparison but this is in a different part of the report
      - Report rejects Standard Life option for being 0.08% more expensive, but P8 shows only £10 difference (0.3% of £3,002), immaterial to the decision
      - Overall consolidation comparison shows £3,000-£12,000 decrease in projected value depending on which plans are switched (P1, P13), yet report recommends consolidation without addressing this material adverse outcome
      - Report rejects employer scheme transfer citing loss of contributions, but does not quantify the value of contributions to compare against switching benefits or costs
      - Report states Scottish Widows 'does not allow adviser management' which client wants to benefit from, but provides no evidence client explicitly requested adviser servicing in objectives
      - Report asserts Scottish Widows flexibility limitation would be 'important in future' but P19 shows Scottish Widows offers full and partial UFPLS, which provides flexibility for income withdrawal
    The comparison can be performed because the pension switch report and policy documents provide detailed plan-by-plan analysis. However, critical discrepancies emerge that undermine the reported justification for alternatives rejection. First, the cost comparisons are stated backwards: both Aviva and Standard Life are described as 'more expensive' when the switch report clearly shows the new solution is cheaper for both. This inversion of facts contradicts the stated reason for rejecting these alternatives. Second, the performance claims about Zurich are unsupported by cited evidence in the alternatives section. Third, and most significantly, the switch report's own projections show the consolidated new solution performs worse than retaining the existing pensions across all growth scenarios. P1 shows a £3,000 or 2.1% decrease; P13 shows a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid-growth. This material adverse outcome contradicts the recommendation's assertion that the new solution provides 'better outcomes'. The report rejects Zurich internal switch without noting that P3 shows switching to Aviva produces even worse outcomes (£2,000-£15,000 decrease). The report also rejects Standard Life and Aviva internal switches citing immaterial cost differences (£10 each), yet the overall consolidation produces far larger value decreases. The guard permits consolidation where the client requested simplification — and P19 shows the client's objectives included consolidation — but the report does not disclose that achieving this objective comes at a material cost in projected retirement value. The report does not transparently present the trade-off: consolidation simplicity versus £3,000-£12,000 lower projected value. Additionally, P19 table shows Scottish Widows does offer full and partial UFPLS (flexibility), contradicting the stated limitation. The report's stated reasons for rejecting alternatives are either factually inverted (cost arguments) or not clearly supported by the evidence presented (performance claims), and the recommendation's own benefits are not clearly shown to outweigh the documented projection decreases.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report claims higher costs are justified by additional benefits in one case: for Standard Life, the report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8) but then asserts the switch benefits from 'Lower Costs' and claims 'By switching to this scheme, you will benefit from lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight.' The report lists multiple benefits of switching: lower overall cost structure, non-lifestyling funds maintaining risk alignment, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. For Aviva, the report claims a cost saving of 0.06% (£2.25 annually). For Zurich, the report claims a cost saving of 0.49% (£506.85 annually). For the small pension (not named in detail), the report recommends encashment. The net benefit conclusions state that 'By switching to this scheme, you will benefit from lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight.'
    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P13) shows comparative projections at age 75 across three growth scenarios. For Standard Life alone, it shows: Low growth £97,600 (new) vs £108,000 (existing), a £10,400 decrease (9.6%); Mid growth £115,000 (new) vs £127,000 (existing), a £12,000 decrease (9.4%); High growth £135,000 (new) vs £150,000 (existing), a £15,000 decrease (10.0%). Critical yields required range from 1.9% to 7.8%. The switch report indicates the new solution projects lower maturity values under all growth scenarios for Standard Life. The charges comparison table (P14) shows Standard Life existing charge as 0.18% with recommended charge as 0.44%, making the new plan 0.26% MORE expensive, not 0.08% cheaper. For Aviva, existing charge is 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.06% saving. For Zurich, existing charge is 0.93% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.49% saving. The switch report acknowledges 'Effect of switching' shows negative outcomes for Standard Life across all growth rates. The report states the new solution's 'Reduction in Yield' is 0.6% compared to existing 0.3% for Standard Life, indicating higher net cost impact on returns.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Standard Life cost 'is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but charge comparison table shows recommended solution (0.44%) is 0.26% MORE expensive than existing (0.18%)
      - Report claims switch provides 'Lower Costs' as a benefit for all plans, but for Standard Life the cost actually increases by 0.26% annually
      - Report claims net benefit of lower costs justifies switch for Standard Life, but pension switch report projects £10,400-£15,000 lower maturity value at retirement (9.6%-10% decrease)
      - Report's critical yield analysis shows Standard Life switch requires 4.8% growth just to break even with existing plan, contradicting the 'lower costs' benefit claim
      - Charge basis appears inconsistent: report references 0.08% difference but underlying charge rates show 0.26% difference, suggesting either misstatement or computation error
    The requirement asks whether higher costs are justified by additional benefits. For Standard Life, the report does claim higher costs are justified: it states costs are 'more expensive' yet recommends the switch on grounds of non-lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. However, the evidence contradicts the cost assertion itself. The charge comparison table (P14) unambiguously shows the new plan charges 0.44% vs the existing 0.18%, a 0.26% INCREASE, not the 0.08% decrease claimed on page 8. This is not merely a rounding discrepancy; it represents a materially different cost profile. The pension switch report's projected outcomes for Standard Life show negative 'Effect of switching' under all three growth scenarios (£10,400-£15,000 losses), with critical yields of 1.9% to 7.8% required just to match the existing plan's maturity value. This means the benefits cited (investment choice, monitoring, flexibility) must overcome not just the same charges but a significantly HIGHER charge burden. The report does list these benefits, and they may be genuine (non-lifestyling funds, adviser access, flexibility), but the file contains no evidence quantifying or substantiating whether these specific benefits justify a 0.26% cost increase and the 9-10% projected shortfall in maturity value. The suitability report does not reconcile the cost increase with the benefit claim; it simply asserts both. For Aviva and Zurich, where costs genuinely fall (0.06% and 0.49% respectively), the requirement does not bite—cheaper plans do not require justification of higher costs. The Aviva case shows a minimal cost saving (£2.25 annually on £3,744), and the switch report shows a projected decrease of £30 (0.6%) at retirement under mid-growth. The Zurich switch shows stronger cost savings (£506.85 annually) and benefits (0.49% reduction, projecting £2,000-£15,000 higher maturity values across scenarios). The material problem lies with Standard Life: a cost increase asserted without reconciliation to the claimed benefits, and with switch projections showing material losses relative to the existing plan. The guard 'Where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' applies to Aviva and Zurich but not Standard Life. The guard 'A benefit already available on the ceding plan is not gained' is relevant: lifestyling is an existing feature being removed as a benefit, not a new benefit; the report does not demonstrate that non-lifestyling status is a benefit for this client (page 4 states capacity for loss is sound because pension funds are 'basis of retirement provision,' yet the switch increases costs, contradicting that rationale). Evidence supporting the claimed benefits is absent: no detail on fund range comparison, no evidence adviser charging facilitation did not exist on Standard Life, no quantification of monitoring value relative to the 0.26% cost premium.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts four overlapping rationales for switching to the recommended Aviva Platform SIPP: (1) 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight' (page 7); (2) 'greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight' (pages 7-8); (3) 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight' (page 8); and (4) for Standard Life, 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8). Specific plan-based rationales include: Aviva existing has 'funds do not meet your risk profile' and 'no Multi-Asset funds'; Zurich has 'lifestyling funds' that 'automatically reduce investment risk' moving portfolio 'outside of your agreed risk range', does not offer 'risk level 5 funds', and has 'no multi-asset funds available within your risk range'; Scottish Widows is discounted for 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; People's Pension is not recommended as workplace scheme where 'you and your employer are still contributing'. The report also states consolidation will provide 'clearer understanding and management of pension funds' and 'be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options'.
    File:     The pension switching report (P5, P6, P20) shows: (1) Charge comparison table indicates Aviva existing 0.50% vs recommended Aviva Platform 0.44% = 0.06% saving (not 0.50% existing); Standard Life existing 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.26% MORE expensive for recommended, contradicting the '0.08% more expensive' claim; (2) Zurich fund data shows client already invested in Managed AP fund (page 7 rationale), indicating some overlap; (3) Aviva existing plan value £3,744.36 with 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation' fund listed (P8), showing Aviva exists on Aviva platform already; (4) Provider literature (P1, P3, P4) confirms Zurich drawdown options ARE available ('it can be transferred to another provider to do this' per P3); (5) File notes (P16, P17, P18) show alternatives were considered but rationales differ: Zurich blend 'have not performed as well', Standard Life funds 'more expensive', Scottish Widows noted but with limited detail; (6) Aviva existing plan (P8) shows 'Lifestage Investment Approach' auto-switching, not a feature unique to the recommendation; (7) Switching report (P6) ranks alternatives showing Zurich at 0.2% charge and £128,000 maturity value, outperforming the new solution (0.6% charge, £125,000 value); (8) Provider documents (P2 Aviva transfer form) warn 'you will lose any features or guarantees' but evidence of specific features lost is absent from file; (9) File note (P16) states Zurich 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally' but provides no numerical support; (10) Switching report shows existing solution projects £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, contradicting the lower-cost/better-performance narrative.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva existing is '0.06% more expensive'; charges table shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.06% CHEAPER for existing, not more expensive
      - Report states Standard Life existing is '0.08% more expensive'; charges table shows Standard Life existing 0.18% vs recommended 0.44% = 0.26% MORE expensive for recommended, opposite direction and magnitude
      - Report claims lower costs as benefit; switching report ranks existing Zurich solution (0.2% charge) as superior to new solution (0.6% charge)
      - Report claims existing Zurich will move 'outside of agreed risk range' due to lifestyling; Aviva existing plan (P8) is also invested in lifestyling ('Lifestage Investment Approach'), yet no equivalent rationale given for Aviva switch
      - Report states Zurich 'does not offer risk level 5 funds'; file notes (P16) confirm blend used, but switching report (P6) ranks Zurich existing as superior option (£128,000 vs new solution £125,000)
      - Report states client gains 'improved retirement options'; Zurich policy info (P3) states drawdown options ARE available via transfer to another provider, contradicting implication existing arrangements lack this
      - Switching report (P6) shows existing solution projects £127,000 at retirement vs new solution £125,000, contradicting assertion that switch improves outcome
      - Report implies consolidation to single provider is benefit; existing Aviva arrangement is already on Aviva (same provider name), making this a platform consolidation within same provider, not cross-provider consolidation
      - Report states Zurich has 'only a limited range of other options'; Zurich policy info shows '60+' funds available (P12)
      - Switching report ranking (P6) shows new solution ranks 5th by maturity value (£125,000) below existing Zurich (1st, £128,000), Existing Solution overall (2nd, £127,000), Aviva (3rd, £126,000), and Standard Life (4th, £126,000)
    The core problem is systematic contradiction between the report's replacement rationale and the numerical evidence in the switching report and provider documents. (1) COST SAVINGS CLAIM: The report asserts 'lower costs' as a primary benefit but the switching report's ranking shows the new solution is more expensive (0.6%) than alternatives including existing Zurich (0.2%) and only slightly cheaper than existing Aviva (0.5%). The charges comparison table in the report itself contradicts the narrative: for Aviva it shows the existing plan is CHEAPER (0.50% vs 0.44%), not more expensive as the text claims; for Standard Life the reverse is true—the recommended solution is MORE expensive (0.44% vs 0.18%), opposite to the stated rationale. (2) PERFORMANCE CLAIM: The switching report ranks the new solution 5th by projected maturity value (£125,000), below the existing overall solution (£127,000) and individual Zurich arrangement (£128,000). This contradicts the implicit claim that switching improves outcome. (3) FUND SUITABILITY FOR RISK PROFILE: The report criticizes existing Zurich and Standard Life for misalignment with risk profile (Zurich uses lifestyling, Standard Life funds don't match risk 5), yet the existing Aviva plan is also invested in lifestyling ('Lifestage Investment Approach'), receiving no equivalent criticism. The Zurich lifestyling rationale is reasonable but applied inconsistently. (4) CONSOLIDATION RATIONALE: The existing Aviva arrangement (£3,744.36 at SP50163762) is being switched to 'Aviva Platform' at the same provider. This is not cross-provider consolidation but platform migration within Aviva, yet the rationale emphasizes consolidation benefits as if it were multi-provider consolidation. (5) RETIREMENT OPTIONS: The report states the recommendation provides 'improved retirement options', but Zurich policy information confirms drawdown options ARE available ('it can be transferred to another provider to do this'), contradicting the implication existing arrangements lack flexibility. (6) FUND RANGE: The report states Zurich has 'only a limited range of other options', but provider literature shows '60+' funds, inconsistent with 'limited range' characterization. (7) ALTERNATIVE CONSIDERATION: File notes show alternatives were evaluated, but the switching report's numerical ranking directly contradicts the recommendation: Zurich existing ranks 1st, the new solution ranks 5th. The file note rationale 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally' lacks the quantification needed to justify rejecting a superior ranking. The guard on charges-based rationales is not applicable here: the problem is not different charge bases but the report's own comparison table showing the opposite of what the narrative claims. The comparison CAN be performed—the evidence exists and is substantial—but it reveals systematic inconsistency between assertion and data. The vulnerability overlay notes an ambiguity about employment income continuation, but this does not materially affect the assessment of replacement rationale evidence, which turns on cost, performance, and fund suitability claims now contradicted by the case file.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that four existing arrangements have no exit penalties ('No charges for transfer and/or switching' stated on page 5 for all five existing arrangements). The report extracts show employerContributionsLost = false for all four replacement analyses, indicating the report does not identify any employer contributions being lost. The extraction metadata indicates featuresOrGuaranteesLost was not present in the document, meaning the report contains no section addressing safeguarded benefits or guarantees that may be lost in any of the switches. The report identifies replacements for Peoples Pension (£6,601.55), Scottish Widows (£211.32), Aviva (£3,744.36), and Standard Life (£3,002.00), recommending consolidation into a single Aviva platform. Page 7-8 state cost comparisons showing claimed savings on existing arrangements.
    File:     Evidence P1-P10 document Peoples Pension contributions showing both employer and employee regular contributions continuing through May 2023, confirming this is an active workplace pension scheme with ongoing employer contributions. Evidence P6 shows a specific date of '04/06/2021' with text 'Switch from B&CE GI (up to 85% shares)' indicating historical fund switches within Peoples Pension. Evidence P11 (Zurich policy) discloses 'If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred... The charge is capped at 1% from age 55' - establishing that Zurich has a material exit charge structure that reduces with age. Evidence P12-P13 (Zurich charges) state 'Fund switch charge £0.00' but separately disclose transfer-specific charges under 'Transfer value and early retirement charge' explaining 'This is the difference between current and transfer values. There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken.' Evidence P16 (Standard Life) states 'We will not normally make a charge for switching funds. However we reserve the right to charge for switches' and 'There is no platform charge.' Evidence P15 (Scottish Widows) provides projection tables but does not explicitly confirm or deny exit charges. Evidence P20 (policy research summary) notes the strategy is 'consolidate where necessary' but does not detail safeguarded benefits analysis. Evidence P14 (Scottish Widows terms) states Scottish Widows 'do not offer annuity options directly' and require members to use independent annuity broking service. No evidence in the pack confirms the absence of Protected Tax Free Cash, pension protection insurance, or other valuable features on any arrangement.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for all five arrangements on page 5, but Zurich policy (P11) explicitly discloses material exit charges of up to 1% from age 55, with charges reducing by approximately 0.3%-0.4% per month until Selected Retirement Age. This is a direct contradiction.
      - Report extracts show employerContributionsLost = false for all four replacements, but Peoples Pension contribution records (P1-P10) show continuous employer contributions through May 2023, and the client is age 69 on the report date (14/10/2025) with stated retirement expectation within two years at age 71. The report does not address whether employer contributions continue post-retirement or cease at retirement, creating ambiguity about whether contributions are 'lost' upon transfer.
      - Report contains no section addressing featuresOrGuaranteesLost for any arrangement, per extraction metadata. Evidence P14 (Scottish Widows) discloses absence of direct annuity options; Evidence P16 (Standard Life) discloses Protected Tax Free Cash may or may not be held but requires provider confirmation; Evidence P11 (Zurich) discloses restrictions on taking 25% tax-free lump sum separately from remainder. The report's recommendation does not address these disclosed product features or restrictions in the context of switching.
      - Evidence P20 (policy research) states alternative strategy considered was 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things' but the report does not document whether alternatives to consolidation (such as maintaining arrangements separately with simplified reporting, or selective consolidation of smaller pots only) were considered and rejected with documented rationale.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Whether Peoples Pension employer contributions continue beyond stated retirement age of 71 or cease at retirement, affecting whether contributions are 'lost' by transfer, Confirmation from providers whether Protected Tax Free Cash exists on Standard Life arrangement, and whether any other Protected Benefits exist on any arrangement being switched, Explicit documentation of alternative consolidation strategies considered (e.g., consolidating only smaller pots, or maintaining largest arrangement separately) and stated reasons for rejecting them, Whether any of the five existing arrangements carry Market Value Reductions applicable on transfer date (distinct from future-date MVRs which would still be material)
    The trigger for CHK-009 is satisfied: the report contains a recommendation to consolidate multiple existing pension arrangements into a single Aviva platform. The guard on employer contributions states 'An employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer; check whether the client is still an active member.' Peoples Pension contribution records confirm the client remains an active member as of May 2023 (latest transaction dated 30/05/2023). However, the extraction metadata ambiguity notes that the report states 'You expect to retire within the next two years' (age 71) but does not explicitly clarify whether employment income and thus employer contributions cease at age 71 or continue beyond. This ambiguity means the comparison cannot safely determine whether employer contributions are materially affected. The report's blanket assertion that all five arrangements carry 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' directly contradicts Zurich's documented exit charge structure (P11, P13), which applies if the fund is transferred and is capped at 1% from age 55 (client is 69, so the charge would apply, likely in the range 0.0% to 1.0% depending on months to SRA). This is a material discrepancy that is not explained by any guard. The report contains no analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or product features unique to each arrangement being transferred. Evidence P14, P16, and P11 disclose specific restrictions or features (annuity limitations, Protected Tax Free Cash status, capital unit exit charges) that are not addressed in the report's recommendation narrative. The policy research document (P20) indicates consolidation was considered versus the 'current set up' but does not document whether intermediate alternatives (e.g., consolidating only smaller pots to reduce complexity while maintaining the largest Zurich arrangement with lowest charges if transfers occur) were considered. The comparison of charges stated in the report (pages 7-8) is separately contradicted by the comparison table on page 11 per internal inconsistencies noted in extraction metadata, but that is a CHK-001 matter. On CHK-009, the material failure is the unsubstantiated claim of no exit charges on Zurich and the complete absence of documented analysis of safeguarded benefits, Protected Features, or alternative consolidation strategies.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). Report asserts no third-party support present; file shows client agreement explicitly provides for Power of Attorney arrangements but no evidence this was offered or discussed. Report silent on support needs; file shows client questionnaire responses indicating 'No understanding / knowledge' of multiple investment types and pension planning, yet Capacity for Loss section records 'No additional notes were recorded'. Report contains no vulnerability assessment framework or output; file records risk profiling but no documented vulnerability considerations despite low knowledge/capability indicators. 7 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that third-party support is not present (isPresent: false) and that no Power of Attorney has been granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false). The report is silent on support needs, communication adjustments, or any vulnerability assessment framework. The extraction metadata confirms that /vulnerability/perClient[]/supportNeeds[] and related third-party support detail fields are absent from the extracted model.
    File:     The case file contains extensive evidence of client circumstances relevant to support needs assessment: (1) Risk profile questionnaire responses [P1, P8] showing the client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, enterprise investment schemes, venture capital trusts, and pension planning and income in retirement; (2) Fact Find [P7, P21] documenting 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' with no additional notes recorded on communication adjustments or support requirements; (3) Capacity for Loss questionnaire [P5] with 'No additional notes were recorded' despite detailed questioning; (4) Client Agreement [P17] explicitly allowing third-party instructions via Power of Attorney: 'may also allow a third party to provide instructions on your behalf by providing a valid Power of Attorney'; (5) Fact Find [P21] recording meeting details as 'Video Call - Recorded' with 'Anybody else present at the Meeting? No'. The file contains no documented vulnerability assessment, no recorded support needs despite low knowledge/experience indicators, no communication adjustments, and no evidence of Power of Attorney being offered or discussed.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts no third-party support present; file shows client agreement explicitly provides for Power of Attorney arrangements but no evidence this was offered or discussed
      - Report silent on support needs; file shows client questionnaire responses indicating 'No understanding / knowledge' of multiple investment types and pension planning, yet Capacity for Loss section records 'No additional notes were recorded'
      - Report contains no vulnerability assessment framework or output; file records risk profiling but no documented vulnerability considerations despite low knowledge/capability indicators

    The comparison can be performed as both the report's assertion and the file contents are clear. However, the file does not substantiate this assertion through evidence of an actual assessment. The key discrepancy is between two states: (1) the report's silence and explicit negation, and (2) the file's record of circumstances that would ordinarily trigger vulnerability/support needs assessment under FCA FG21/1 guidance. The client's responses show complete lack of knowledge in stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension planning [P1, P8]. The Capacity for Loss questionnaire was administered but 'No additional notes were recorded' [P5], suggesting either no discussion occurred or no findings were documented. The report's blanket assertion that third-party support is not present appears to rest on absence of a documented arrangement rather than evidence of an assessment that concluded no support was needed. Under PRIN 2A.2.1R and FCA FG21/1, where a client demonstrates limited knowledge and experience, support needs and communication adjustments should be actively assessed and documented.   [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client characteristics: (1) Age and health status: 'You are currently aged 69 and married to Kim, aged 65. You are both in good health' [Page 3]; (2) Family circumstances: 'You have three grown-up children, all of whom are financially independent' [Page 3]; (3) Financial resilience: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' [Page 3]. The canonical model shows `/vulnerability/perClient[]/characteristics[]` populated with these three items. Notably, the extraction reports that `/vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[]` is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument). The report contains no recorded adjustments made to support client comprehension, no accessibility requirements, no communication preferences specific to comprehension support, and no documented measures taken to ensure informed decision-making beyond standard product literature and ongoing review arrangements.
    File:     The case file records the following relevant evidence: (1) Fact Find dated 19/09/2025 [P2] shows 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' with standard marketing consent options. (2) File notes [P11] record ATR discussion and capacity for loss assessment, noting client's limited investment experience ('although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements') and confirmation that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' due to state pension and employment income. (3) Risk profile report [P19] and [P23] show sections for 'Investor Experience' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' but record 'No additional notes were recorded' in both sections. (4) Client Agreement [P17] and [P18] reference standard services including 'ongoing review' but contain no client-specific adjustments for comprehension. (5) The report itself [P5] states the client 'completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire' and adviser felt it 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite', leading to agreed increase from 4 to 5, with explanation that as a 'Balanced investor' the client 'may be suited to this approach if you make investment decisions reasonably quickly and don't tend to be particularly anxious about those decisions.' No specific comprehension support measures are evidenced.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts vulnerability characteristics (age, health, family, financial resilience) but does not explain how these characteristics informed specific adjustments to support the client's understanding or comprehension. The report provides generic educational content about risk, pensions, and tax but no tailored adjustments specific to this client's circumstances or demonstrated needs.
      - The extraction model identifies that `/vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[]` is absent from the report (NotPresentInDocument), yet the requirement asks whether adjustments made are fully supported. An empty or absent field suggests either no adjustments were made or they were not recorded.
      - Fact Find [P2] records 'No Preference' for preferred delivery method and no accessibility format requirement recorded (marked 'No Requirement' [P16]), yet the report makes recommendations involving complex financial instruments, multiple pension transfers, and risk rating changes. There is no evidence that the adviser assessed or documented whether the client required any form of accessible format, specialist communication, or additional support given limited prior investment experience.
      - File notes [P19], [P23] show placeholder sections for 'Investor Experience' and 'Capacity for loss' with 'No additional notes were recorded', indicating these sections were present in the assessment framework but not completed with detail. This suggests a process was contemplated but not executed or documented.
      - The report [P5] notes the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and the adviser had to correct the client's risk rating from questionnaire result (4) upward to 5. This pattern suggests the client may have required additional support in understanding the increase, yet no tailored explanation, repetition, or comprehension checks are evidenced in the file.
    The requirement asks whether information the suitability report includes relating to vulnerabilities, support needs, communication preferences, capacity and comprehension requirements is fully supported by evidence. The report does include basic vulnerability characteristics (age, health, family, resilience) consistent with the facts (client is 69, married, states good health, has liquid reserves). However, the requirement is not merely that these characteristics exist, but that any adjustments made to support the client's comprehension are supported. The extraction tool reports `/vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[]` as 'NotPresentInDocument'—meaning the report does not record adjustments made. The file evidence shows: (1) no specific communication preferences recorded beyond 'No Preference' in delivery method; (2) no accessible format requirement; (3) risk profile assessment sections left blank ('No additional notes'); (4) no documented comprehension checks, repetition, or support measures despite the client's limited investment experience and an initial risk rating disagreement requiring correction. The report provides standard product explanations and ongoing review commitments, but these are regulatory-standard features, not client-specific adjustments. The combination of limited investment experience, need to correct risk rating upward, and absence of documented comprehension support or adjustment measures indicates the report's vulnerability section is descriptive but not paired with evidence of tailored support measures. The guard (standard cancellation rights are regulatory, not adjustments for this client) does not apply here—this concerns comprehension support, not regulatory boilerplate. The finding is that vulnerability characteristics are stated but supporting evidence for comprehension adjustments is absent or minimal.
