      · Risk profile report.md [D] (P23)
        "Capacity for loss questionnaire: No additional notes were recorded"
      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md [A] (P17)
        "Should you choose to proceed with our recommendations, we will provide you with our Ongoing advice and review service, as described in the 'Important Information about our Services' document we have supplied to you, until either you, or we, consider this service to be no longer appropriate for your needs."

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies five foreseeable harms with mitigations: (1) Market volatility and potential fund value decline—client confirmed awareness that funds may go up and down and higher risk results in larger potential drops; (2) Underperformance against existing arrangements—critical yield analysis shows recommended plan needs 4.8% per year growth to age 75 to match existing arrangements; (3) Inclusion of pensions in Inheritance Tax estate from 6 April 2027—report explains IHT changes and exceptions, client to complete beneficiary nominations; (4) Market timing risk during pension transfer process—client advised stock market could rise or fall between realisation and reinvestment, confirmed comfort with concept; (5) Access delays to pension funds—client informed access typically takes 10-15 working days, confirmed comfort given liquid savings. The extraction model shows /vulnerability/foreseeableHarms[] is populated with these five items. However, the extraction reports that /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument). The report does not explicitly state that any identified vulnerability materially affects the suitability of the recommendation or how the recommendation has been adjusted to account for vulnerability.
    File:     The case file contains: (P1) Risk profile showing client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension planning and income in retirement; has not invested in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products within the last 5 years; only experience is Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA (by self). (P2) Risk profile notes: 'No additional notes were recorded' for Investor Experience and Capacity for loss questionnaire sections. (P10) Natural risk level based on questionnaire was 4 (Cautious Balanced), described as someone 'quite a cautious person' who 'view[s] risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity' with 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. (P14) Suitability report states: 'We felt that [the questionnaire] didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4.' Report notes client 'confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential' and 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down.' Report also states: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' due to 'full State Pension of approximately £230 per week' and employment income. (P6, P7) Fact Find records: client is 69 years old, employed as HGV driver earning £300/week net, receives State Pension £230/week net; household income £1,430/month; household expenditure £1,718/month (monthly shortfall £288); wishes to retire within next 2 years at age 71. (P20) Report recommends setting up Power of Attorney for financial affairs and health/welfare. No record of vulnerability assessment, health conditions, mental capacity assessment, communication adjustments, accessibility requirements, support needs, or third-party involvement recorded anywhere in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts five foreseeable harms and mitigations but extraction model shows /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] is absent, meaning the report does not explicitly link identified vulnerabilities to how the recommendation has been adjusted or why it remains appropriate despite them.
      - Client's natural risk level from questionnaire was 4 (Cautious Balanced, limited experience, cautious person) but report recommends Risk 5 (Balanced). File shows adviser felt questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and upgraded to 5. Client has no understanding/knowledge of stocks and shares or pension planning per risk profile (P1), yet is being recommended a balanced portfolio.
      - Report states client 'not reliant upon your pension funds' (P14) but also states 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living' due to state pension and employment income (P14). The file shows client currently has monthly household shortfall of £288 (expenditure £1,718 exceeds income £1,430) per Fact Find (P12), creating ambiguity about actual financial resilience.
      - Risk profile (P1, P10) reveals no investment experience (no stocks/shares, no Unit Trusts, no pension products), yet no discussion of this as a vulnerability driver or how the Risk 5 recommendation accounts for inexperience. P2 shows 'No additional notes were recorded' for investor experience discussion.
      - Report recommends Power of Attorney be set up (P20) but provides no evidence in file that client has capacity concerns, health issues, or vulnerability indicators that would trigger this recommendation. No cognitive assessment, health history, or vulnerability flag documented.
    The case presents a material inconsistency between what the report asserts about foreseeability of harms and what it demonstrates about vulnerability's impact on the recommendation itself.
    
    First, the foreseen harms are genuine and documented. The report does identify five specific risks—market volatility, underperformance, IHT treatment, market timing, and access delays—with stated client awareness and comfort. These are evidenced in the text passages and cashflow projections (P4, P14).
    
    However, the vulnerability overlay asks whether vulnerability has a *material impact* on the recommendation. The extraction confirms that /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] is absent from the document. This is not the same as saying no vulnerability exists; it means the report has not articulated how identified vulnerabilities shape the suitability conclusion.
    
    The more acute issue is a fundamental mismatch in the risk profile assessment itself. The file shows:
    - Client questionnaire result: Risk 4 (Cautious Balanced, for someone with 'limited or moderate experience of investments' who is 'quite a cautious person')
    - Client knowledge/experience: 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares or pension planning; no self-directed investment experience except cash savings
    - Current financial position: Monthly household shortfall £288 (P12)
    - Recommended risk level: Risk 5 (Balanced)
    
    The report justifies the upgrade from 4 to 5 by stating the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and by asserting client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' and wants 'best growth potential'. However, the file also records that any underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living' (P14), which acknowledges the pensions do matter to living standards. The household currently runs a monthly deficit that must be closed by employment income or pension drawdowns.
    
    The recommendation to set up Power of Attorney (P20) is present in the file without any documented vulnerability indicator that would justify it. No health issue, no capacity concern, no disability, no cognitive assessment appears anywhere.
    
    Under FG21/1 principles (Principle 2A: Fair treatment of vulnerable customers), the firm should:
    1. Assess whether vulnerability characteristics are present
    2. Record and reflect any identified characteristics
    3. Ensure the recommendation is appropriate in light of them
    4. Adjust support and communication accordingly
    
    Here, the file contains objective indicators that could constitute vulnerability drivers under FG21/1: age 69 approaching retirement (life stage / low resilience), zero investment experience with stocks/shares and pensions (low capability), monthly budget deficit (low financial resilience). Yet no vulnerability assessment is documented anywhere. The report's silence on whether these have been considered is itself the finding.
    
    The gap between questionnaire risk rating (4) and recommendation (5) is material. The report does not explain why a client with no understanding of stocks and shares, no pension planning knowledge, and natural risk rating 4 should be placed in Risk 5, nor does it discuss this as a potential vulnerability issue requiring additional support or adjusted communication. The upgrade is justified only by assertion that questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' appetite—circular reasoning without evidence of the grounds for that judgment.
    
    Per FCA FG21/1 guidance, a recommendation that raises a customer's risk significantly above their natural risk level, when they lack experience, should be supported by clear explanation of why it is appropriate *for that customer*, not just that they said they agreed. The file shows agreement was reached but does not evidence that the firm considered whether a client with zero investment knowledge can genuinely consent to higher risk or whether additional safeguards are needed.
    
    The guards note that 'Age alone is not vulnerability' and 'Where vulnerability is genuinely absent and that has been assessed and recorded, the overlay adds nothing.' However, this case shows the opposite: vulnerability has not been assessed or recorded at all, despite indicators in the file. That is a finding on the overlay.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts hasVulnerability = 'No' and records three characteristics: (1) client aged 69, married to Kim aged 65, both in good health; (2) three grown-up children, all financially independent; (3) client has confirmed retention of sufficient liquid capital to cover unexpected emergencies. Assessment date: 2025-10-14. The extracted model shows no vulnerability categories recorded.
    File:     The file contains extensive evidence of capacity and resilience assessment. P1 (File notes) records Attitude to Risk questionnaire completed with initial rating 4, adviser and client agreed to increase to rating 5 after discussion. Capacity for Loss section states: 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' due to pension funds 'which form the basis of your retirement provision'. Client receives full State Pension £230/week, works as HGV driver earning £300/week net, receives £20/week from Sea Cadets role. Kim contributes £1,100/month and will receive full State Pension. P3-P4 (Fact Find) shows client details: age 70 (discrepancy with report's 69), married, British national. P9 shows current monthly cash flow is negative: £1,430 net income vs £1,718 expenditure = -£288 monthly disposable income. P8 shows £6,000 liquid savings. P13 (Risk profile report) states: 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for loss questionnaire sections. P19 shows planning scenario with retirement ages: Kim retire age 66 (2026), client retire age 71 (2027), illness events age 100, death events age 100. P1 also contains contradiction: capacity for loss justification states pensions 'form the basis of your retirement provision' but Attitude to Risk section states client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client age 69; Fact Find P3 records age 70 at time of advice (dated 18/09/2025, with DOB 07/06/1956 making client 69 at report date 14/10/2025, but Fact Find dated 19/09/2025 shows age 70 — this is internally inconsistent within the file itself, not necessarily a report-file mismatch)
      - Report asserts 'sufficient liquid capital to cover unexpected emergencies' with no quantified figure stated; file shows £6,000 in savings (P8) but monthly cashflow is -£288 (P9), meaning disposable income is negative
      - Report states both client and Kim are 'in good health' as a characteristic; file notes make no positive health affirmation, only describe capacity assumptions
      - Report provides no specific evidence of how vulnerability assessment was conducted; P13 states 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for loss questionnaire sections, suggesting minimal assessment documentation
      - Report characterises financial resilience as confirmed by client; file shows active employment income (HGV driver £300/week, Sea Cadets £20/week) that is scheduled to end at retirement age 71 (2027), creating future income cliff not explicitly addressed in resilience claim
      - File notes (P1) contain internal contradiction: Capacity for Loss states pensions 'form the basis of your retirement provision' but immediately following Attitude to Risk section states client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds' — these directly contradict each other
    The report's assertion of no vulnerability is supported by the file at a surface level: the client is married, has independent adult children, possesses liquid savings (£6,000), and was assessed as capable of withstanding investment volatility without impacting day-to-day living. However, critical gaps emerge on examination. First, the file shows a material internal contradiction within the capacity assessment itself (P1): the Capacity for Loss section explicitly states pensions 'form the basis of your retirement provision' — which would typically indicate vulnerability to investment underperformance — yet the Attitude to Risk conclusion states the client is 'not reliant upon pension funds'. These statements are mutually exclusive. Second, the monthly cashflow position in the Fact Find (P9) reveals -£288 monthly disposable income (£1,430 income vs £1,718 expenditure), which directly contradicts the characterisation of 'sufficient liquid capital' providing reassurance. The £6,000 liquid savings at this burn rate would cover only 21 months of deficit. Third, the vulnerability assessment documentation is sparse: P13 explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss questionnaire sections, indicating minimal investigation despite the client being aged 69, about to retire, with pension funds as stated basis of retirement provision, and currently in negative cashflow. The FG21/1 framework identifies low financial resilience (current deficit spending), low capability (limited investment experience per P1 'limited to workplace pension arrangements'), and a life event (retirement imminent at age 71) as vulnerability drivers. The report does not acknowledge or address these. The age discrepancy between report (69) and Fact Find (70) at dates 14/10/2025 and 19/09/2025 respectively is arithmetically consistent with DOB 07/06/1956 and may reflect reporting date vs completion date, not a substantive error. However, the contradiction between 'pension funds form basis of retirement' and 'not reliant upon pension funds' remains unresolved and is material to capacity for loss assessment.
